mr president, as mrs joly has already said, there are times when words and figures lose all meaning, become ridiculous: 2 million refugees - we have heard it - perhaps more, more than 200 000 dead, a country that is devastated, a capital that is almost wiped off the map. the urgent task now is to exhume the last survivors, if there are any, to bury the dead, to feed the living, to distribute water, to treat, to operate and to rebuild. each country left quickly, very quickly, with its means, its equipment, its dogs, its men and its generosity. the effort is immense, it is magnificent, but it is national. europe must now become more powerful and give substance to the idea that we are certain, many of us, to defend, here in the european parliament and elsewhere for some years, the implementation of a european civil intervention force, green helmets or white helmets, regardless of colour, men, means and common strategies. eu-fast, the initiative launched in 2003 by guy verhofstadt, or europaid launched in 2006 by michel barnier, it doesn't matter who the father is, the idea is the ability to act quickly, to be ready together.
mr president, iran is undoubtedly going through the most serious crisis in its history since 1979, due to the fault of this hermetic regime, which has increased, since the electoral farce of 12 june, the targeted assassinations of opponents, the roundups, the imprisonment of peaceful demonstrators, including journalists. at the beginning of the debate, mrs ashton referred to the reports by the ngos, which are catastrophic in this respect, not to mention the travesty of justice reserved for the frenchwoman clotilde reiss, the minority baha'i - seven of whom have been on trial since last monday in tehran. they risk life imprisonment or worse, simply because their religion is not that of the government. the fact is that the presidential elections have not changed anything, or rather, they have further radicalized, if that were possible, the iranian regime, radicalization also vis-à-vis the outside, with the reversal of these iranian authorities on the draft nuclear agreement negotiated in october 2009 in vienna. when will we consider - and i am talking about here - intelligent and targeted sanctions, as mrs lochbihler said, sanctions against this regime which is openly anti-western and anti-semitic? i know that this window of opportunity is narrow, madam high representative, but don't you think that we should do more and better here, help the iranian youth, who are crying out their revolt on the internet, denounce the recurring calls for the destruction of the state of israel and, above all, support this civil society and the opposition, this democratic movement that challenges this vote-stealing president and his militias? we must avoid escalation, i am well aware of that, but we must also avoid stealth and not repeat the mistake we made in afghanistan where europe did not know, could not protect commander massoud. it is our duty to support the leaders of the iranian opposition to prevent them from suffering the same fate.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, commissioner, i too would like to congratulate mrs joly on the accuracy and rigour of her report and on the very adequate analysis contained in it. the second revision of the cotonou agreement must strengthen the lomé acquis while consolidating unity, cohesion and solidarity among the acp states. the agreement must of course also guarantee that the latter take ownership of their development policy. the programming, review and monitoring of the agreement should therefore also be the prerogative of the parliaments of our partner countries. i know that this is obviously an extremely important challenge, but i think we must make a special effort to stimulate these parliaments. this is an expectation which we must also express very openly to the authorities of our partner countries. some governments, as you know, are reluctant to encourage parliamentary debate in their own countries. i also call for a strengthening of democratic control and the role of the acp-eu joint parliamentary assembly, in particular by including provisions in the cotonou agreement which would allow the acp to receive national and regional strategy papers for discussion. i believe that in future national and regional parliaments must also be consulted much more systematically on the process of drawing up regional and national strategy papers. a word on the organization of the work of the joint parliamentary assembly: i think that two sessions should be held. to reduce them to one session would send a very negative signal to our partners. perhaps regional meetings should be organised in a more restricted way; mr president, article 13 of the cotonou agreement has not been revised since 2000. it touches on a sensitive subject: the dialogue between the european union and the acp countries on immigration, the fair treatment of immigrants, the principles of non-refoulement, the root causes of immigration and, finally, the fight against illegal immigration and readmission, and it is on these two aspects that the european union has mainly focused. the crude absence of a common immigration policy, which is in keeping with the values that europe demands, risks reinforcing the repressive approach and the hunt for illegal immigrants in the proposed revision of article 13. the tragic example of the bilateral agreement between italy and libya, which led to intolerable humanitarian situations, does not seem to have discouraged the zeal of those who want to tighten article 13 to strengthen the fight against illegal immigration. and in some countries - i am thinking of calabria - the hunt for illegal immigrants is now open and ruthless. instead of being driven by the fears of the current crisis, we must remember the synergy that must be established between immigration and development. if the acp countries need help to get their economies off the ground, we will need immigration to cope with the crisis we are facing. the focus should therefore be on legal immigration and mobility. they are the only safeguards against the hateful climate that is creeping into our own countries.
mr president, i too would like to express my support for the very good report by my colleague, mrs joly. the acp-eu partnership is more necessary than ever, and the tragedy in haiti shows how essential the acp-eu partnership is, and that it certainly needs to be strengthened. so i would like to - it is a particular thing to speak to a commissioner responsible for development today, who will be responsible for trade tomorrow, to talk about all this - and to make three suggestions in this connection. the first is, as others have said before me, to maintain two annual meetings, not just one, for parliamentary and assembly control. secondly, why not subject economic partnership agreements to parliamentary control rather than to a strictly commercial logic in the case of the acp, so that, here too, parliaments can be involved and held accountable for monitoring the impact of economic partnership agreements on the people and defending their interests in this context.

the statements by the spanish presidency on immigration policy, and on border controls in particular, are characterized by a very peculiar combination of hot and cold. the spanish creed in particular that such matters should be regulated at european level is rather hollow when one considers that spain has only just completed such a massive legalisation of illegal immigrants, the cost of which the more - let us say - northern european countries may soon share without ever having a say in it. i have also noticed that the spanish presidency continues to advocate an active immigration policy at a time of very severe economic crisis, when no one knows how many millions of europeans are out of work, how many millions of europeans are unemployed. what we really need is closed borders, a real immigration moratorium and, of course, economic support for countries that need it. the further mass immigration you advocate can only lead to mass problems.
i voted against the jolly report, mr president, although i think we all agree on one of the central points of the report, namely that we must pursue a policy aimed at promoting the economic development of the african countries themselves, so that brain drain can be prevented and the educated africans can finally devote themselves to the development of their own country. i understand and support that. but why, in the same report, do we so stubbornly advocate a policy of new emigration from african countries and new immigration into europe? this is what is encouraging the brain drain of the most skilled, the most dynamic, the most enterprising africans. i keep saying that this so-called 'blue card' is disastrous for europe and also particularly harmful for africa and for africans. the so-called circular migration of which we speak last but not least is an illusion, because those immigrants are not going home and we are creating a new breeding ground for illegality.
president, paragraph 31 of the jolly report alone was enough to vote against, which calls on the commission to incorporate the principle of circular migration and facilitating it by issuing circular visas. the so-called circular migration is a fata morgana. it's something that exists in official eu documents, for example, but it doesn't exist in the real world - with a few exceptions. circular migration is when an immigrant with a limited residence permit, after expiry of validity, still stays in europe and then plunges into illegality. this is the reality of what is known as circular migration. circular migration is a driver of illegal immigration. the commission knows that. parliament knows that. yet, the term is used again and again, because people think that many immigrants eventually return to their country of origin. so it would be good if we stopped camouflaging reality as soon as possible.
in writing. − the cotonou agreement - which dates from 2000 and sets out the cooperation between the eu and the acp countries - is revised every five years. the aim is to eradicate poverty and gradually integrate the acp countries into the world economy, while maintaining the objective of sustainable development. the review comes at a time of global financial crisis, rapid climate change, food and energy prices under pressure, and the debate over land use and the sustainability of foreign investment. it is high time to provide for a strengthening of parliamentary scrutiny of the country strategies and the edf and to seek consistency in trade, foreign and development policies. it's time to tackle climate change holistically and make a big push for renewable energy. it is time to tackle illicit money flows from developing countries and to reform the eib's policy towards greater transparency on tax havens. it's time to recognize that land ownership and clean water are fundamental rights. time to admit that fair access to natural resources can really lift people out of poverty. the report stresses this. that's why i support it.

mr president, i support the environmental goals for 2020, but i have some serious doubts about some methods. the copenhagen conference was a good example of a bad method. and what were the lessons? the conference was too massive to be productive: a rock concert of 50 000 people is possible; a meeting, not. secondly, europe was outfoxed by china and india, and the us joined in. instead of being a decisive player, the eu was a confused spectator. what should we do? i think we have to reassess the form we used, rather a g20 than an event of woodstock proportions. secondly, we have to stop displaying moral arrogance by telling china and india what to do. important barriers to punish both countries would be disastrous. on the contrary, the eu should re-examine its own stance, which will ultimately undermine our own growth and employment. finally, we need serious research on the body of evidence. ‘climategate’ was already a reminder, and the claim that the himalayan glaciers are retreating now seems to be based on speculation. some members of the ipcc now even predict global cooling. we need objective, impartial scientific research, and not an agenda-driven political activism. what is true and what is not: at present, we simply do not know, and i think we need to have the facts right, first. commissioner, minister, i hope you will look at these questions with an open mind.

on behalf of the alde group. mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i should first like to thank and congratulate the commissioner on the commission's position. i am totally in favour of this position, which seems to me much more reasonable than what motivated - i do not know on whose suggestion - this debate today. tunisia is the first country in the euro-mediterranean region to sign an association agreement. it is an active partner in the european union's neighbourhood policy. it is worth recalling, as mr kasoulides has done, that it is the only mediterranean country to have had free trade relations with the european union since 1 january 2008. this cooperation is working very well. it is a country that has an excellent absorption capacity. tunisia has made substantial progress, which has resulted in a very good level of development with socioeconomic results recognised by international institutions. on the economic side, tunisia achieved an average growth rate of 4.6% during the period 2002-2008. moreover, she has been able to reduce her debt burden. on the social side, it is not right to ignore the progress made, especially in favour of women. the indicators speak for themselves, 59% of students in higher education are girls. compulsory education is generally compulsory for girls aged 6 to 16. women make up almost a quarter of the active population in tunisia. i admit that this assessment cannot of course be allowed to obscure the urgent need to encourage the tunisian authorities to make further progress on governance and human rights; we know that there is still work to be done. i am concerned about this issue, as are other speakers, in the name of our democratic values. but we also have to remember that we are not alone in carrying these values. the latter also live in the heart of tunisian society, which is a dynamic and pro-european society. for these reasons we must support tunisia's request for advanced status in this partnership with the european union. and i think that it is in this dialogue that we will find the appropriate framework to encourage our partners to make further progress on the governance path. we must, of course, conduct the debate with our partners without complacency, but also without falling into that kind of moralising obsession which europe too often has in secret and which is often counterproductive. i am not, of course, insensitive to particular cases which some of you may be wondering about. we have the right, of course, to question our partners about situations which we consider unacceptable, but i would like to say, in conclusion, that tunisia has succeeded in forging a political system based on the principle of separation of religion and state. the public authorities in tunisia have the capacity to provide a set of basic services to the population, which other countries in the region have not yet been able to implement, and i think it is good to recall this as well. so i am extremely optimistic about the future of relations between tunisia and the european union, as long as it happens in mutual respect, between partners who respect each other and who agree on the essentials.

for the greens/efa group mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in recent weeks it has become fashionable to sound alarm about the budget deficits of certain member states. we can certainly criticise some public expenditure - and we will not refrain from doing so - we can think of the billions of euros in subsidies for fossil fuels, but let us not forget, as our socialist colleague said, that the worsening of public deficits is first and foremost due to the financial and economic crisis. i think that governments have no lessons to learn from those who, by their preference for risky operations financed, for example, by debt, have been at the origin of the crisis. however, we agree that public deficits are unsustainable at their current level because they undermine europe's chances of being at the forefront of a much-needed global green new deal. we must therefore tackle this not only on the expenditure side but also on the revenue side, and it is in this spirit that we read the reports tabled today, in particular those by mrs alvarez and mr domenici. by making automatic exchange of information between tax authorities the norm, they are giving the member states the means to tackle tax evasion in a serious way, which, i would remind you, is estimated at between 200 and 250 billion euros per year, or two points of gdp. before we even talk about restructuring european taxation, let us ensure that the tax due is collected. the project also supports the establishment of a common corporate tax base which will provide greater clarity for both taxpayers and member states. in this respect it is a step forward, but it should pave the way not to more competition but to more cooperation. it is time to put an end to tax dumping, a race to the bottom which is undermining member states' tax revenues to the detriment of whom? taxpayers and smes, which do not have the means of large transnational companies to play the member states against each other. the consolidated tax base is therefore, in our view, the prerequisite for the gradual harmonization of corporate tax rates, starting with the setting of minimum thresholds, as we are doing with vat. finally, to put member states' taxation on a sustainable basis will require far more profound changes: to continue reducing burdens on labour income and to compensate for this by introducing a progressive taxation on energy on non-renewable resources and a taxation on financial transactions and capital gains. but, as my grandmother used to say, this is another story. in the meantime, the greens congratulate mrs alvarez and mr domenici on their excellent work, which does not merely repeat the european parliament's past positions but makes them more ambitious and operational. i would also like to end by saying goodbye to mr kovács. i wasn't here when you arrived. my colleagues have told me that you have made a much better impression on them than you did on us when you were appointed. so you've pleasantly surprised us. good for you.
in writing. mr president, the task of tax authorities in a globalised and digitalised world is not an easy one. the detection of tax and social fraud is also complex in the internal market. the lack of up-to-date european legislation on cross-border administrative cooperation between tax authorities is even problematic. the creation of a fiscal liaison office in each member state to speed up and simplify administrative cooperation between member states is therefore worthy of our support. today, requests for the exchange of tax information take so much time that tax authorities often prefer not to wait for the information. the commission's proposal for an automatic exchange of information has my full support for two reasons. firstly, the member states will be able to collect their taxes more efficiently, which is a just and not an unnecessary luxury in times of crisis. secondly, because players in the single market will be treated equally. the principle of reciprocity on the exchange of tax information is also in line with the agreements in the oecd and the g20. this is a message that cannot be misunderstood and one which the court of auditors in my country rightly called for urgently. i shall therefore wholeheartedly support mr álvarez's report.
member of the commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, this debate concerns a matter of great interest to the european public: the public financing of the restructuring of the opel/vauxhall group by one or more european governments. the commission has followed this matter closely. on 14 july 2009, commissioner kroes and i discussed the opel/vauxhall case with you here in the european parliament. the commission also held several informal meetings with the relevant european ministers. as you know, in the autumn of 2009, general motors decided to keep opel/vauxhall and to restructure the company. at the end of november 2009, general motors submitted a summary of its restructuring plan to the commission's competition services. although the commission's role does not normally involve the prior assessment of the industrial and commercial logic of a restructuring case, and in the absence of information from the member states on potential state aid to the plan, the commission's services undertook such an assessment following a request from the competitiveness council. on the basis of the contents of the general motors restructuring plan and the information provided to the commission, it appears that this restructuring plan is not based on non-economic considerations which would undermine the future viability of the european business of the opel/vauxhall group. the current general motors plan shares a number of features with other restructuring plans prepared previously by general motors itself and other interested investors, and in certain key areas. it is consistent with previous decisions taken by general motors before the start of the crisis, in particular on the allocation of a given model to a given production site. in addition, general motors has given the economic justification for its decisions to reorganise its production facilities across europe by referring to the specific situation of individual plants. these decisions appear to be motivated by considerations such as the range of models allocated to different sites in europe, the respective life cycles of the different models, the respective production volumes of a given model, currently produced in more than one site, the relatively low level of additional investment resulting from increased centralisation of production of a given model, the interest in installing certain upstream processes, etc. i understand that general motors has yet to discuss this plan with the workers' representatives, who are supposed to make commitments to allow for significant cost reductions in europe. the commission will remain vigilant and ensure that, if state aid is involved, the restructuring of opel/vauxhall remains based on economic considerations, that it is not influenced by non-commercial conditions linked to state funding and, in particular, that the geographical distribution of the restructuring efforts is not altered by political requirements. the commission will of course continue to monitor closely all developments within the opel group. in this context, you are probably aware that on 21 january 2010 general motors officially announced its intention to close the site in antwerp in 2010. i understand the concern raised by the announcement of thousands of job losses at this plant. it is sad to see that the opel/vauxhall group has deemed it necessary to close plants. i want to emphasize that this decision is the sole responsibility of general motors. the commission cannot and must not try to impose where these reductions will take place. she cannot prevent them, but she can anticipate their consequences. the commission is prepared to use all the resources at its disposal, in coordination with the belgian authorities, to help these workers. the possibility of belgium applying for assistance under the european globalisation adjustment fund was discussed. this is certainly an option to be explored and, at first sight, it would appear that this request for assistance, if confirmed, would meet the required criteria.
on behalf of the ppe group you say you want to be vigilant, but that seems a little too little to me. we believe that the attitude of general motors in europe is unacceptable. general motors is counting on the release of state aid from various national and regional governments in the european union, and with that public money they want to pay, among other things, the social costs of the closure of the factory in antwerp to which you refer. we cannot allow that, commissioner, nor can the workers accept it, nor can europe accept it. we believe that this dossier is a very important test for you, for the credibility of the european commission. we cannot allow this, because it would set a huge precedent for other cases in the future. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. it undermines the foundations of the european union and it is always the smaller ones who are the victims, as has been demonstrated here once again. in recent months the governments of the european union have been allowed to be played like choirboys by the americans of general motors, and it is clear that this cannot happen again. we can only avoid this by a common european approach. today in the german press your colleague on competition policy, mrs kroes, says that state aid in the car sector is only possible if it is directed towards the development of innovative and environmentally friendly products. we agree with that, but it is much better to coordinate this at european level than to negotiate with general motors in the various countries of europe individually, as is the case today, because that will lead to nothing. moreover, if we were to make a joint european offer to general motors, we would obviously be much stronger and could also demand guarantees in the social field - which you must be very concerned about - and in the field of employment. and why should there not be a new prospect for the workers of the opel plant in antwerp which has been affected, and of course for the workers in the other opel plants in europe who have been affected? it is not too late for that, mr spidla. we believe that the european commission should take the lead in such crossborder restructuring and adopt a proactive policy rather than a wait-and-see or a vigilant attitude. what does that mean, specifically? that means setting up a common strategy, taking a much more active approach and, above all, using all the european instruments at our disposal more forcefully and combining them rather than acting in a dispersed manner as we are doing now. i think we have been too soft on this issue so far. the european commission stood by and watched, and general motors triumphed. it is in the interests of the workers that this should be done, because they are the ones who are suffering today from this lack of european political will. the commission has already made a number of proposals. the european union's industry ministers are meeting today in spain to launch a european action plan for the automotive sector. i think we need that very badly. you as the european commission must bring together the main players in the sector to prevent us from being overrun by chinese products in the automobile sector too. this thursday the european heads of state and government will meet in brussels for the extra-european summit, at the instigation and invitation of our president herman van rompuy. and there's only one agenda item: the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the matter. let us start with the car industry, commissioner, because it is and will remain our most important industrial sector, a sector which is a real driver of economic innovation and which can also be a powerful engine for employment.
i have urged that this matter be taken up again in plenary, not only because it concerns thousands of workers in my backyard in antwerp, flanders - you will see that many members of the european parliament will be speaking on this - but above all because it concerns a restructuring in the whole of the european union, with one establishment closing down and thousands of others losing their jobs too. thousands of men, women and their families face a bleak future. i think that these people today look at the european union with hope and fear. hopefully, rightly, with a question mark maybe? i would like to ask you, commissioner, what your plans are for the future. because i will not hide under chairs or benches that i am particularly disappointed by the statements made today by mrs kroes, who reiterates once again that if the matter comes up, she will apply the state aid rules. but i'm also disappointed by your answer and statement today. of course, if it ever comes to a close, we'll have to make sure people are helped. but we want to see a very different european commission today, a european commission that takes this matter to heart, that plays the political role it should play. and that begins with demanding that opel's european management finally bring the business plan to the table and that there be full disclosure of the economic and other arguments underlying this decision. because you undoubtedly know that in recent years the european management in antwerp, for example, has received a lot of support and resources from both the flemish and belgian governments to ensure that it can remain competitive. today all that is being swept away and there is no strong european commission to oppose it. secondly, and this is my second message and also a very clear question to the commission, i would like the commission to be much clearer and more firm in its approach to restructuring in the future. if two companies merge, the european commission must be notified. why can't we do that in restructuring? why can the commission not apply the same economic and social criteria when it comes to restructuring? only then will there be a clear vision of both industrial policy and a social europe. that is what we, the s&d group, are asking for.
first of all, as has already been said, what is happening in antwerp is a blow to thousands of families and to the workers in the supply companies, but i must say to you, mr commissioner, that i am a little shocked by what you are saying. first of all, you say that it is the responsibility of general motors, as if there were no responsibility on the part of the regional, belgian and european authorities. i have already said it here last time, in fact, given that this is a crossborder case involving several countries, the restructuring of a company, the commission should have taken the case to itself to make sure that the european rules were respected. it has not done so, it has left it in the hands of the member states, of germany, and then of the management of general motors. i would urge that in future, when multinational companies are restructured in europe, the commission should take up such a matter as part of its industrial policy, and not merely act as a coordinator between the various economic ministers. secondly, commissioner, i am shocked that you say you still do not have that file. the press is full of talk about the 2.7 billion in state aid that will be given, but the commission comes here to tell you that it has no business plan or dossier yet! i would like to say to mr eppink that between 2000 and 2009 ford did manage to stay open in belgium, and secondly volkswagen did manage to stay open and the audi 1 was produced there, and that this was done by reducing the burden of shiftwork, and therefore the cost of wages. this has kept these companies and has led to new investments. i therefore believe, and i ask him if he does, that opel is indeed a profitable establishment which could remain if we continue with that policy.
i think there would have been a future for opel antwerp if the crisis had not come and the whole concept of general motors had not collapsed. but what mr verhofstadt cannot deny is that the car industry has been in decline in recent years in europe and also in flanders, and mr noels, the economist to whom i referred, said that high wage costs were one of the reasons why the americans closed opel antwerp and not one of the others. opel antwerp was an efficient factory, but that is also what is said about opel in britain, in leuton, and also in the german factories, and so i think that high wage costs always have an eroding effect on industrial policy and that we must prevent that if we want to preserve employment.
the closure of the opel plant in antwerp is a tragedy, not only for the workers and the supply companies, but it is also a tragedy because there is clearly a trend towards the closure of all forms of industrial production in flanders and elsewhere in europe. the commission will examine whether state aid, if any, is granted under strict conditions. it should be possible to grant temporary state aid to an undertaking such as opel antwerp if it is part of a transformation towards more innovative production. it is not the intention, of course, to keep whole branches of industry alive forever and ever, but we must not forget that opel is a profitable company in antwerp with well-trained, highly productive employees and modern equipment. i therefore refuse to believe that we are dealing with some kind of industrial dinosaur which is doomed to disappear as soon as possible. we are being asked in this debate to make another great leap forward. some people say that europe should be given even more powers, on the axiom that more europe automatically leads to a better europe. it would be good if the eu were to adopt a flexible and at the same time consistent approach within the framework of its current powers, for example by ensuring that the rules apply equally to germany and flanders, that the large member states cannot afford to be more generous than the smaller ones. finally, a word to the representatives of the belgian governing parties. you have helped make belgium one of the most expensive countries in europe for industrial production. you have caused the flemish worker to pay considerably more than a colleague in another country, and at the same time to have less to live on, by applying a heavy tax burden, and that too plays a part here, and it is time you reflected on this.

the rapporteur. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by congratulating the european commission on its excellent report. the report on equality between women and men, which we adopt every year, has a special resonance for me. this is my first report to the committee on women's rights and gender equality, of which i am a full member. and it is a report that is absolutely indispensable this year in view of the economic, social and financial crisis that the european union is experiencing in particular. that is why i insisted in my report on the impact of the crisis on women. first of all, men suffered more from redundancies at the beginning of the crisis - this is normal because of the impact in heavy industry in particular - but in recent months, unemployment rates for women and men have increased in the same way, at the same rate. in more than a dozen member states, unemployment remains higher for women, and women are overrepresented in parttime jobs. i therefore ask the commission and the member states to provide accurate statistics on the impact of the crisis on women and men, and for member states to carry out gender impact assessments before implementing a policy of budgetary austerity to avoid disproportionate impact on women. the fight against violence against women is also, i believe, an important point. 20 to 25% of european women suffer physical violence in their adult lives, but 10% of them are also victims of sexual violence. i therefore believe that men must also take action against violence against women. the spanish presidency of the european union has decided not only to put the fight against violence against women at the centre of its priorities by creating a european observatory on violence to have harmonised data, but also to create a european mandate for the protection of victims. i therefore support this approach and also call for the creation of a european year against violence against women so that awareness of this issue is increased in all member states and the fight against it can be made a priority. the job. one finding: women have invested heavily in the labour market. 59.1% of women are now working. the lisbon strategy set a target of 60%. we are almost there, but the gap remains large depending on the member states, as does the average pay gap, which varies, according to the studies, between 17 and 25%. i therefore call on the member states to implement the directives on equal treatment for men and women in employment. i call on those same member states to take legislative measures to promote a balanced presence of women and men in positions of responsibility, particularly in private enterprises. i think we have to fight against gender stereotypes, obviously. i met, in my previous capacity as minister for education, a woman who had to stop her training as a school bus driver because she was being teased by her trainer. i would like to see that in the future no one is surprised that a man can wash dishes or that a woman can also drive a school bus, as i have just mentioned. in short, the commission and the member states should be asked to launch awareness campaigns in schools. sexual and reproductive rights are an important chapter because women must have control over their sexual and reproductive rights. thousands of women have fought for this right at the risk of their lives and today, 24 out of 27 european countries have legalized abortion. the european parliament has a duty to protect this fundamental right of women, and we insist that women should have control over their sexual and reproductive rights, in particular through easy access to contraception and abortion. the paternity leave. it is noted that men have the right to participate fully in the first days after the arrival of their child in the family. it is true that in this connection the commission may be asked to support any initiative to introduce paternity leave at european level. we believe that maternity leave should be combined with paternity leave. i shall conclude, madam president, commissioner. when presented to the committee on women's rights and gender equality, this report received wide praise from representatives of all political groups in the european parliament. the vote in the committee went very well and the report was adopted by a comfortable majority. i therefore invite all my colleagues to support my report again when we vote on wednesday, but i am already looking forward to the debate which will take place and will then be able to answer your questions.
the rapporteur. - mr president, i too would like to congratulate the commissioner on his latest speech - i have often met him in other places - and simply thank everyone for this very rich debate, which involved quite a few colleagues. i've listened to them all and all very carefully, and it's true that there were many people there, and i'm glad of that. in any case, there have been proportionately more than in the committee on women's rights, since we are four out of sixty-one, and that is far too few. i agree with those who have said that this is a fight that men must also fight. i can't answer everyone, but mrs lulling said the vote in committee was uncomfortable. well, 15 yeses against 5 noes, three times as many yeses as noes, and 7 abstentions. i know that there has been a lot of discussion on chapter 38, which deals with reproductive health, sexual rights and in particular contraception and abortion, and i know that this is perhaps a more sensitive subject than others, and i did not want my report to be all-inclusive, but i could not hide this one. i am referring to the teenagers in particular, who, when they become pregnant against their will because they have not had the information that would have been needed, find themselves in distress. what bothers me most is the hypocrisy of those who want to ignore these situations and make fun of the problems of these young teenagers and who, when it happens to one of their children, have the means to offer them a "revitalisation" treatment, in switzerland or elsewhere, which goes quite unnoticed. as for the wage gap, mrs bauer has pointed out very well, it is 25%, if we add the effects of part-time work which is forced. it's hard to tell, of course. it is not about - and i'll end here because i have only a few seconds left - a full-blown case, even though we have talked about genital mutilation, the burqa, forced marriages. i can assure you that in a democracy, i do not consider these practices to be cultural, but they are practices that we must combat through democracy. but if i had put that in the report, it would have obscured everything else that i thought was more important.
on behalf of the alde group. mr president, on behalf of my group, i am delighted to be here today and to finally have the barroso ii commission before us. i think that is good because we are ending a period that was bad for the european union. the fact that we have not had a commission with real powers for six months is something that should not happen again in the future, especially in this period of economic and financial crisis, when there are big issues like climate change, copenhagen and so on. in any case, believe me, in my experience, there must not be a period in the future without real governance by the commission, especially for such a long period of six months. i hope that this commission will start its work as soon as possible after we have voted. we, mr president of the commission, expect three things from you. firstly, that this new commission should be the driving force behind the european union, which we believe has not been the case over the last five years. we are asking this time for a commission that breaks with the past five years and is the real engine of much deeper integration of the european union. indeed, everything that has happened in the last few weeks, in the last few months, shows that what is problematic about europe's position in this multipolar world is the fact that there is no unity of view, not enough european integration - i am thinking of copenhagen or the lack of coordination in haiti. we therefore ask for a commission which, contrary to what has been seen in recent years, does not always seek immediate compromise with the council and then come to us and try to make us swallow it. what we expect from the commission is that it presents ambitious plans to the council - even though we know in advance that it will not be 100% right - and then comes to have a strong ally with the european parliament to convince the council. for a very large number of reasons which led us not to support this european commission, it is obvious that a very limited speaking time can only be used to make a small point. for example, a small part of this is the fact that this european commission is simply continuing on the road of accession of non-european islamic turkey to our european union, although this is absolutely opposed by a large majority of europeans who are never allowed to speak out on this. for example, the fact that this european commission is still advocating the immigration of millions and eventually tens of millions of new non-european immigrants to a continent which already has tens of millions of unemployed. for example, the fact that it has already been shown at the hearings that none of the new european commissioners is prepared to do anything about the democratic deficit. these are all reasons enough not to vote for this new european commission.
i voted against the new commission because there is no indication that the new commission will be working to close the gap between the average european on the one hand and the european institutions, and in particular the european commission, on the other. i heard the then candidate for commissioner for enlargement during his hearing in the committee on foreign affairs and it shows, for example, a very strong willingness to sweep away any objection to the accession of non-european turkey, as has been the case for the past five years. the new commission also wants more economic immigration, more patronage and more meddling, which is a particularly bad thing under the lisbon treaty and offers little reassuring prospect.
in writing. - i have agreed with this commission because it has shown that it can succeed in its work, particularly after its (re) appointment. the result of the negotiations between the commission and parliament is also satisfactory. in particular, i think it is important that the commission should not be given a free hand for the next five years, but should undergo a 'lasting review'. the challenge remains to ensure coherence between the different policy areas, such as environment, social and economic, and to ensure that external communication is such that eu policy is more transparent and understandable to all citizens.
in writing. - i voted 'no' to the question whether we consider barroso ii to be the engine, initiator and inspirer of the european project. barroso is not the forceful leader we need. he let five very crucial years pass. the eu could have pursued a less neoliberal and more social policy in those years. years in which europe could have given smes a boost. years in which he could have helped to support the quality of european independent media and to help democratize european decision-making, thus restoring the confidence of millions of citizens in the european cooperation project. none of that happened. the blind focus on liberalisation, high stock market prices and macroeconomic growth has obscured the social and sustainable face of the eu. the world is afflicted by three crises: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem. a lack of collective response fuels public cynicism and political nihilism. barroso's weak and vague policy plan gives little hope of change. he accepted that member states sometimes put forward candidates who were simply incapable of doing so, and he split the portfolios of some commissioners, leaving it unclear who is really responsible for key policy areas. barroso lacks vision and political courage, which is not good for europe. that's why this is a no.
i think that, unlike the representatives of the ppe group, i am not really in favour of the commission's intervention. i think that we must first of all recognize that what we are experiencing with greece is also the result of the failure of the lisbon strategy. the countries have grown apart over the past decade. the difference between germany and greece has not diminished over the past ten years, but has increased. and that is because we have followed a much too weak lisbon strategy. secondly, i also think that the european institutions, the european commission and the european central bank, have made a tactical, strategic mistake by not acting immediately. they acted much too late. for six weeks, all kinds of statements have been made by european leaders saying that the greek leaders must take action, that the measures are not strong enough, or rather that they have no confidence in the capacity of the greek leaders, etc. we heard everything. i say to you that we are partly responsible for the reaction of the financial markets to greece. how can the financial markets have confidence in a club if the members of that club themselves no longer have confidence in greece and in the measures proposed by greece? so i think there's been a miscalculation. if the european central bank and the european commission had been much more quick to impose a package on greece from the outset, we would not have had the eurozone infection we are experiencing today. and it is not to be said that they were not aware of the greek problems. the european commission has been talking about the greek dossier for three or four months now and has said that at some point there will be a problem with greece. that is the only reason, ladies and gentlemen, for saying that there is a rate on greece with a 12.7% deficit, while there is no rate on england with a 12.9% deficit. so it is no longer a question of the figures, but of the financial markets throwing themselves at greece because we ourselves do not have sufficient cohesion, confidence and solidarity, which could have been avoided by very strong intervention by the european commission and the european central bank. a strategy or a piece of advice, commissioner: let us deal with it ourselves! i am absolutely against the fact that we are bringing in the imf, the international monetary fund, to solve the problems in the euro zone. we are going to solve these problems in the euro zone ourselves, we do not need the imf for that. finally, the last point, which is about more than greece. the current situation is a test case for the cohesion and internal cohesion of the euro.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, it is clear that housing is a difficult issue to deal with at european level because it is not really a community matter. but it is widely recognised that the development of quality housing is necessary to ensure social cohesion and contribute to the success of the commission's recovery plan. i am therefore pleased that the structural funds are increasingly taking into account the financing of investments in the housing sector, particularly in the field of energy efficiency, but we must go further. so, beyond this proposal, special attention should be paid to the homeless - there are an estimated three million of them in europe. this is why this is one of the priorities of the belgian presidency in the second half of 2010. i hope that this will make it possible to put this issue on the european agenda and to work on it in an even more concrete way. this includes accurate methods of counting homeless people to achieve a comprehensive understanding of the phenomenon, and increased support for social housing.
public opinion in europe is understandably opposed to the accession of non-european and islamic turkey. the promise of the previous commission was that negotiations with turkey would be suspended if turkey were found to be failing in its democratic obligations and that the negotiations would have to keep pace with the reform process in turkey. none of those promises were kept. on the contrary, new chapters have been opened again and again, while turkey is going backwards rather than forwards. political parties are banned, kurdish mayors are arrested, christians are victims of intimidation, violence and administrative opposition. critical writers and academics should go into hiding. and i'm not even talking about phenomena like forced marriages and so-called honor killings. what deadline does the commission give turkey to recognise all the member states of the european union? when will turkey have to respect the ankara protocol? when will the illegal military occupation of cyprus be brought to an end?
in writing. - i voted with great conviction in favour of mr david casa's report. as first vice-chair of the committee on budgetary control and rapporteur for a report on vat fraud adopted in september 2008, i have always advocated an efficient fight against vat fraud as it is a major problem for member states' revenues and the proper functioning of the internal market. a common and particularly serious form of this fraud is the so-called vat carousel fraud. the total amount of uncollected revenue is estimated to be between 20 and 100 billion euros per year. that is a huge sum, which could certainly be put to good use in times of economic crisis. criminals active in this sector are highly inventive. it has recently become clear that they are also active in the framework of the emissions trading scheme. transfers of allowances between taxable persons within the ets, which are made exclusively by electronic means, are considered to be supplies of services and are taxable at the place where the recipient is established. the traders buy carbon credits from vat-free sources in other member states and sell them to companies in their member state at a price including vat. ploffers don't pay the vat to the treasury. fighting this kind of crime is crucial.
in writing. to learn from the failure of the copenhagen conference is the priority of the european parliament with this resolution that i voted for. the problems are well known: the un method no longer works, the united states and china have behaved as adversaries in the fight against climate change, and the european union has not been able to speak with one voice. while the problems are well known, the remedies for reaching an agreement in cancún in november 2010 have yet to be invented. to maintain its leadership, europe will need to be climate innovative and offer something other than the single goal of global emission reductions through the highly speculative greenhouse gas emissions trading system, a tool that the us government has just rejected. it is time to change the method and propose a 'technological' bridge between industrialized countries and regions and micro-states that are mainly exposed to climate change. the hopes of tomorrow, those of an agreement at the next cancún summit, the most important ones of a common world vision, will come from the addition of ambitious measures in the field of clean technologies, energy efficiency in buildings and transport, and the promotion of green jobs.
in writing. - i voted in favour of this resolution because it calls for a stronger stance in the negotiations on a global climate policy. moreover, the absence of an international agreement is no reason to delay further eu policy action to deliver on earlier eu commitments to reduce its emissions by 20% by 2020. the ep reiterated its intention to reduce the rate to 30%. the ep is right to state explicitly that the initiatives taken to promote and encourage the green economy, the security of energy supply and the reduction of energy dependence will make it increasingly easier to meet a 30% reduction commitment. the important thing is that lessons are learned from the failure of copenhagen. hence the important self-criticism that the eu has been unable to build confidence in the negotiations through previous specific commitments to international public financing of climate efforts in developing countries. hence the important statement that the eu's collective contribution to developing countries' reduction efforts and adaptation needs for 2020 should not be less than eur 30 billion per year. i hope mexico leads to success!
in writing. the aim of this resolution on the cites convention on endangered species is fundamental: to achieve a european consensus on the prohibition of international trade in bluefin tuna. the fishermen are fiercely opposed to the ban, and scientists and environmental experts are sounding the alarm. at the current rate and despite the drastic efforts made in recent years, bluefin tuna could be gone in as little as three years. italy has already done so by adopting a moratorium as early as 2010. france also, but in a much more ambiguous way, since it talks about an 18-month period to "assess the state of the resource". spain and malta are guilty of silence. however, the emergency is here and only radical measures can allow stocks to be replenished. it is also the only way to safeguard the economic activity of fishermen in the medium and long term. this resolution does not mean that we should ban all fishing for bluefin tuna or that we should have no bluefin tuna on our plates. small-scale and sport fishing will remain permitted. the ban on international trade should be sufficient to protect the species, as 80% of bluefin tuna caught in the mediterranean is exported directly to japan.
in writing. - i voted in favour despite the fact that i thought the original text of the committee on the environment was stronger, especially as regards the reference to bluefin tuna. it is important, however, that the council and the commission of the european parliament receive the signal that we support the inclusion of bluefin tuna in cites annex i. i would have preferred not to have included the three conditions formulated by the plenary, although i did support the amendment which provided for aid for the affected fishing communities. the commission and the member states will also have our support in moving the polar bear from cites annex ii to cites annex i. an important demand to the commission and member states is to reject the proposals of tanzania and zambia to move the african elephant from cites annex i to cites annex ii for trade purposes. the resolution also calls for the eu to take action to combat the spread of the virus and to ensure that the virus is contained in the body of the person who is infected.

member of the commission. − madam president, i would like to thank the honourable members for their oral question, which confirms the interest of the european parliament in the eu-south korea free trade agreement. as this will be the first major trade agreement to which the european parliament will be asked to give its formal consent under the lisbon treaty, i very much welcome this opportunity to reply to the many aspects of the free trade agreement raised in the oral question. i might not be able to give detailed replies to all aspects of the question, but i am happy to address them in a possible follow-up question. the eu-south korea agreement is not only the first fta negotiated by the eu with a country in asia, it is also the most ambitious and comprehensive free trade agreement ever negotiated by the european union. after more than two years of negotiations, the free trade agreement was initialled on 15 october last year. throughout the negotiating process, the commission remained in close contact with parliament. in this respect the commission welcomes the resolution on korea voted by parliament in december 2007. many of the issues raised in this resolution have been addressed in the free trade agreement. the whole process was also accompanied by a constant dialogue with civil society and a comprehensive trade sustainable impact assessment. the fta is currently being translated. by the end of march the commission envisages presenting to the council the decisions to authorise the signature of the fta as well as to conclude the fta. the objective is to sign the agreement in april. the commission is currently completing its analysis as to whether the agreement also falls within the competence of member states. in this case, the fta would require the ratification of all member states before formally entering into force. as this process may take some time, it may be necessary for the commission to propose that the agreement be provisionally applied pending its entry into force. the provisional application is decided by the council on a proposal by the commission. the commission is sensitive to the desire of parliament to be able to give its views on the fta before it is provisionally applied. the commission will continue to seek out the best means, together with the council and parliament, to ensure that this takes place in a manner that does not cause unnecessary delays. it is of great importance that we apply the agreement as soon as feasible. as regards the regulation on safeguards, the commission intends to present its proposal for such a regulation to the council and the european parliament in the coming weeks, and hopes that it will be possible to adopt this legislation rapidly so as to ensure that its application takes place as close as possible to the application of the fta. the economic benefits of the fta are mostly in the eu’s favour. it will relieve eu exporters of industrial and agricultural goods from korean tariffs, saving them 1.6 billion in duties annually, half of these directly from the day of the entry into force of the agreement. obviously potential gains from these duty savings will be even higher, as trade between the eu and korea is expected to expand over time. according to one study, the fta will create substantial new trade in goods and services of 19.1 billion for the eu compared to 12.8 billion for korea. existing studies have not identified any sectors that would be negatively affected by the fta, but in case of serious injury or threat thereof, the commission would not hesitate to activate the safeguard procedures built into the agreement. let me also remind you that, in the event of such a necessity, we have the possibility of employing the support measures under the european globalisation adjustment fund. with regard to intellectual property rights, the fta contains ambitious commitments going beyond wto rules. besides, the commission has been careful to ensure that no element in the fta contradicts the eu acquis communautaire. on the implications of the protocol on cultural cooperation, the fta would set a framework for engaging in policy dialogue on cooperating and facilitating exchanges regarding cultural activities. it is, above all, a demonstration of the eu’s commitment to actively promoting the 2005 unesco convention on the protection and promotion of the diversity of cultural expression. on rules of origin, the negotiations with south korea resulted in a number of changes which are consistent with the ongoing process of reform of such rules. these changes go in the direction of simplification, thus implementing a specific request by the european parliament. at the same time, strict rules have been maintained for all sensitive sectors. the commission has paid particular attention to creating a new market-access opportunity in korea for european fruit and vegetables. in order to encourage korea to open up its market, european union entry prices have been eliminated except in the case of the three most sensitive products: tomatoes, oranges and korean citrus. obviously, the fta also opens the eu market to korean products. to protect our industry against import increases causing or threatening injury, the fta contains an effective safeguard mechanism. this safeguard clause can be invoked from the entry into force of the fta. the commission will monitor the eu market and korean imports to assess the need to invoke the safeguard clause, and remains committed to making use of it if conditions are met. the same applies to the special clause on duty drawback, which can be invoked based on publicly available trade statistics on imports into and exports from korea. i am aware of certain concerns expressed, notably by the eu car industry, about the duty drawback provisions in the fta, but i have to emphasise that duty drawback is currently allowed and used by koreans as well as by eu exporters. thus, maintaining it in the fta does not accrue any new benefit. the current economic impact of duty drawback is very small, about one eighth of the value of the korean duty concessions. in the case of cars, available studies show that, on average, the foreign content of korean cars is between 10% and 15%. if foreign sourcing were to increase significantly, the special clause on duty drawback would provide for an effective remedy. it cannot be ruled out that implementation of the fta may give rise to different views and interpretation between the parties. to address such situations, the fta contains an efficient and rapid dispute settlement mechanism. the commission will monitor closely the implementation of commitments by korea and will not hesitate to invoke dispute settlement procedures if warranted. the fta would also break new ground in promoting the protection of the environment and respect for labour rights, containing comprehensive commitments on a range of social and environmental standards. the scope of these commitments is broader than that of any comparable agreement. to ensure that these commitments are respected, the fta establishes a strong framework for monitoring their implementation, involving civil society, business, trade unions and ngos. finally, i would like to inform you that korea and the eu have been discussing possible adjustments to the tariff dismantlement schedule. these adjustments would imply, inter alia, that products classified under three years and five years would be subject to four- and six-year tariff cuts respectively, so that duties would be eliminated one year later than originally scheduled. while this implies a modest sacrifice in terms of our offensive interests, we thought that such an adjustment would be appropriate, taking into account the sensitivities of certain sectors. korea has indicated its readiness to agree to those changes on the basis that the sensitivities of both sides are equally respected.
on behalf of the ppe group. first of all, commissioner, i would like to wish you every success in your new role. we are in economically troubled times, but we know that you are not afraid of that. as regards this draft free trade agreement with south korea: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. daniel caspary has already referred to it. look at the figures, commissioner, for every car we export from europe to south korea, 15 cars from south korea enter the european union. we are at a ratio of 1 to 15 and that is mainly due to those non-tariff barriers to trade in south korea. it is also no coincidence that a manufacturer like general motors is backing down on its decision to stop producing this new small suv in europe, in the antwerp plant in particular, and to move the whole business to south korea. this future free trade agreement with south korea is the direct cause of this. and i think, commissioner, that we cannot accept that and just sweep it under the rug, arguing that many other sectors in europe are benefiting from this. i think we still have plenty of time to correct this situation for our car industry, and you are right to refer to the safeguard clause, the safeguard measures. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this. the figures and trends are far too disturbing. the fact is, and you know it better than we do, that the american government is also negotiating with the south koreans to make adjustments to a similar agreement. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. it's a lot of jobs that are under a lot of pressure right now. i think we cannot afford to have any more jobs at stake and that is why we are asking you to put extra guarantees in this agreement.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. you yourself have referred to a number of impact studies, but i have not read any impact studies indicating the impact on employment by sector. many have already spoken about the automobile sector. we know what that effect is, both i in my own backyard and a little bit of your backyard too, mr commissioner. the opel car plant in antwerp is closing and the trade unions there - and these are people who have been working on this for years - are really convinced that this trade agreement is a very important part of opel's famous business plan which we are not allowed to see and which is the reason why the suv is not going to antwerp. i therefore have two questions to ask you. do you know what the impact of this agreement is on employment, the effect on employment by sector? if not, are you prepared - and i would urge you to do so - to have an impact study done for the time being, to ensure that we as parliament have that information when we finally have to give our consent? and secondly, do you intend to introduce such social impact studies in the future - as you are just beginning your mandate - ?

barroso, i hope that in a few days’ time, at the beginning of march – i think it is 3 march – you come forward with a paper on this. i hope it will include a bolder strategy than the things that were on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, the goldstone report tries to fulfil the mandate it was given. that mandate is, or was, the following: ‘to investigate all violations of international human rights law and international humanitarian law that might have been committed at any time in the context of the military operations that were conducted in gaza during the period from 27 december 2008 and 18 january 2009, whether before, during or after.’ and that is exactly what the goldstone report has done – although it was not just mr goldstone, it was also his two female colleagues. they conducted investigations on what happened in gaza and came up with conclusions that are disturbing to say the least. they are extremely disturbing because they find in most of the instances they looked into that breaches of international law and international humanitarian law and breaches of the geneva conventions have indeed been committed by the forces of a state that claims to be the sole democratic state in the region. that is indeed very disquieting. we should not let it pass. so when we are called upon to take a position on this report, the question is not whether we are for or against israel, for or against the palestinians, for or against hamas; the question is whether we condone breaches of international law, of international humanitarian law and of the geneva conventions, no matter by whom they were committed. that is what we need to answer.
