vice-president of the commission. − mr president, the train accident in buizingen on monday 15 february 2010 was a shocking tragedy. i understand that in the aftermath of this serious accident several technical and political questions concerning rail safety can be asked. first of all, my thoughts are with the victims of this tragedy and their relatives. it is always difficult to find words of consolation for these sad occasions and silence is sometimes more telling. the causes of the accident are not yet fully known and the technical investigation has been launched in accordance with the provisions of the eu safety directive. it is the responsibility of the belgian investigation body to carry this out. it should be accomplished independently of any judicial inquiry. the objective of the technical investigation is not to apportion blame and liability, but rather to identify causes with a view to improving railway safety and the prevention of accidents. the belgian investigation body has asked the european railway agency to support it in its investigation. two investigators from the agency joined the belgian team in charge of the investigation just a few hours after the accident occurred. i would like to stress that, since the causes of the accident have not yet been clarified, it is inappropriate to draw conclusions. of course, i am prepared to clarify some issues, which were raised in the aftermath of this tragedy and i will share with you my understanding of the situation. as is too often the case when rail accidents happen, remarks alleging a link between european rules or regulations and the accident are made. i would first like to be very clear on the opening-up of the market. alongside the opening-up of the rail freight sector to competition and the establishment of requirements to separate the activities of infrastructure managers and railway undertakings, a stringent regulatory framework has been introduced governing rail safety and interoperability. we want to ensure that the high level of rail safety is maintained, while recognising the procedures and methods specific to each member state in this area. the european railway agency produces reports at regular intervals on the safety of the european network and monitors the development of the situation in the member states. indicators demonstrate that the opening up of the rail sector to competition has in no way had a negative impact on railway safety. let me say very clearly that any proposition linking rail safety levels to the opening-up of the rail market is, in my view, just an excuse to steer the debate away from the true causes of the accident. the question of the coexistence of national and european train control systems can be put in these terms. more than 20 different national systems are used in europe today to ensure the safe movement of trains. these systems developed at national level consist of a trackside component and an onboard component. the trackside component sends information to a computer in the train, and the computer applies the brakes when a dangerous situation is detected. for the systems to work, trains and lines must therefore be equipped with a compatible system. levels of safety and performance of the various national automatic train protection systems are different, as are the rules concerning equipment of lines and locomotives in these national systems. the incompatibility of the different national systems poses a major problem for international trains because either locomotives have to be changed at each border or they have to be equipped with as many onboard systems as there are systems on the tracks that they will run on. there are even cases where different national systems coexist in one single country. the thalys, for example, has to be equipped with seven different national systems to run in four countries. for this reason a single system for use at european level has been designed and developed and is currently being installed on major international lines and trains in europe. the system is known as ertms – european rail traffic management system. as regards the timing we can say that most of the national systems were developed in the early 1980s but their deployment is a long and costly process. in most countries, only part of the national networks and locomotives are equipped today and this partial equipment took approximately 20 years. the ertms specifications have been available since 2000. a number of pilot projects were carried out between 2000 and 2005. since 2005 several ertms equipped lines have been put into service. at present 10 member states have lines with ertms and there are ongoing projects in almost all member states. in belgium, for example, the line between aachen and liège is equipped, and ict trains running on this line are equipped as well. ertms was created primarily with a view to promoting interoperability. that means the possibility for locomotives to cross borders, but it is also recognised as a system offering an advantage in terms of increased safety. the system is fully operational today but, due to the long time needed to install it on board trains and the long lines, existing national systems will continue to co-exist with ertms along the lines. third countries like taiwan, to give just one example, have also chosen ertms, and not just for operability reasons. taiwan invested in ertms simply because the system is the best system available on the market today. several questions were asked about comparing the situation between member states. it is quite difficult and not very useful to establish a ranking of the member states and make meaningful comparisons. it all depends on the choice of indicators, the reference period and the quality of the data reported. one or two serious accidents can also have a significant impact on whatever type of ranking we may have. overall data show that the belgian performance is average. it is however true that belgium lags behind the european average in terms of tracks equipped with an automatic train protection system, be it the national or the european one.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission is not i would like to thank you for your explanations. in the light of the accident in buizingen, respect is obviously due first and foremost to the victims, but also to the investigation. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i think it is quite right, however, that the commission has clearly rejected the belgian railway's assertion that the european safety system has been waiting too long. because we know that security has been and will continue to be a national priority. we at european level - whether the commission or parliament - have always regretted that railway companies do not give greater priority to safety and interoperability between the different systems at european level. after accidents, it is very often the case in politics that new proposals are put forward. i must say that everything was there, both the legislation and the technology. what was perhaps lacking was the will to make this technology a priority. the technology needed to ensure the transition between the european system and the highly qualified system in belgium, the so-called tbl 1+ system, had been available since the mid-1990s. so there was no lack of time or technical specifications. i'm all about the future, and i'd like to make a request. firstly, we should not under any circumstances cut back on training in the railway sector. this was not and is not an obligation on the part of the eu. the railway has highly qualified staff and should continue to do so. secondly, we need firm and binding restructuring plans on the part of the railway companies and the countries. it's not enough to leave it to goodwill. we also need a precise timetable; we will soon have the opportunity here in parliament to inform about precise objectives. the third proposal i would make is that passengers should also have the right to be informed whether the route they use and also the rolling stock meet the highest safety standards. that would be the minimum information that you would have to give to passengers in the future. we should also consider how we can make this compulsory.
i would like to begin by expressing my deepest sympathy, on behalf of the socialist group, to the victims and to the relatives and friends of the victims of the train disaster in buizingen. we must of course await the results of the study to draw any real detailed conclusions, but that does not take away from the fact that railway safety must remain a permanent focus at all levels, including at european level, and that we must therefore regularly evaluate, improve, supplement and adjust our safety policy. i too, like my colleague and like you, commissioner, would like to make it very clear that we regret that some persons with important responsibilities in belgium have wanted to shift the responsibility for the accident onto europe, or at least have given that impression. in particular, the delay in the development of ertms was cited as a reason why only a handful of locomotives in belgium are equipped with that automatic stop system today in 2010. on the other hand, ladies and gentlemen, let us be clear and honest: the development of this great idea, one signalling system to replace the 20 systems, has taken more time than hoped for and than expected. this is all due, of course, to the technical complexity and to the fact that, of course, you have to agree on a standard with 27 countries - at the time there were a few fewer - so a lot of countries. of course, there are budgetary implications, but as you rightly said, this has not prevented any member state from developing its own system or from starting to use a kind of embryonic version of ertms at an early stage. i think we should now look ahead and ask ourselves how we can contribute to a safer railway. i believe that we can contribute to this on several fronts at european level. firstly, thanks to the further deployment of ertms. that deployment plan is in. we budgeted eur 240 million for ten-t in 2009 to help member states. but secondly, and i think this is an important task for the commission and the european railway agency, we must also check whether the existing european safety regulations are actually being enforced on the ground. i will give you an example of directive 2004/49/ec on safety on the community's railways. it requires, inter alia, the establishment of a safety authority in each member state, independent of the railway undertakings and the infrastructure manager, with the task, inter alia, of monitoring, promoting and maintaining railway safety and developing a regulatory framework. i feel, ladies and gentlemen, that this is not working very well in belgium and i think you should organise some kind of audit to see whether the member states really have the capacity to guarantee that safety in their national railway system in a proactive way. that is a task i would like to give you.
i would also like to express my sympathy on behalf of my group to the victims and i would also like to await the results of the inquiry. i hope that this will be done as quickly as possible, but also as thoroughly as possible. in my country a number of questions have been raised about the way in which europe has approached the railways. i have to tell you frankly that i am shocked by people who have said that we did not pay attention to security. i was the rapporteur for this parliament on the security directive to which mr el khadraoui has just referred and i am proud of the work we did then. for we have said: we must set very high standards for security in all the member states and we must create a european framework and set requirements for this. we have had great difficulty getting it through the member states. i note, and mr el khadraoui rightly pointed out, that in some countries - unfortunately also in my own country - the organization of the national application of this directive is actually inadequate. i hope, commissioner, that you will give it some thought. perhaps we should also review this directive in this parliament and tighten up the framework, as mr grosch has said. so it is not that we do not pay attention to safety. - no, it's the opposite. secondly, there is a paragraph in the passenger rights regulation which we hoped we would never need, advances for people who have lost their lives or been injured in railway accidents. i am glad that the nemf has announced that it will use that system and that it will pay advances. times have changed once. there are people who have said that the opening of the market has created insecurity. i would certainly disagree. it's not true! if you look at the latest data from the european railway agency for 2008, you see that's not true. the best countries in 2008 are the uk and the netherlands, two countries that have opened up their markets. i also think we should pay attention to the people, those who will have to do it on the track, the engineers, the people who work in the stations, the people in the administration. we must look at this again and ensure that training, work organisation and the whole company culture are focused on safety first. in conclusion, mr president, we must not lose sight of the fact that the railway is still one of the safest means of transport.

transport. my thoughts are once again with our colleague claudia candeago, but also with all the other victims of this tragedy, their families and all the bereaved railway workers. i will not draw any for the ecr group the investigation into the causes of the train accident in buizingen has not yet been completed and i cannot therefore say anything about it. i do come to the following conclusion. when a train crashes in england , people say , "that's because of the privatization of the railways". the belgian railways receive the highest subsidy in the european union. belgium gives 32 cents to the railways per passenger/kilometre. second is france with 24 euro cents. in the netherlands it is 15 euro cents and in england it is 4 euro cents. despite all these subsidies, the belgian railways still have a debt of 10 billion euros. where's that money going? when i look at the infrastructure of the belgian railways, the neglect is striking. everything is old and worn. is the security system also neglected? maybe too much money goes to staff salaries because of the power of the unions. in belgium, the railways are a socialist domain and are poorly managed. these are questions that must also be asked, because lack of money is no excuse for the belgian railways!
first and foremost, a simple observation. the tax burden in belgium is one of the highest in the world, but in that unmanageable country the taxpayers - and especially the flemish taxpayers - get very little for their money. the railway system is a perfect illustration of this. it is hopelessly outdated and is characterized by multiple and long delays and by overcrowded wagons during peak hours. the management of the belgian railways is highly politicized and inefficient and it is typical of belgian politics that when something goes wrong, nobody is ever responsible for anything. in 2001 there was a train accident in pécrot similar to the one in buizingen last week. nine years ago, everyone thought that there would be serious work on a better security system. well, it didn't. in anticipation of the european train control system, a system of its own was developed, but much too late and far too inadequate. the lesson to be drawn from this is that more investment in security is needed and that the various existing systems need to be better coordinated. and if that does not prove to be possible, the switch to the european etcs should be encouraged as much as possible, because it is not healthy for a thalys train between paris and amsterdam to have as many as seven different security systems.
mr president, the train disaster in buizingen, near brussels, claimed 18 lives and injured more than 160. of course, our first thoughts are with the families. the causes of the accident are still unknown an investigation is under way and there is a need to look at the assessment of railway safety in europe, member state by member state. this evaluation should, of course, cover the safety systems, the state of their implementation, their interoperability and their effectiveness. but this assessment of the safety level of the european rail networks must be understood in a broad sense and, in particular, include an assessment of the working conditions of railway workers, since the best guarantee of safety is the railway worker. i therefore ask the commission to carry out an assessment of the safety of the european rail networks through a comprehensive approach which starts from the launch of the rail liberalization process. it is clear that the time has come to evaluate the concrete results of these policies of liberalization and dismantling of public services without taboos. what has been the added value? who benefited from it? and who was affected? the aim is to compare the implementation of european safety and signalling standards with working conditions since the railway liberalization in each member state became a reality. the commission must therefore consider the role of public services more generally. the ps - the party i belong to - has long been calling for positive action by the european union to protect and develop public services. the idea is that a framework directive on services of general interest would give public services the guarantee of a stable legislative framework, adequate funding, respect for the principles of universality and equal access.

mr president, i will simply reply to mr eppink that we are not at a polling station here. a disaster has happened and claimed lives - others have happened in the past - and it is natural to wonder about its causes. the investigation is ongoing, so we have to let it happen. one factor, however, must never be ignored, and that is the human factor. in any liberalization - i know you are in favour of liberalization, i am much less in favour of it, and not at all in public services - a human factor is neglected. the pressure on the staff is terrible, and they work "endless" hours, at any rate for longer periods without any rest. when you're a train driver, you're committed to the safety of your passengers. i think it is therefore important to ask ourselves what working conditions are and how they are changing. that seems to me to be an important element.

in writing. - i voted against this report because it provides for an increase in the envelope for the recruitment and remuneration of assistants by eur 1500 per month from 1 may 2010. fortunately, a green amendment provides for an evaluation of the compensation system as it currently exists and has been in force since the 2009 elections. but the increase in the secretarial allowance is unconditional and does not necessarily take account of the results of this evaluation. the report also provides for the strengthening of parliamentary committees and political groups. the cost of this operation is eur 13.3 million on an annual basis, of which eur 8.832 million is for the assistant's allowance. this is a bad decision. it damages the reputation of the institution. the decision is ill-considered. no further consequences are contemplated. where are the new assistants housed? in a new building? another extra charge? i am also afraid that much of this money will be used to recruit non-accredited assistants under national pay conditions. in some cases, this system was responsible for fraud. now we may be opening the door wide to abuse. i therefore call for a prior and clear evaluation of the existing system. only then can we make an informed decision.
in writing. blue europe is navigating in troubled waters: fish stocks are dwindling, 400 000 european fishermen and their families are worried about their future and are questioning the effectiveness of the common fisheries policy (cfp), which has so far been a safety net. the structural problems identified, such as overfishing, underinvestment, overcapacity of the fishing fleet and waste (how many tonnes of fish are discarded daily because they are unfit for consumption?), require a reform of the cfp. it is obvious that it must take account of the specificities of the different fishing basins and avoid a single management model at all costs. we do not fish in the same way and with the same boats in the north sea and the bay of biscay. that is why the reform proposed by the commission, based on regionalisation and supported by the european parliament this afternoon, is essential. responsible policy must also take account of the reality of the figures: the european union accounts for about 4.5% of world fisheries production and is not meant to be a 'giant' in the sector. all the more reason to opt for small-scale, innovative and sustainable fishing.
in writing. - i abstained on the vote on the report on the common fisheries policy because it contains too many points which not only contradict a number of other points in the report but also run counter to my political view of the european fisheries policy. on the one hand, the report states that european fishing fleets should no longer overfish the waters of developing countries, but on the other hand it also states that fisheries policy always takes precedence over environmental and development policy. this position is manifestly contrary to the treaty of lisbon. article 208 of the treaty states that our foreign policy must not undermine the development objectives of poor countries. the policy proposed by parliament is certainly positive for europe, but outside european waters europe is only concerned with its own fishing fleets and not with the fish stocks and the fishermen on the spot. that is why the greens abstained. the report also acknowledges that there is a major problem of overfishing and that sustainability should be the guiding principle of policy, but it fails to recognise that the capacity of fishing fleets and the subsidy and quota systems are at the heart of this problem. hence this abstinence.
president. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, our new commissioner has already said it. the legislative framework is no longer adapted to today's reality, given the very changed travel market, which has caused many problems and frustrations not only for consumers but also for travel agents, agencies and tour operators. it is clear that the number of people still effectively protected by the current directive has fallen dramatically despite the fact that more people are travelling. the european parliament has indeed been calling for a revision of the directive for years. you have said it, the bottlenecks and the possible solutions are known. another consultation just concluded. i think it's high time we cut some knots. i welcome the statement made by the commissioner, but i would like to make a few points explicitly, which are very important to us. firstly, the scope of the directive must certainly be clarified and even extended. more and more consumers - we have heard this before - are putting together dynamic travel packages or booking a separate travel service. we have seen, for example, recently, with the recent problems with the eurostar, that thousands of tourists got into trouble because they were not compensated for hotels they had booked or for theatre tickets they had bought. this is unacceptable. i think that passenger rights should also be clearly stated in the directive. of course, we must take due account of the very different travel markets, the different holiday behaviour of consumers in the different member states and national jurisprudence. but harmonization is necessary because some concepts are very different, such as tour operator, mediator, force majeure. i read in the commission's 2007 consultation that there was nevertheless very detailed feedback from industry and stakeholders. so i wonder what the problem is. why not decide now? they also asked for some kind of travel security label. i would like to hear the commissioner's opinion on this. a second point concerns the role and responsibility of the travel agent. it is necessary to define it much more clearly, because consumers are bombarded with a lot of information on the internet, which is not always correct and sometimes even quite harmful, to the point of paying rent for holiday homes that do not even exist! the role of the travel agent should therefore be standardized much more precisely. we've already discussed the bankruptcies. in a resolution parliament has made a very clear call for better protection of the passengers concerned. as a resident of a border region between flanders and the netherlands, i would also like to draw particular attention to crossborder sales, because protection is often limited to the travel contract concluded in a particular member state. finally, information to consumers on prices. in most other sectors, prices, including those of the travel services sold, should be fixed and all inclusive. do you share this view? i'd like to hear your views on that. the rights of consumers should also be clarified in cases of force majeure and in the event of changes in the supply of travel services, and information obligations should be stricter and more targeted. perhaps we should also consider introducing sanctions in the new directive. i hope that the commission will make some progress very soon and that we can discuss a new proposal here in parliament.
mr president, this is one of those cases where the european union can really add real value by offering a form of protection to the millions of people in the european union who go on holiday and travel in europe. cross-border problems require a cross-border strategy and approach. we all agree that the 1990 directive is hopelessly out of date. twenty years ago, most people would choose a trip from the travel brochure and then go to a local travel agent to book their trip. today, more and more people are planning their own vacations and booking travel via the internet. in addition, relatively new phenomena have emerged, such as the emergence of low-cost carriers in the airline industry and the growth of the cruise industry. what do i think should be included in this new directive? first, a definition of the scope of the directive. in short, what types of package holidays are covered? secondly, there must be a precise definition of legal liability and - last but not least - there must be a strong protection of consumers in the event of the bankruptcy of an undertaking. only if these matters are settled in a way that is watertight will the reform be successful and the millions of consumers in the european union be better protected.

vice-president of the commission. − the train accident in buizingen on monday 15 february was a shocking tragedy and, in the aftermath of this serious accident, several technical and political questions concerning rail safety can be asked. the causes of the accident are not yet fully known and the technical investigation has been launched in accordance with the provisions of the eu safety directive. the belgian investigation body has the responsibility for carrying this out. two investigators from the european railway agency joined the belgian team in charge of the investigation just a few hours after the accident had occurred. i would like to stress that, as long as the causes of the accident have not been clarified, it is inappropriate to draw conclusions. as is all too often the case when rail accidents happen, remarks alleging a link between european rules or regulations and the accidents have been made. i would first like to be very clear on the opening-up of the market. alongside the opening-up of the rail freight sector to competition and the establishment of requirements to separate the activities of infrastructure managers and railway undertakings, a stringent regulatory framework was introduced covering rail safety and interoperability. we have carefully monitored this opening-up of the rail sector to competition to ensure that it has no negative impact on rail safety, and the indicators clearly show no such impact. i also see no relationship between the accident and the reasoned opinion we sent to belgium in 2008 regarding the lack of independence between infrastructure managers and rail undertakings. any proposition linking rail safety levels to rail market opening is, in my view, just an excuse to steer the debate away from the true causes of the accident. the question of the coexistence of national and european train control systems can be put in these terms. more than 20 different national systems are used in europe today to ensure the safe movement of trains. the incompatibility of the different national systems poses a major problem for international trains because either locomotives have to be changed at each border or they have to be equipped with several onboard systems. for this reason, a single system for use at european level has been designed and developed and is currently being installed on major international lines and trains in europe. the system is known as ertms, the european rail traffic management system. as regards the timing, most of the national systems were developed in the early 1980s, but their deployment is a long and costly process. in most of the countries where these systems exist, only part of the national networks and locomotives have been equipped to date, and this partial installation of the equipment took approximately 20 years. ertms specifications have been available since 2000. a number of pilot projects were carried out between 2000 and 2005. since 2005, several ertms equipped lines have been put into service. at present 10 member states have lines with ertms and there are projects ongoing in almost all member states. in belgium, for example, the line between aachen and liège is equipped and intercity trains running on this line are equipped. the ertms will, therefore, probably coexist with national systems for a period of 20 years. some member states will benefit earlier than others from the european system. we see, for example, that the italian and spanish high-speed network is already almost fully equipped, that the conventional network of luxembourg is also almost fully equipped, while in 15 member states there are only pilot lines or projects. it should also be noted that automatic train protection systems are just one element contributing to the safety of the network. appropriate training, sound maintenance and better protection of level crossings are other important components for safety. if we take a broader range of safety indicators, overall data show that railway safety standards in europe are generally very high.
the lessons to be drawn from this tragedy, mr president, commissioner, are indeed a question for the belgian services, for the belgian authorities. in fact, a special committee of inquiry will be set up in the belgian parliament shortly to look into the matter. one more question for you, commissioner. what is your view of the social aspect, the aspect of the workload for the train crew, and especially for the train drivers? the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.

vice-president of the commission. − mr president, i should like to thank mr simpson and the committee on transport and tourism for initiating this discussion and for promoting competitiveness and openness in the railway sector. i have always had strong support in parliament and i hope this will continue. the report on rail market monitoring which the commission published at the end of 2009 shows that the progressive decline of the railways since the 1970s has been stopped in all market segments after the opening of the market and adoption of the first package. so there are some positive notes as well. however, the economic crisis has had a serious impact on rail, with rail freight operators losing up to 30% of their business; this crisis has highlighted and increased existing structural problems of the railways. these problems are on the one hand linked to the economics of rail and the persisting financial weakness of some actors. a number of member states still fail to ensure sufficient budget for the infrastructure managers. this not only results in underinvestments which undermine the quality and performance of the rail network, it also builds up indebtedness levels. on the other hand, there are still economic and technical barriers to market entry. very often new entrants find themselves discriminated against, in particular where incumbent rail operators also have indirect control over the provision and use of rail infrastructure. the newly established regulatory bodies do not all have the necessary powers and independence to ensure fair and transparent market conditions. the commission adopted a two-tier approach to tackle these problems: infringement procedures to address incorrect implementation of the rules, and changes to the rules when they were not clear or precise enough. the first line of approach – infringement procedures – required a detailed analysis of the legal situation in all the 25 member states that have railway systems and resulted in the reasoned opinions that were sent out in 2009. the main problems are, first, insufficient implementation of the provisions of the directive on track access charging; second, lack of independence of the infrastructure manager in relation to railway operators and the failure to ensure sufficient independence, resources and powers for the regulatory body. the second line of approach was to take advantage of the announced process of recasting the existing rail packages to propose improvements to the existing rules on rail market access. in parallel we will pursue our holistic approach with a view to achieving a genuine internal market for rail. we will continue to promote the technical harmonisation of rail in conjunction with the european railway agency.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals. if the belgian painter magritte had painted the guidelines of the first railway package, he would certainly have written under them: 'these are not guidelines'. in fact, i think the whole discussion we have had for some time is almost surreal. we made decisions in 2003, implementation should be completed by 2006, and in 2010 we ask the question why 21 countries do not join what they themselves signed up to. the liberalisation should allow new players to enter the market. that was the theory. the practice here is also quite different. and today we are in a situation where we would have to make an assessment of this liberalization, whether we are opponents or supporters of liberalization in this area, and we are faced with the problem that it has not been implemented to a large extent. the companies, for their part - we have seen this in various countries - have taken decisions in the name of so-called liberalisation, affecting personnel and technology, which were not always pleasant, although this liberalisation was not implemented. against this background, we must also note that historical railway beneficiaries, in this sense, continue to hold the keys to the opening of the market. these keys can open the door to an open market, but they can also close the door. and that was the case in most countries and is still the case today. that is why the proposals you have made here, which we have briefly noted, are a first step. i think that in order to assess liberalisation correctly, we must implement it quickly or force it through with the means that the commission has or will have to provide.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, commissioner, first of all i would like to point out that the market share of rail freight transport first declined from around 13% in 1995 to 10.5% in 2002, and then stabilised. in passenger transport, where liberalisation has not really worked or is not being applied, we have actually seen an increase in recent years. i would like to say that market opening can only be a means and that a successful single european railway market must actually be a combination of measures. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. only if we tackle this in a coherent and coherent way can we succeed in our endeavour. commissioner, i have one more question. we are told that there is indeed a review of that first railway package. the question is: i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter.
for the greens/efa group mr president, commissioner, the inspiration for this first railway package was almost 15 years ago. at that time, the main objective, which i of course share, was to gain market share for the railways. liberalisation, which was one of the means to achieve this, has produced mixed results and has not always been very successful. as we have said, rail freight market shares are stagnating while road is gaining market shares. at the same time, the number of passengers has increased considerably, while there has been no process of liberalisation and the high-speed rail network, which is built on the basis of cooperation and not competition, is rather a success. besides, you mentioned some new entrants. these new entrants are very few in number and many of them have been absorbed by large companies. in other words, i am not sure that the monopoly of large companies was the goal. as regards implementation, if we refer to the number of infringement procedures, there is objectively a well-known problem, in particular with regard to the lack of independence of the regulatory and appeal bodies, including where there is a functional or institutional separation, a separation which can also lead to other problems and costs in terms of internal coordination. in the meantime, commissioner, i can only encourage you to adopt a prudent approach, one that does not force things, that continues the holistic approach you have mentioned, that goes through the evaluation of the previous packages without bias, but that goes through the evaluation first before taking the next step. this assessment must therefore be uncompromising, including with regard to working conditions, safety and security, public service obligations, insufficient internalisation of external costs, before further committing to the liberalisation process. i would therefore be interested to hear what your priorities are in this area, given some of the progress - it must be acknowledged and others have said it - namely, better transparency in accounting reporting, progress in interoperability, harmonization of training, licensing, and in signage and safety. but there is still a lot to be done and i would ask for a careful assessment, without complacency and without taboos, so as not to move too quickly on to the next steps.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0179/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0179/97). madam president, i first of all wish to welcome you and to express the hope that your excellent and substantial speech today does indeed mark the end of a particularly difficult period for all of us, which began in november when the mandate of the previous commission was coming to an end. because if one thing is clear, it is that we cannot afford such long periods of business continuity. in the period from the end of november until recently it seemed - i am sorry to say - as if the eu had disappeared from the world scene, or almost disappeared, and we cannot - i repeat - under any circumstances allow that to happen. because the world is not waiting for us, of course. you have quoted a number of figures which clearly illustrate this. but a number of events have also illustrated this clearly: i am not going to go into the details of the situation, but i would like to say that i am very pleased that the commission has taken the initiative of putting forward a proposal for a regulation on the protection of the environment and the protection of the environment. we therefore need a high representative/vice-president of the commission who has the ability to be present on the ground and also in the european and other decision-making centres in the world. you knew, and we knew, that you were embarking on a nearly impossible task. i admire you for taking them on. we promised you we would support you in that. we are glad to hear you today and to hear you speak strongly about the european external action service, which we all so badly need. if everyone would stop fighting what are called turf wars - one with gloves and the other without - then i am sure that we can all prepare well for the future. thank you for your attention.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. but we have unfortunately not seen much of a strategic vision. what do you propose to do, for example, to ensure that there is a greater rapprochement with russia, rather than allowing russia to continue to slip into collusion with criminal regimes such as those of iran and north korea? what are your proposals in relation to iran's efforts to develop nuclear weapons? what attitude will you take to the growing anti-western and anti-european tendencies in the muslim world? this is a trend which can also be observed in a candidate country like turkey. are you prepared, mrs ashton, to defend european achievements, such as the right to freedom of expression and the separation of church and state, in a clear and uncompromising way in the face of the growth of political islam? the weak stance adopted by the eu a few years ago in connection with the danish cartoon crisis is, in my view, not likely to be repeated. i would also like to ask you, as mr provera has done, whether you are prepared to use the common foreign and security policy to control the mass flow of immigration into europe? i'm talking about both illegal and legal immigration flows. you didn't answer that.
for the greens/efa group mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i think that the vote we have just seen is remarkable in two respects. the first is that the three main groups decided to make a joint resolution by tabled six minutes before the deadline, which made it impossible for the other groups to amend. this closed-minded attitude is not worthy of this parliament. second, if only to produce a text that made sense! at a time when the commission, with the same political families, is able to come up with five objectives, with six policies to support those objectives, this parliament is coming out with a resolution that says absolutely nothing about nothing, with the support of the three big political families. i think it contributes to the ridicule of this parliament which, in a debate as important as europe 2020, is capable of nothing but breaking open doors.
in writing. the european parliament approved on wednesday a proposal to remove the obligation for small and medium-sized enterprises to publish their annual accounts. in an effort to reduce the administrative burden, the european commission has proposed that countries that wish to do so may exempt their smes from the annual reporting requirement under current eu legislation. i was not in favour of this repeal because because of the commission's proposal, the european regulatory and harmonised framework will disappear for more than 70% of european companies. the option of exempting micro-entities from the obligation to draw up and publish annual accounts will not result in a reduction in administrative burden. my fear now is that irregular application by member states of the option to exempt micro-entities will lead to a fragmentation of the single market. this is also why belgian meps and the government have fiercely opposed the european proposal (belgium has also gathered around it a blocking minority in the council of ministers, where the proposal has yet to be voted on).
in writing. i voted against the lehne report and the commission proposal. this is a step backwards in terms of the internal market, with a clear risk of distortions of competition between small european smes. let us be clear: the consequences of this proposal have been woefully underestimated, and they have not taken into account the fact that in the absence of a european directive, each member state will impose its own rules in this area. it is therefore not surprising to see all the associations for the defence of smes, european associations - and also belgian ones, as far as i am concerned - the union of the middle classes, the feb - massively opposed to this proposal. yes, a thousand times yes to reducing the administrative burden on businesses, and in particular on smes, but in a coherent way, through a proposal for a general revision of the 4th and 7th company law directives.
in writing. - the adoption of the 'small business act' by parliament in march 2009 launched a series of policy proposals to make the business environment in europe more sme-friendly, including through administrative simplification. the abolition of the accounting system for so-called micro-entities may appear to be a major administrative simplification, but given the importance of financial information for all stakeholders including creditors, tax administrations and trading partners, it will open the door to greater administrative burdens and higher costs. moreover, the companies deny themselves a useful tool of internal business monitoring. i do, however, support the request made by the economic affairs committee, in particular a thorough impact assessment of the meaning of a possible exemption for micro-entities, to be framed in a general review of the fourth and seventh directives. in my view, the proposal does not contain the right instruments to deal effectively with the problem of red tape. for these reasons i voted against the commission's proposal. since the report by my very respected colleague mr lehne uses the same principles as the commission proposal, i was unfortunately unable to support his report either. i look forward to a wise and considered decision by the council.
in writing. there were high hopes for the lisbon strategy, whose economic, social and environmental objectives were as necessary as they were ambitious. the successor strategy, the europe 2020 strategy, which was voted on today, seems to me to be much less ambitious. while it retains some targets to be achieved, such as the 3% of gdp to be devoted to research and the maintenance of the stability and growth pact (3%), it is nevertheless regrettable that employment and the (very reduced!) environmental dimension do not occupy a transversal place. we are still a long way from a truly comprehensive strategy for sustainable development! nevertheless, i supported this proposal because it is very clear that in the face of the crisis and its many consequences, especially on employment, we cannot sit idly by. i welcome the adoption of the paragraphs on an ambitious social agenda and improving support for smes. in short, a new breath was needed. let us hope that the europe 2020 strategy will bring it. above all, we hope that the 27 member states will do their utmost to make this strategy effective.
in writing. europe is not the most competitive economy in the world in 2010. far from it, with a gdp declining by 4% and 23 million citizens unemployed, it has a not very good record. if the european union needs a severe shock to put the economy and employment back in a virtuous circle, it must do so with similar objectives but a profoundly revised methodology compared to that of the lisbon strategy. it must also take account of the negotiations that await it in the fields of energy, climate, industry and agriculture. that is why i support the pragmatic approach of the new 2020 strategy. ensuring that 75% of the working age population is actually in employment, and achieving the 3% of gdp target for research, remain more than ever the objectives that europe must achieve. but the 27 capitals still have to play along. this is why we ask that we consider penalties and incentives for good and bad students in the 2020 strategy (§14). punishment on one side, incentives on the other. the stick and the carrot. it's old as the world, but it works!
in writing. - i voted against the joint resolution because the three major parties seem to be endorsing the barroso ii commission's business as usual approach. my constituents expect a different approach and want europe's eu 2020 strategy to be a green new deal, a 21st century green revolution, reconciling human development with the physical limits of the earth. the european commission remains committed to uncritically increasing gdp growth. greens and environmentalists want the eu 2020 strategy to be transformed from a purely gdp growth strategy into a broader political concept of the eu's future as a social and sustainable union that puts people and environmental protection at the centre of policy and aims to create wellbeing and the best opportunities for all. in our view, gdp should include a range of indicators of well-being, as well as indicators which take account of broad external economic factors and pressures on the environment. my group therefore submitted an eight-page text detailing our alternative approach. i prefer that text to the compromise of the three largest groups.
in writing. i voted against the resolution on the eu 2020 strategy because paragraph 6 on the restructuring of social security systems and greater flexibility for employers was adopted. furthermore, it is more like a catch-all of more or less good intentions to the detriment of precise quantitative and qualitative objectives; the eu's lisbon strategy is a success story, but it is not the only one.
in writing. - president, ladies and gentlemen, in the short term, it is crucial to work on an exit strategy from the crisis, but in the medium term more is needed. if we really want to give the social market economy, our social model, a chance, we need more economic growth, green growth that makes us competitive and creates new jobs. furthermore, investing in research and development into innovative products, manufacturing processes and services is crucial to maintaining our standard of living in the global economy. this drive for structural reforms is reflected in the 2020 strategy proposed by the commission. it is also crucial that the commission should shift its focus from the current situation to a smaller number of measurable targets to be set by the member states. as this resolution rightly states, we are still hungering for the enforceability of the objectives proposed. the lack of a genuine sanction mechanism for failure to achieve the objectives, even if there is insufficient effort, leaves this 2020 strategy with the same defect as its predecessor. the joint resolution provides a good basis for further discussions with the commission, the council and the president of the european council. i therefore strongly supported this resolution.
in writing. - i voted in favour of the joint resolution on the goldstone report, not least because it recognises that the people of gaza continue to live in appalling conditions as a result of the blockade and calls for the immediate, permanent and unconditional opening of the border crossings. the adopted text calls for the implementation of the recommendations in the goldstone report and for accountability for all violations of international law, including allegations of war crimes. the report is the result of balanced and thorough research, after site visits and interviews with witnesses. the report states that violations of international humanitarian law have been committed by various parties. the report points out the following responsibility of senior officers in the israeli army: the indiscriminate use of white phosphorus, the failure to distinguish between civilians and combatants, the human consequences of the blockade, which is a collective punishment and a violation of the laws of war. the report contains enough elements to call on the un secretary-general and the security council to initiate a legal procedure, which is the best guarantee to dispel all doubts and discussions about the events in gaza. i regret that the epp did not support this text.
madam president, commissioner, minister, the death of orlando zapata is a dramatic testimony to the despair to which the lack or absence of freedom can lead. as the minister said, this obviously should not happen. we must denounce the continued imprisonment of prisoners of conscience and demand their release. we cannot condone the arbitrariness of a power which obstinately refuses to open itself to the exercise of the most fundamental freedoms, but i am convinced that we cannot deprive ourselves of the virtues and prospects of a political dialogue which remains, more than ever, the most tangible expression of our european values. relations between cuba and the european union have been complex for a very long time, often based on blindness and misunderstanding which have led to serious rigidities and which regularly undermine progress or prospects for political dialogue. we all know that cuba is now at a turning point in its history. i am more convinced than ever that we would be wrong not to preserve the advantages and advances, however modest, of a dialogue nourished by particular historical, cultural and linguistic ties. the european union is perhaps the only political power capable of convincing the cuban people that the isolation in which they are confining themselves is suicidal and can only lead them, sooner or later, to a tragic fate. we cannot escape the responsibility we have to continue the dialogue without excluding any difficult issues but without practicing - as i believe is too often the case - double standards.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, since i would like commissioner rehn to understand me loud and clear, i will speak in english. i would like, starting from where we are today, to take a look forward. where are we? we have huge and unsustainable public deficits and, mind you, the greens do not support this kind of deficit. second, we cannot ignore the fact that we have already huge and deepening social inequalities. 16% of europeans below the poverty level is not a small figure; 40% of young spaniards under 25 unemployed is not a small figure, and i could carry on. then we are facing climate change and resource scarcity, and all this means there is a need for investment in infrastructure, in education, in research and innovation, etc. so what we believe is that we should really switch gear. herman van rompuy said, after the february summit, that coordination of macro-economic policy needs to be considerably increased and improved. of course. what does that mean? it means of course that in terms of budgetary expenditure we need to have more peer review, more ex ante control. greece has 4% of its gdp going to military spending. they have got an air force that is as big as the luftwaffe. i mean, how come? it is such a tiny country and they are arming themselves like hell. but if we just look at the expenditure side, we will not succeed. we need to look, and to take a hard look, at coordinating our tax revenue because, what do we need to do? we need to rebalance tax income away from labour income towards other forms of income, including capital income. we need to make sure that we have an effective corporate contribution – effective, not just on paper – and that means ccctb country-by-country reporting, etc. we need to implement the financial transaction tax; we need to implement the carbon tax, both to shift behaviour and to provide income. and we cannot do that just by asking countries to coordinate their policies. we need more integration. if we do not do that, our governments will prove incapable of reconciling the need to balance budgets and to meet social needs and investment needs. so i believe that europe is now at a critical juncture. the choice is between a higher degree of integration, not just coordination, and decline. the lesson that i take from copenhagen is not just that we missed the climate agreement. it is that europe has proved to be irrelevant if it is not acting together. we spend too much time coordinating, too little time acting together. so that would be our contribution to this debate.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i would like to make two remarks on last month’s council. the first concerns the mechanism which the council decided on for greece and about which i have serious doubts. i hope that i am wrong, but i do not think that it can work for the simple reason that it is a system of bilateral loans and not a system – a european solution – under which a single loan is made by the european commission to greece, which was the initial idea. (applause)if you look at the markets, it is very apparent at the moment that they do not believe in this system. this did not start yesterday, but a week ago now. last monday – one week ago – there was already an issue surrounding greek bonds being at an interest rate of nearly 6%. that is 300 base points higher than the lowest interest rate in the european union, which is 3%. since there is now a discussion inside the european union between different member states about what the interest rate applicable to the bilateral loans should be, that even increased yesterday to 400 base points, which is 4%. that is not the way to help greece. greece has to take the measures which are necessary, but not with bilateral loans. that mechanism is not in fact helping greece, but punishing it at the moment. it is absolutely necessary that the european commission return as fast as possible to its initial idea of a european loan, issued by the european commission. then you will automatically have lower interest rates than those on the markets today, because there will be this guarantee from the european commission and the european institutions. that is the only way to help the greek government achieve its targets. at the same time, the greek government naturally has to stop its internal struggles. if there are differing points of view with regard to intervention by the international monetary fund, and should such discussions continue, then interest rates will also automatically increase. my second point is that what we need now is more than merely a mechanism for greece or other package of the magnitude which we had in the past from jacques delors, who at a certain moment came forward with a package of european economic and monetary union, or the internal market, to solve the problems. we need that now. we need a courageous package. only the european commission can do this. the council cannot do it – not even the president of the council. it is the commission which has the right of initiative, and it is the commission who must now come forward with a real package. president van rompuy, you will not be surprised to learn that not only i but my whole group are very disappointed with the council's results. you said in your reply that the council is very aware of the balance of the commission's proposal between the economic, the ecological and the social aspects. only you have ignored this balance in the results. you have indeed achieved results in the field of the labour market and r & d. you've been preaching the status quo on climate policy. the 2020 objectives have long been accepted in this parliament and in the various european institutions. you have not - and that is a real shame - respected the poverty objectives, at least in my view, by the fallacy of going to school a little more, you have covered up the disagreement that undoubtedly exists within the council over this social objective with a little love. i am very sorry about that and it is a slap in the face to the 80 million poor people in the european union. you have always - and you are proud of it - presented your role with both modesty and ambition. modest, because you do not take on the role of president of the european union, but ambitious, because you say that your role is primarily to develop a long-term strategy and to implement it in the coming years. well, this is your test - the 2020 strategy and the targets in all those possible areas. you have a re-exam in june. we support you, but the strategy must include clear objectives on poverty.
on behalf of the s&d group, i would like to say that it is of course a little surreal to have a debate on the blacklist at a time when the whole of european aviation is in chaos, but i understand that we will have the opportunity tomorrow to have a topical debate and we will do so. the blacklist is an important issue in itself and we can say that it has been a success, thanks in part to the impulse of mrs de veyrac, who indeed worked hard on it a number of years ago. we must build on and learn from the experience we have gained. in some areas, some additional efforts are still needed. firstly, in terms of communication with the passengers. it is true that passengers should be informed if they are going to take a blacklisted aircraft somewhere, through a travel agent or something, but often it is passengers who decide on the spot whether to take a domestic flight. i would therefore suggest that we should examine whether it is not possible for travel agencies and airlines which book travel to countries where there are unsafe airlines or unsafe aircraft to inform passengers automatically, even though they are not themselves on the blacklist. we should think about that. a second element is to help avoid adding aircraft, airlines and countries to that blacklist. the european union could do much more to help build capacity in third countries, and certainly in the poorest countries, by providing know-how and by ensuring that the aviation authorities also have the necessary capacity to deal with this. one point raised by mrs de veyrac is, of course, the deception. are we sure we have a waterproof system? are there not gaps here and there which allow airlines from a country on the blacklist to operate via other countries, islands? examples of this are known. finally, i also think we should evolve towards a global blacklist. i would like to know what efforts you have made, for example, in the field of icao, to come to such a list with colleagues from other countries.
the commission has already done so. the blacklist was an interesting achievement of this parliament, led by christine de veyrac and her colleagues of the previous legislature. the blacklist can concern companies, countries or aircraft, and i think that the questions raised in this context should be answered a little more precisely by the commission, especially as regards the way in which companies' rights of participation are regulated in different countries and why and why this procedure is being carried out in relation to individual aircraft. we are also interested in further development. we have already stated in the debate a few years ago that it remains important for us that the commission should improve its follow-up of these acts and measures in communication at least to parliament, because we still have some questions open to us, not least the control of airlines or aircraft that might suddenly fly under a different name, and in view of the fact that today, especially the decisions that could be taken at the last minute, also require a certain degree of transparency. this brings me to the most important point for me, namely information. we are all of us of the opinion - and i think this applies across the groups - that citizens and passengers have the right and therefore the obligation to ensure that companies systematically provide information on the blacklists, that this information is readily available at airports, not least in travel agencies, and that, particularly when flying to certain countries which are already known to have problems, travel agencies should indicate which companies are problematic when there are rebookings. the aim is for this measure, which has already substantially improved safety, to be extended not only to europe and european passengers, but to the whole world.
i would like to thank the rapporteur for his excellent work, some would even say fantastic work. so thank you and all those who have contributed. perhaps i should remind you that the problem of funding is not new. this case has actually been going on since the moment we adopted a european regulation on the adoption of security measures at european level after the attacks of 11 september 2001. the european parliament has always stressed from the outset that this problem of financing is serious and that we must find a european solution to it. it has always been the council that has prevented this. the council has already made a number of proposals, and i am sure that the commission will be able to accept them. there are, of course, a number of aspects. the first and most essential is the element of transparency. we are not at present taking a position on who exactly is to foot the bill in each of the member states. but what we do want is that consultations be organised, that agreements be reached and that the agreements be respected, in other words that the revenue from safety charges actually be used to finance safety measures and not, for example, some kind of parking. that is essential and it must be clear that we cannot give in on this point. the second important element is that we want to distinguish between security measures which we have agreed on jointly, all of them at european level, and other additional measures which are taken by individual member states. the first is that the rules on transparency are clear and must be applied, the second is that we must make it clear that the financial responsibility lies with the member states and that they must pay the bill for these additional security measures. it is also a bit of an incentive to get more and more european coordinated security measures and if member states decide on their own to introduce body scans and the like, well, then they have to bear the financial consequences of that too.
the commission has already done so. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for his work, as well as the other groups. we had an interesting discussion in the committee on transport and tourism. but i think we have already made many compromises with the council, which has shown itself to be very uncompromising here and in the past. because when it comes to the application, the discussion is already underway. the commission's proposal is to be adopted by the council and the european parliament, and the council is to be consulted on the proposal. we should not repeat what we have already done in other areas - whether it is ground handling or air fares - that there are exceptions affecting two or five million passengers. the second point, which is even more important to me, is that passengers are of course given transparency about who is paying for what and how much, and that this is clearly a requirement for both airports and airlines. because that's a very important aspect in my eyes. this brings me to the third point, which is always the sticking point when it comes to payment: financing. i personally believe that public safety should be paid for with public funds. we are already making the compromise that we will leave some things in the so-called subsidiarity. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. personally, i'm not very happy about it but good. i think that we should at least stick to what we have said here, namely that special measures, more stringent measures, must in any case be paid for by the public authorities. security fees and overall security have increased sharply in recent years. that is why this dossier has an economic aspect. we are now talking about 30% of the cost. and when we talk about security and who pays for it, there will be an economic aspect to it tomorrow. that is why these three points are crucial for me. i hope that we will at least be able to make the council and the commission accept the present demands.
deputising for the rapporteur. − madam president, the chair of the committee on transport and tourism, mr simpson, is not able to join us today and has asked me to read out his statement. the recast on the development of the guidelines of the ten-t network seeks to bring together the various amendments adopted over the years to the guidelines and to incorporate amendments that take account of the enlargement of the eu to 27 member states. to give a bit of background to the report, the guidelines give direction to the development of the trans-european transport network. the objective is to establish the network gradually by 2020 by integrating land, sea and air transport infrastructure networks throughout the union. turning back to the report at hand, it is, strictly speaking, a codification of the legislative text, but, because the commission made some minor changes to the annex, they were forced to do it via the recast procedure. the changes to the annex are to correct initial mistakes that were made during the accession of the 10 new member states that joined in 2004. the changes are not about updating the maps or making any substantive changes. the two changes the rapporteur has introduced to the report involve minor modifications in the recitals and some technical corrections to the maps. these are in line with the changes made by the council and in line with correcting the guidelines. following the commission’s advice, the decision was taken, both by council and by mr simpson as rapporteur with the backing of the transport committee, not to make any substantive changes to the guidelines. the rapporteur is, however, aware that the greens have retabled their own amendments seeking to update the line between munich city and munich airport, but the rapporteur would like to reiterate the point that the changes we are making in this report are about correcting the text, not updating it. the point of this recast is to bring everything into order ahead of the real revision of the guidelines during early 2011. it is important to ensure the clarity of the guidelines in preparation for the more substantial changes that will take place next year. we are all eagerly awaiting the real revision of the guidelines and the chance to further develop the ten-t network but, in order to get to that stage, we need to get the current legislation right, and that is what we have done here.
deputising for the rapporteur. − madam president, i would just like to thank the colleagues who have contributed to the debate today and reiterate my point that we are looking at correcting mistakes made in the past and not at making any substantive changes. that will come later on with the real revision of the guidelines and, of course, we are looking forward to that debate. for this reason, the rapporteur, mr simpson, and myself, as coordinator for my group, will be instructing my own political group to vote against the amendment put forward by the greens. we hope that the other political groups will be able to support us on this.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr van dalen, for his excellent report. it emphasizes a number of essential elements, starting with the importance of shipping for our economy. he also points out that we must compete in a global context, that we must therefore take account of the competitiveness of our maritime sector, but at the same time we must also strive for more sustainable transport and that we must therefore, in other words, counter the risk of de-flagging. at the same time, we must also play on a level playing field. that is why it is important that the state aid rules announced so long ago are brought to bear on our ports. we'll certainly come back to that. three main points of concern. firstly, employment and working conditions. the profession must be made attractive again to young people. that is a very difficult matter today. we need to make more efforts to disseminate information on career opportunities, whether in the countryside or outside. those are things that need to be developed. the working conditions must also be improved by ratifying the legislation of the international labour organisation and also by monitoring it at a global level, not just within europe, of course. the second important point - and this has been mentioned many times - is the impact of shipping on the environment. it is a sustainable means of transport in itself, but there is still much room for improvement. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area, including the establishment of a european maritime safety agency. finally, safety is of course essential. the only way to create an attractive profession is to have it in a safe environment. we must implement the third maritime security package and continue our support for the atlanta mission off the coast of somalia.
mr president, mr president-in-office of the council, mr commissioner! this ban on flights brings us back to the broad debate on safety which we have already had here in parliament on several occasions, and in which we have indicated that we, this parliament - and not at national level, please - and perhaps provide aid packages which are also compatible throughout europe and do not distort the market again, as we have often known in the past. i am not saying that we should not do this. the passengers - president. - ladies and gentlemen, i think there are three important elements in this debate. firstly, the reception and repatriation of stranded travellers, that must be the absolute top priority for all authorities at all levels. in this connection, we can see that the european regulation on passenger rights has ensured a minimum of comfort and reception for a large number of them. in practice, however, as you know, there have been a number of problems: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's air transport policy is not disrupted. i therefore also call for a european road map to be drawn up, in cooperation with airlines, airports and all other parties concerned, to see how we can help in situations like this. secondly, i would also like to call for the creation of a kind of task force at the level of the commission and the member states to organise this repatriation as well as possible. i know that airlines have to do that, but it may be that people stranded at a distance will have to wait a long time even when airspace is opened. that should be a focal point. a second important element, chapter two, is the procedure for obtaining a flight ban. what has happened is another plea for more cooperation, more coordination at european level and - as has already been mentioned - the european single sky will help in the future. it is true that today the european union has no direct decision-making power over the airspace of the member states, including eurocontrol, so it is very difficult to take good and coordinated decisions there. but it is also true that until last night we were actually using a very conservative mathematical model at european level. this model is based on the worst-case scenario, where a volcanic ash cloud is interpreted as a no-fly zone. you know that in the united states a different model is used, whereby a no-fly zone is applied only above the volcano itself and the operational risks are in the hands of the airlines. that's a different model. the three-zone intermediate model agreed is a good model. let's see how we can actually combine safety and efficiency there. thirdly, and lastly, the economic impact of the measures. it is a good idea to list the different possibilities, but we need a european approach. and finally, we should not give the illusion that we can reimburse the damage to everyone. i just can't.
