president, commissioner, colleagues, good morning to you. of course, this is a matter of exceptional circumstances. air traffic is more disrupted than it was on 9/11, but it is clear that we were not prepared for this emergency. despite the best efforts of the staff of tour operators and airlines, many passengers have been left to fend for themselves. we must clearly learn from this and take appropriate action. firstly, mr president, commissioner, we must have an emergency plan, a coordinated european plan. the plan must, of course, focus on safety, information and assistance for stranded travellers, so that those affected have at least a point of contact and a guarantee of shelter. we must take these events to the extreme to improve the lot of the affected travellers in the future. in addition, one thing has become clear in recent days, namely that we in europe need to invest much more in a crossborder high-speed rail network, an environmentally friendly alternative to the clearly fragile air transport. let us work towards an attractive investment project on the railway that is good for the citizen, good for the environment and good for employment in the context of eu 2020.
mr president, chaos, paralysis, cacophony, catastrophe, disaster - there is no shortage of words in the press to describe the closure of european airspace and its consequences. i will not dwell on what has already been said, particularly on the extent of the colossal financial impact - abysmal, one might be tempted to say - of this crisis in direct or indirect terms. while i welcome the possibility of considering granting exceptional public aid to the aviation sector, which has already been badly hit since 11 september 2001, i am nonetheless puzzled by the european management of events. firstly, given that 750,000 european passengers have been affected, many of whom are still stranded in the four corners of the world, and given that the economic losses are exponential with the passage of time, how can it be that it took not one, two, three or four, but five days for the european transport ministers to meet by teleconference to coordinate their actions and decide on the creation of differentiated traffic zones? secondly, no one disputes the need to apply the precautionary principle as a matter of priority - and i stress this word - but today, as traffic is being gradually allowed back into safe areas with unchanged weather and as the volcano is still active, we are entitled to ask ourselves what additional safeguards for passengers we could not have provided earlier, more quickly. third, while a scalable action model has been developed the evolution, according to the weather, and the activity of the volcano can change from hour to hour , who will continue to carry out the updated assessment of the corridors' safety? should these test flights be carried out by civil aviation and airlines? finally, if this continues, worsens or repeats itself - which is likely - the management model put in place will have to provide for greater coordination of member states and special operational procedures based on real data, better coordination of other means of transport in exceptional circumstances but also taking into account the necessary coordination of repatriation assistance for tens of thousands of stranded passengers who also have the right to information and assistance. so far, however, only isolated national initiatives have been taken.
mr president, this debate has given rise to many speeches by transport experts. it is true that we have focused a great deal on the economic problems that this particular and unpredictable situation creates. there are also human aspects, as has been said, and i am more of a consumer citizen advocate, for hundreds of thousands of citizens who are now stranded somewhere in the world and cannot be repatriated. i am thinking more of them, especially those who can no longer afford to stay, are stuck and have no other choice. in view of the empty skies and the airports full of people in distress, we may have to think about revising the transport guidelines, particularly those for air transport. we'll probably have to go over the package-trip thing again. could it not be that - and this has not yet been said - insurance should be taken out to ensure that in cases of force majeure these people are covered, in particular to avoid leaving them in distress?

- i'm the reporter. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i was wondering what discharge is. discharge is a parliamentary and public procedure, a public critical examination of financial management. in my case, i did that exercise for the european parliament about the year 2008. this will give us as members of parliament, but also the citizens, a better understanding, a better understanding of the particular way parliament is organised, its management structure and its working methods. because, ladies and gentlemen, citizens have a right to know what is happening to their tax money. it's a lot of money. the budget for 2008 is 1.4 billion euro. the budget for 2011 is likely to be eur 1.7 billion. that's an awful lot of money. the procedure is important and the work of the committee on budgetary control is important, because a critical committee on budgetary control makes progress. that's been proven in the past. a critical attitude on the part of the committee on budgetary control has led, for example, to the introduction of a statute for members of parliament, to the introduction of a statute for assistants, to our critical examination of the purchase of buildings here in strasbourg, to the introduction of an emas procedure which has reduced the environmental impact of our work. positive news, ladies and gentlemen, positive news! we have managed to reduce electricity consumption by 25% in three years through our critical attitude. we have managed to use 100% green electricity. we've managed to reduce co2 emissions by 17%. we've managed to reduce our waste streams by 50%, compost or reuse. i have also introduced a new concept in my report, ladies and gentlemen. the concept of 'damage to the reputation of parliament' means that even small effects of financial resources can cause enormous damage to the reputation of this parliament. we'll have to keep an eye out for that. it is very good that a risk manager was appointed to the administration on 24 february. i invite that person to come to the committees responsible and debate with us how we can reduce the risks of malpractice in this parliament. a critical approach is essential, i said. that is why i am calling for transparency, for openness, for the establishment of a system of checks and balances, for responsibility and accountability. i propose, mr president, that you be discharged, madam president, ladies and gentlemen, mr president-in-office of the council, commissioner, the debate on pnr data is very similar to the discussion we have just had on swift. fundamentally, it is about finding a healthy and acceptable balance between safety and the protection of privacy. both are important, of course, and must be carefully weighed. the difficulties in european aviation in recent days have once again demonstrated the essential role that the transport of people and goods plays in the way we are organised as a society today. pretty much everybody gets on a plane sooner or later. it is also impossible that we should be constantly transmitting and updating dozens of data, often quite unconsciously, without having in place the most robust safeguards against abuse, especially since, for example, the us authorities have long been using very different sources of information to assess whether or not a person is a suspect, from the visa application to the check-in on the plane. i myself was able to observe a few weeks ago at the pnr centre in washington that a whole team is working around the clock to reduce an initial rough list of some 5,000 people a day to a small list of a handful of people who are denied access to the territory. it appears that the only way to challenge that entry ban is by administrative action. it is clear that this flow of data must be kept within certain limits, that minimum conditions must be laid down as stated in the resolution, such as restricting its use for the purpose of detecting terrorism and international crime. i agree with colleagues who say that we must define this well, that all this must of course be in line with european data protection standards and that this also applies to transfers to other third countries, where this is the case. i think we also need to clarify a little more what is called sensitive pnr data, which i think is open to interpretation. i therefore support the proposed postponement, so that a new negotiating mandate is put forward as soon as possible, taking account of our questions. i take note of the constructive attitude of the council and the commission and, like my colleagues, i expect more clarity by the summer months.

in writing. all initiatives to reduce global warming are welcome. as was the case yesterday in the european parliament, where 1 500 elected representatives from major european cities pledged to reduce their greenhouse gas emissions by more than 20% by 2020. the adoption today of the le foll report on the adaptation of european agriculture to climate change is part of this same logic. it should be noted that the agricultural sector is responsible for almost 10% of co2 emissions. agriculture has much to gain from anticipating the adverse effects of climate change: flooded areas, reduced arable land, deforestation, uneven yields. the sustainable aspect of agriculture must therefore be promoted. the promotion of the wise use of fertilizers and pesticides, together with diversification of production and livestock sectors, will ensure farmers greater autonomy and better financial support. clearly, european agriculture has a major role to play in the fight against climate change. there are ways: fixing carbon sinks, supplying renewable energy, new irrigation techniques. the only thing left to do is to translate them into concrete policies and integrate them into the reformed cap in 2013.

on behalf of the alde group. mr president, let me first say that i hope, as i believe all my colleagues do, and as the president of the commission, that the mechanism we have put in place will work. the fact that i have had my doubts and been critical of the bilateral lending mechanism from the beginning does not mean that i do not hope that the system will work and stop speculation against the euro. because, ladies and gentlemen, little by little, this is speculation against the euro and an attack on the euro, and not just an attack on greece or linked to the situation of public finances in greece. so it's much more serious and broader. i hope that this mechanism, once it has been formalised on 7 may, will be able to take full effect, for the simple reason that we have no other instrument. we have no other instrument! this mechanism must therefore work, and it must be supported! but we must also - and this is my second point - understand that in the near future it will not be possible to use it every time. a structural mechanism will need to be in place as soon as possible. maybe not for the next few months, but certainly for the next few years, because we're going to have more of these situations. and if we want to have a structural mechanism for the future, we must take one thing into account: we must learn from what has happened in the last five months. it took us five months to put a mechanism in place: three months to decide on its principle, then two months to decide on its conditions. why would you do that? because it's an intergovernmental system! i think, once again, that the first lesson to be learned for the future is that we must follow the commission in its community approach. the commission had proposed a european loan, which could have been decided immediately in december or january and which could already have had its effect today and put an end to this speculation against the euro. i hope that on 7 may the first decision, the first lesson to be drawn from what has happened in the last five months, will be to say that - hoping that this will work - from now on the commission will be asked to propose a community loan which can immediately stop speculation against the euro. the whole credibility and liquidity of the european union depends on such a proposal, which is not the case in an intergovernmental system where 16 countries have to say 'yes', 16 parliaments, perhaps, have to say 'yes', etc. and i also hope - but mr rehn has already begun to make proposals - that the second lesson we learn from all this is that we must introduce a number of structural reforms, that is to say a preventive chapter in the stability pact - what mr rehn has proposed - a european monetary fund, a structural mechanism, which can be used immediately and, thirdly, a 2020 strategy which is much more solid than the strategy on paper today. then reform also in relation to rating agencies, even if rating agencies are like weather forecasts: either they are too flexible and you want them to be a little bit harder; when they're too hard, you want them a little bit more flexible. but an initiative at european level is certainly a good idea that needs to be considered. finally, mr president, the spanish presidency calls for financial supervision to be approved very quickly. i am sorry, mr secretary of state, but this is not our fault, it is the council's! it is he who has changed the commission's proposals, is it not? there were proposals from the commission which i even criticised, but which went much further than those from the council. we are the ones who are doing the commission's work again at the moment and i have a good proposal to make to you. if you want financial supervision and proposals to be implemented within a month, then accept immediately, together with the council and ecofin, the amendments which parliament will be proposing in the coming days. it will be done very quickly and financial supervision will be applied. i hope you can tell your ecofin colleagues about this, because in their proposal they have done nothing but find a way of escaping the financial supervision that was being set up by the commission.
i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in the negotiations, but that it should be involved in the negotiations. and another one i only got to watch on tv as a kid. ladies and gentlemen, i share the concern of the europeans about what is happening. the savers, the pensioners, they're all wondering: where is this going? and it's a legitimate question and a legitimate concern. a package of 110 billion is a huge package. first we're talking about 35 billion, 60 billion and now 110 billion. that is a huge amount and the austerity package in greece is also huge; we must not forget, however, that greece has lived too long on the dole with a retirement age of 53. who wouldn't want that? and the question is, does greece come out of this? - yes or no? we now see strikes, riots, riots, and so on. so the greek problem, mr president, is a european problem, our problem. the problem in athens - mr cohn-bendit - affects dutch, flemish, german, all of us, and the risk of contagion has remained. i believe that greece should have been excluded from the euro area when the budgetary irregularities were established. we should have made a cut-off point, but we didn't, and now we have to move on and hope that it works. we also need to rewrite the rules of the stability and growth pact. it does not bring stability and, for the time being, no economic growth. i think that there should be stronger supervision, that the european commission should be more courageous, that there should be more supervision of compliance with the rules. that hasn't happened in years. but i think there should also be an exit procedure for a country which can no longer sustain itself in the euro zone. we do have an exit procedure from the european union, but not from the eurozone. i therefore believe that there should be such a possibility, so that a country can introduce its own currency, devalue it and thus run out of money. why do we have an exit procedure in the european union as such, through the lisbon treaty, and not in the eurozone? mr. rehn said to me the last time: "a country leaving the eurozone is in conflict with ever closer union", but greece is now showing exactly where ever closer union is at its limits. at some point we have a weak euro and low growth. ladies and gentlemen, we are becoming prisoners of the theory of the ever closer union and we are holding the taxpayer in europe hostage and that taxpayer is becoming more and more uneasy. don't you forget that.

madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, with this excellent report by mrs lulling, the european parliament is striving to reduce even more the differences in treatment between men and women in the field of work, and i welcome this approach. it is a further step on a long road ahead. it seems to me essential to stress the importance of social protection for the caring spouses or recognised life partners of selfemployed workers. it should be borne in mind that in many european countries, caring spouses still do not have a status of their own, their work is not recognised and they do not benefit from independent social security. it is 2010 and women in some member states are still suffering from the non-recognition of their rights and are entirely dependent on the insurance of their spouse. in these times of economic crisis, we cannot leave these caring spouses dependent on a system that could plunge them into poverty overnight, in the event of divorce or separation for example. we cannot therefore accept that it is possible for member states to maintain national provisions restricting access to specific social protection schemes or to a certain level of funding. the caring spouse must be covered for pension, family allowances, health care, incapacity for work and maternity. finally, as negotiations currently stand, it is for the member states to decide whether this social protection should be implemented on a compulsory or voluntary basis. i therefore strongly urge all member states to do their utmost to make this protection compulsory. we must all fight against precariousness, the non-recognition of rights, especially in times of economic crisis.

on behalf of the alde group. madam president, we are therefore waiting for tomorrow for this directive on organ donation and transplantation, which is essential, legislation that has been awaited for years by some 60 000 patients in europe waiting for a heart, a lung, a liver, a kidney. twelve of them die every day because they wait too long. i would like to stress, as the previous speakers have done, the excellent result we have achieved, which would not have been possible without the collaboration between our rapporteurs, mr perello and mr mikolášik, the fictitious rapporteurs of which i am, and the spanish presidency. i take this opportunity to pay tribute to our doctor matesanz, who was our excellent guide during our visit to madrid. let's not miss the point here. reducing the shortage of organs in europe is the top priority and, of course, at the same time, raising awareness among europeans about organ donation, because the disparities are telling, they are even glaring 34 dead donors per million inhabitants in spain, 27 in my country, belgium, and only one donor in romania , therefore, educating on this issue of donation, allowing potential donors to access information, also breaking down administrative barriers it is essential to establish in the 27 member states a national authority in charge of monitoring the quality and safety of organs, of the collection from the donor to the transplant, and also to take inspiration from this the best examples were the organisation of the alert system in spain, not to mention the ongoing progress, where there is a chain of deaths, up to the pre-transplantation, to name one. our compromise directive, our famous amendment no 106 which sums up all our work, conveys the right message to patients, that of hope, by allowing - and this was sometimes after very thorough discussions - the use of a non-optimal organ in cases of extreme urgency, especially by encouraging the donation of a living person when this is possible, of course. i am talking mainly about kidney or liver donation, which is a necessary and growing donation, which is encouraged by practitioners and specialists and which still represents only 5% of the average donation at the moment. doing more and cooperating better is the raison d'être of the action plan that mr perello has been talking about. one example is my proposal to register online in a national or european donor register, with the idea of putting a mark on the donor's identity card or driver's licence to identify him as such and thus to go faster if necessary. i just want to summarize for a second that we did a great job and we preferred the ethics of hope to the ethics of prohibition.
about two years ago, the european parliament overwhelmingly adopted the adamou report, which for the first time discussed policy action at eu level in the field of organ donation and transplantation. as one of the shadow reporters, i have also had the pleasure of working on that. today i am very pleased to note that the rapporteur, mr mikolášik, and his shadow rapporteurs have done a splendid job and have succeeded, even in this short time, in reaching agreement with the council at first reading. the main points of the report of two years ago have been largely taken up and developed further. i therefore hope that this directive and the action plan will lead to an increase in the number of organ donors in the european union, so that patients will no longer have to be put on waiting lists.
for the greens/efa group madam president, commissioner, we are talking about the legal basis. this is very important of course, but behind this legal basis, i would like to highlight the fact that housing, and especially social housing, is obviously not a market like any other or a business activity like any other. social housing is much more than housing for low-income or needy people. there are the issues of social mix, which we have discussed, but there is also the integration of these dwellings and the people who live in them into their neighbourhoods, into their towns, sometimes in the town centres, sometimes in the outskirts. there is a lot of work being done with the tenants, a work of integration, a work of building solidarity, including between the different generations living in these neighbourhoods and in these housing and so, as such, i really think that it must be a different matter. we will also be working on services of general interest during this parliamentary term. it seems to me that it sends a rather bad signal to come here and highlight problems with subsidies or limitations on access to social housing. and as for the income ceiling, which has been mentioned by other colleagues, and which is set at 33 000 euros, i think it poses a problem: there is a very different range of families, single-parent families, elderly people, step-parents, other broken families. how do we calculate their income? all this leads me to ask, as others have done, for more flexibility and subsidiarity if we want real social projects in these neighbourhoods.

800,000 litres of crude oil have been pouring into the gulf of mexico every day for nearly a month, and the environmental and economic damage is going to be catastrophic. i see a huge gap between the colossal sums spent on deeper and deeper mining and the totally inadequate resources devoted to downstream prevention and risk anticipation. so i too, like my colleagues, ask for several things. i believe that first of all there should be an audit of all oil platforms to check their state of obsolescence, their level of safety and their compliance with standards, and that there should also, of course, be an inspection of the platforms, even if this is the responsibility of the member states, and that the commission should be in charge of the inspection itself. i also believe that the most fragile ecosystems must be protected once and for all. in this respect, i think that, in addition to the initiative you have taken, which i welcome, to try to see how we could cope with such circumstances if such an accident were to occur on our coasts, europe must take initiatives to protect the most fragile ecosystems once and for all. i'm thinking of alaska, for example. we need an international convention on the same model as that which exists for the protection of antarctic resources and europe could be at the forefront of this kind of work and convention. finally, if it were still necessary, this is a very good reason to invest more quickly and more in renewable energy. the volcano - i won't mention its name, because it's unpronounceable - has given us a signal on the question of air transport; vice-president of the commission. − madam president, i would like to thank first the rapporteur, because mr marinescu and his shadow rapporteurs have done very positive and important work. now we are at the end of the second reading, and the commission would like to reaffirm once again – and in the strongest possible terms – the political and industrial importance of the subject on which parliament has to decide. notwithstanding the adverse economic cycle we are in, there are some clear lessons to be learned: how to revitalise the rail freight sector, because rail freight transport is primarily international, and an indisputable factor in its success is the availability of good-quality rail infrastructure at the european level. that is what the customers ask for. good-quality rail infrastructure across borders is an essential condition for the competitiveness of many rail freight market segments, and it is primordial if rail wants to compete with other modes of transport. this also explains why the commission proposed, at the end of 2008, the development of a european rail network for competitive freight based on freight corridors. these corridors link the main industrial regions of europe, where the most substantive flow of goods originates and terminates along the main international axes. the legislative proposal to which your attention is drawn today contains decisive elements which favour this development of high-quality rail infrastructure at international level. these include cooperation between rail infrastructure managers, reliability of infrastructure capacities allocated to the corridors, good coordination between rail infrastructure management and goods terminal management, and non-discriminatory access to these corridors for all operators. the council’s position at first reading maintains the basic principle put forward in the commission’s original proposal but waters down some of the elements. the tran committee has reintroduced, at second reading, important amendments which strengthen the regulation, and they bring back the original commission proposal. the commission considers that the compromise obtained during the negotiation is balanced and that the general objectives of the commission’s proposals are duly taken into account. we are very grateful to parliament in that it has succeeded in reinforcing the role of the one-stop shop, which had been reduced to a mere mail box in the common position of the council. i know, rapporteur, that some people want to go back to the common position. i invite you and parliament to resist these attempts, because they would weaken the compromise that you have been able to reach. for the railway undertakings and for other applicants which wish to have access to the corridor infrastructure, the one-stop shop is a considerable simplification of the present, very burdensome procedure, because the one-stop shop will be able to take decisions itself instead of referring matters to the individual infrastructure managers, which cover only national sections of each corridor. but, of course, the one-stop shop is limited to catalogue or reserve capacity: that is, the capacity which the infrastructure managers themselves have already decided to set aside for freight trains. corridor managers have also several options as to the way in which the one-stop shop should be established. let me emphasise that the one-stop shop is a joint body set up or designated by the management board of each corridor: its function is that of a coordination tool. it may be a technical body within the corridor management structure, or one of the infrastructure managers concerned, or the bodies already established by rail net europe. so it is not a supranational body, but it is a tool which speeds up and simplifies, in a very transparent and non-discriminatory manner, the path allocation process. the infrastructure managers have the responsibility to predefine capacity. the one-stop shop just sells paths to applicants on the basis of decisions already taken by infrastructure managers on the predefined infrastructure capacity. some have gone as far as saying that, with this regulation, the commission wants to give priority to freight trains over passenger trains, thus threatening the efficient provision of rail passenger services, in particular at regional and local level. now let me be very frank: this is improper, it is not the right reading of the proposal, and it does not correspond at all to the texts that everybody can read in article 14, in article 15. priority rules for trains are not something the commission will ever want to decide from brussels. freight traffic is systemically discriminated against today because passenger traffic always has priority, even in cases of disturbance. what the text says is that the infrastructure managers themselves must take the responsibility to define transparent, consistent, non-discriminatory rules for both types of traffic so that freight trains which are on time do not have to be systematically delayed in case of disturbance. this is, i believe, a very good compromise, and if you parliamentarians adopt it and if it is also then correctly implemented, the commission is convinced that it will contribute to the revitalisation of rail freight and will help the european economy to start again after the present crisis.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. it is a pity that what has been said and clarified here by the commission does not have the same resonance in the press as what certain companies are saying about this regulation. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is, in effect, driving societies into an exercise they do not like to do, as the past has shown. it's the practice of simply working together between countries. we have just been talking about liberalization: we are signing a liberalization agreement with the countries, and 21 countries manage to do nothing in rail transport. we are now talking about freight transport. normally, you wouldn't have to make such a regulation. we want to help them become more efficient. study after study says that we need to move more traffic from the road to the rail, and every time we have an initiative to think european in rail transport, there is always some society or some country that says: no, i'm better off and you should accept my rules. i would like to thank the rapporteur and also the shadow rapporteurs for this work, which they have done together with the commission and the council. we are in favour of this cooperation. we stress that passenger transport remains a priority as defined in 2001. we want transparent rules in advance so that in emergency situations it does not matter what is decided. all this is intended to give the passenger transport beneficiary and the infrastructure managers in the country themselves the confidence to participate. no, you don't want this! why would you do that? president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would also like to thank the rapporteur and the shadow rapporteurs for their good work. i think the result is obvious and important. the freight transport sector has lost market share to road transport almost systematically over the last 20 years and has also been hit hard by the economic crisis. it is therefore necessary to take measures to re-energize rail transport. the present regulation is, of course, only one means of making rail freight more competitive. we will also have to take a whole series of other measures, for example on technical interoperability and on the financing of infrastructure. we will also have to optimise the functioning of the market, promote intermodal transport, etc. but it is clear that the development of international freight corridors along which freight trains can travel smoothly across europe is an important step in the right direction. the one-stop shop system, which some colleagues are opposed to, is just one way of making the use of the corridors easier, simpler and more transparent for potential customers. it is not a duplication of work compared to what national infrastructure managers already do today. on the contrary, it is complementary and improves coordination. i also agree with the honourable members who have stressed that this regulation will not be to the detriment of passenger trains. however, it is necessary to establish clear priority criteria in order to ensure that freight and passenger transport can be used in the most optimal way.

i support the commissioner. a self-employed person is someone who manages his own working hours and that should remain the case. there is a difference between self-employed and employed, and that is also correct. it has nothing to do with road safety, because the driving and rest periods are identical for both the selfemployed and the employed. they are checked with the tachograph and that is what the tachograph is for, not anything else. the employee and the selfemployed are not checked for what is happening outside in the field of road safety. the second point, which i find very difficult to accept in the proposals of those who say that the selfemployed should be included in this: how the hell are you gonna check that? how do you walk into a self-employed person's house and say, "when, where and how much did you work?" we are thus arriving at a kind of control system which is unattainable and which is then said afterwards that it cannot be done. we should not make such legislation, we should not include the selfemployed in the directive on employees.
i would like to say that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the question of the right of selfemployment. it is useful to recall that since march last year all selfemployed drivers have been covered. that is the situation today, and that is the starting point which the rapporteur should have taken into account. it is therefore not a matter of precedent, as the commissioner has claimed. it should have been possible to deal with this in a more pragmatic way, in particular by including self-employed people in the scope, but at the same time taking into account the specific nature of self-employed drivers and keeping bureaucratic burdens to an absolute minimum. i had - and you know this, madam rapporteur for the committee on transport - drawn up a number of proposals, such as the digital tachograph, which would not only measure driving and rest times, but also loading and unloading, supplemented by a kind of uncontrolled fixed-term work for a number of other tasks, including administrative work, but also cleaning and suchlike. you know very well that, for example, customer contacts are out of control anyway and outside working hours. i therefore regret that neither the commission nor the rapporteur has taken this into account in any way. i do not think your proposal to give member states the option of including selfemployed people in the scope of application or not is a good one. the aim should be to establish common rules of the game at european level which are equivalent, so that everyone is covered by the same rules.

member of the commission. − mr president, let me first of all thank ms pack for presenting this oral question on behalf of the committee on culture and education. i am fully aware of the political importance of this issue. i would recall that the commission has now published the results of the independent study on sports agents. the study provides a comprehensive analysis of the issue at stake. we have learned, for instance, that around 6 000 agents are active across the member states in all disciplines. however, 95% of them are active in only four sports: athletics, basketball, football and rugby. and within this group, football clearly predominates. the study also tells us that only a limited number of member states regulate agents through a specific law. most countries simply rely on general provisions of either labour law or contract law. some international sports federations have also adopted regulations on agents, notably in the disciplines that i just mentioned: athletics, basketball, football and rugby. although the existing public and private regulations may conflict with each other and create legal uncertainty, the findings of the study show that few obstacles exist to the free provision of agents’ activities across the eu. however, the study highlights a number of dubious practices related to agents’ activities: for instance the payment of secret commissions, the exploitation of young players, etc. we must address such problems to preserve the integrity and fairness of sports competitions. the sports movement has been trying to tackle these problems for some time. this is why a number of governing bodies have adopted measures to regulate the activities of agents. the rules put in place by sports bodies may have an advantage: sporting sanctions are sometimes the best way to deter unscrupulous actors in the market, be they agents, clubs or players. but sporting authorities do not have the power to investigate and punish acts which all member states consider to be crimes: corruption, money laundering, tax evasion and illegal immigration. the sports movement is looking for assistance from the european institutions to tackle the challenges posed by agents. the commission has an important role to play. we can help to develop the common principles and standards that might serve as a minimum set of rules for countries and sports federations across europe. we are now assessing which type of action at eu level would be most appropriate. we have several options in mind but, to finally decide what action we should take, we will certainly need further deliberations and consultations.
i would like to refer first of all to the world cup in south africa, which shows every day how beautiful sport can be, how beautiful sport is for a country, a continent and, above all, for society. it is therefore all the more important that we either protect sport from the criminal activities to which mrs pack has already referred, or that we try to tackle these activities with force. the study you have just referred to, the results of which are now known, literally says the following: "sport is increasingly being influenced by organized crime". i'm sorry, but it is. it is about human trafficking, including trafficking of young children, and also about all kinds of financial malpractices. it is clear that the sports federations themselves do not have the power and the competence to deal effectively with these abuses. that is why a european initiative is necessary. we already have various legislative instruments in the treaty, in directives, to take action. i refer to a few proposals for action. firstly, we could work with strict criteria that apply to all sports brokers throughout the union. the best way to achieve this is through licensing systems for clubs and through a mandatory register for players' agents. the sports federations can impose this, but we as the european union can certainly play a driving role. the european union can, for example, coordinate the various national control bodies. a second measure we could take is absolute transparency of the money flows that circulate in the world of the players' brokers, because that is the greatest source of all evil today. we have no visibility into these dark money flows, and that means they can continue to use elite sports for money laundering on the one hand, for bribery on the other. a third proposal - and mrs pack has already spoken about it - concerns a ban on fees for players' agents who want to make money from the transfer of underage players. we should definitely work on that. we have enough instruments to put pressure on the professional sports federations to push this through and to take initiatives ourselves. this parliament already called on the commission in 2007 by a very large majority to take the initiative in combating the waste in the world of sports betting. the study makes a number of concrete suggestions. they're good, but they're clearly not enough. we are pleased to hear that you are indeed planning to switch to higher gear. the industry, the european association of player brokers, is also calling for this. we have had a lot of contact with them and they themselves are insisting that the european union should take action to filter out the rotten apples. i suggest that we answer their question in the interests of sport and also in the interests of the extremely important social role of sport.

in writing. − road transport legislation on safety and driving times should be the same for all possible stakeholders, whether drivers are employed or self-employed. the basic directive 2002/15/ec (which entered into force in march 2005 for drivers employed as self-employed persons) also provides that the rules apply to self-employed persons from march 2009. this backsliding is not good governance and is capitulation to the market. tired drivers are dangerous drivers, whether self-employed or not. people need to be protected from overtime. it puts your own safety and that of others at risk. self-employed people may be under economic pressure more than hired drivers. if we exclude the selfemployed, we are creating unfair competition with the transport companies with their drivers who are obliged to respect the driving and resting times, and this cannot be the intention. the current directive proves that if selfemployed people are not required to work the same hours, drivers in that selfemployed status are forced to work the same hours just to get around them. that is how you create the illusion of independence, and i want to fight that too. we need to have a uniform law that sets the same rules for everyone. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the directive is implemented.

in writing. − the revision of food labelling legislation is a step in the right direction, despite some minor points. the regulation will ensure that consumers are better informed about food in the future. i regret that the amendment on the traffic light system did not pass the industrial lobby's test. an indication in green, orange or red would give consumers a simple and clear picture of the (healthy) content of the products. the new rules are strongly opposed by the eu, which has proposed that food producers must now disclose the amount of energy, salt, sugar, fat and saturated fat in their products. the origin of meat, fish and milk products must be clearly indicated on the packaging. consumers can now find out for themselves where the animals were born, bred and slaughtered. they can choose local and regional products and avoid unnecessary food kilometres. milk that has been stored for more than 7 days will no longer be labelled as fresh milk. trans fats and flavor enhancers are being addressed. if a product is sweetened, this must be indicated on the packaging. all these decisions are progress for the consumer who will now be well informed and able to make an informed choice.

on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, the european union is a source of great hope in the countries around us, because it continues to embody human rights. but too often this hope is disappointed because little concrete results come to back up our generous speeches. now, we know that human rights are not won by force, and that it is only over time, through coherent policies based on civil societies and emerging democratic forces, that we can change human rights in the world, especially when political regimes are corrupt or dictatorial. europe has a wonderful instrument, namely the election observation instrument within the framework of the ihop. but what we observers of the elections see is that in too many cases the elections are declared to be largely flawed or fraudulent, without necessarily leading to a coordinated and audible response from the council or european politicians. and finally, when politicians commit fraud, they do it after the election period and they repress the population quite harshly. it is a paradox that in time we end up legitimising, out of weariness, regimes which are corrupt, which is actually a perverse effect of our instruments. so, mrs ashton, do you intend - and we support this instrument, i repeat - to have a response strategy that is a little bit more audible when we actually face these cases. i did not want to give any examples, but there are, unfortunately, many.
member of the commission. − mr president, i would like first to thank parliament for the opportunity to present the outcome of the negotiations with the united states on the second stage air services agreement. earlier this year, on 25 march, the european commission initialled the second stage agreement – an agreement of which parliament has been very supportive. parliament played a hugely constructive role throughout these negotiations, for which the commission is extremely grateful. let me go through the key elements of the second stage agreement. this agreement builds on the first stage agreement, in effect since 30 march 2008, by creating the prospects for additional investment and market access opportunities, as well as further strengthening the framework of cooperation on regulatory issues such as the environment, social protection, competition and security. on the environment, the draft second stage agreement establishes a robust framework for tackling the local and global environmental challenges affecting europe and the us through the pursuit of joint projects addressing practical solutions such as cleaner in-flight and air traffic management technologies and cooperation in international forums such as the international civil aviation organisation. both sides also formally recognise the importance of avoiding duplication and enhancing the compatibility of their respective market-based measures. i would mention here in particular the emission trading scheme. europe is also set to benefit from progress made in the area of security, where the agreement seeks to reduce the security burden on airports through the promotion of assessment activities and timely consultation on future requirements. in the area of competition, the agreement enhances cooperation between the responsible authorities as well as the transparency and predictability of regulatory decisions on both sides of the atlantic. for the first time in such an aviation agreement, the need to balance market access opportunities with strong social protection has been recognised through a commitment to implementing the provisions of the agreement in a way which does not undermine labour rights. on the commercial front, europe has gained further rights, including immediate access to the ‘fly america’ programme (with the exception of defence), which up to now restricted us government-funded air transport to us carriers only. but perhaps the most important element of the draft agreement is the commitment to move forward. as part of the agreement, europe and the united states have committed to the goal of removing the remaining market barriers that face the industry, including those that limit airlines’ access to global capital. progress towards this goal will be reviewed annually and will involve working together through the joint committee. together with a high-level review if progress is too slow, we have a targeted approach for advancing in this area. both sides have an incentive to make progress in this area through the guarantee of additional rights to operate passenger seventh freedom rights and develop stakes in third-country carriers. to summarise, the agreement advances cooperation across a wide range of regulatory matters, including areas such as security, consumer protection, the role of the joint committee and the environment. it delivers immediate and future additional commercial opportunities, establishes a roadmap for change in the vital area of investment reform and, importantly, secures the benefits of the existing first stage agreement that would have been lost if we had not reached this second stage agreement. some of them would have been lost. a study commissioned to examine the benefit of the first and second stage agreements has estimated that it could be worth up to eur 12 billion in economic benefits and up to 80 000 new jobs – both very valuable in the current economic climate. for these reasons, i hope that parliament will support this important agreement.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already done so. the air transport agreement with the united states is of course very important. that is why parliament and our committee have been very active in the discussions and visits. 60% of all air traffic in the world is between america and europe. that's the importance of it. we also hope that both partners will then see the need for a genuine bilateral agreement and will be encouraged in the future to improve what is now or has been. the european parliament has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we had some open points. one was the equity stake. we were hoping there would be some progress. but there's been very little progress, quite frankly, other than the fact that people are saying they want to talk about it. secondly, we wanted to see progress in the mutual interest of the technical inspection bodies. we didn't see her like that. as far as market access is concerned, we dare not speak of cabotage because it seems to be almost a taboo word in the united states. but i 'd look at it this way: the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. that's not quite the case. i am therefore of the opinion that, apart from one point which should not be underestimated, namely the social aspect, not much progress has been made in concrete terms, but there is a great deal of will to make progress in the future. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. firstly, such agreements require a legal basis. we must also respect data protection legislation, particularly when it comes to the exchange of data and information, and under no circumstances should parliament be presented with a fait accompli. secondly, we should emphasize the bilateral nature of these agreements, i.e. not unilateral agreements but genuine bilateral agreements. thirdly, it is also important that we set a date for the resumption of negotiations. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, when we agreed to the first phase of the aviation agreement in 2007, it was for two important reasons. firstly, the concept of the european airline was accepted, which meant that from the entry into force of the agreement any european airline could fly from any point in europe to any point in the united states. this was in itself a major innovation and was not the case for all our companies before. secondly, there has been the creation of a joint committee, which gives us a structure for regular consultation with a view to harmonizing the regulatory framework, which is of course necessary in order to achieve a truly unified transatlantic aviation market in time. but the agreement remained unbalanced because american companies have more access to the european internal market than vice versa through historical concessions, and that access to each other's capital structure is also to the disadvantage of our companies in the european union. that is why the deadline clause, to reach a more comprehensive agreement before the end of this year, was so important in keeping the pressure on the boiler and forcing the americans to make concessions. the result is a new step in the right direction, but it largely preserves the imbalances that already existed, and in particular there has been insufficient progress in terms of market access for european companies in the united states and potential european ownership and control of american companies. so despite some positive elements, which my colleague, mathieu grosch, has also mentioned in the field of the environment, for example, in the social field, we will only be able to approve this agreement if there is more than a commitment to take new steps. we must develop some sort of mechanism to make further progress in the coming years, including on market access, including passenger rights, so that we all have good rights at a high level, and that in the meantime we do not make any new concessions to our american friends. finally, i would ask the commission to keep us fully informed of the work of the joint committee, to send us all the reports and invitations, as well as the other interested parties.
i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. if we are to work towards this, we must of course take a whole series of measures. it is not just about market operation, but also about technical interoperability, strengthening the european railway agency and a solution for financing new infrastructure, as well as the maintenance of existing infrastructure. clearly, efficient market functioning is also part of the story. the first railway package was intended to contribute to this. we approved that in 2001. it should have been transposed by march 2003, and now, seven years later, we find that 22 countries are not actually in compliance and have not transposed sufficiently. that is unacceptable, of course, and the commission has waited too long to react. three things are very important. we need an independent regulatory body with sufficient resources to impose and enforce good market practice. secondly, there is of course the independence of the infrastructure manager - as has already been mentioned - which can create a level playing field for all players. thirdly, the level of infrastructure charges must also be examined more closely. in this respect, we should also look at and compare the cost structure in other modes of transport in order to create a level playing field across all modes. but that's a whole other story. as regards further liberalisation, we must now focus on freight transport and the correct application of the first railway package. i would like to be very careful about national passenger transport, which many people would like to see liberalized. i would particularly like to stress the following: in any case, good public services at a high level, extensive, efficient and comfortable rail transport for all people, rather than mere liberalisation as an end in itself.
in writing. the saga of the bluefin tuna is continuing. fortunately, is it right to say, since despite the firm position of the 27 member states of the european union and the united states, in favour of a ban on the marketing of this species, the request for a ban was blocked in march by japan, a country consuming more than 80%, and its allies. yet the facts are there: 70 percent of the world's fisheries are in danger of extinction, and we are literally plundering the oceans. that is why the message from the european parliament today is important: we need more control and better quality. the powers of the international commission for the conservation of atlantic tunas (icct) must be strengthened, as it has failed over the past 20 years to ensure sustainable fishing and to preserve stocks. the traceability of fish, from the time of their capture to the time of export, must also be improved. finally, we should welcome the remarks made by commissioner maria damanaki, who made it clear during the debate that the european commission would use all its legal and diplomatic arsenal to save bluefin tuna. the time is critical: stocks have fallen by more than 85% since the beginning of industrial fishing.
author. - mr president, on the eve of the celebrations of the fiftieth anniversary of the independence of the congo, it seemed important to us that the european parliament should take a position on the murder of mr chebeya. mr. chebeya is a human rights activist. he's the president of the ngo "the voice of the voiceless". he's a famous congolese defender. he was found dead in his car in the suburbs of kinshasa on 2 june and his driver, mr fidèle bazana, has also not been located or found. my questions are: why was he killed? on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, let me first say that it goes without saying that the member states of the union have to play an important role, a vital role, in the economic strategy of the union and of the euro zone in the coming years. but the most important question today is another one, namely who will carry out the control and who will do the sanctioning if some of the member states are not fulfilling the conditions – that is the key question. will the control be in the hands of the member states themselves, of the council? will the member states be sanctioning themselves or will it be an independent institution, a communautaire european union institution? that is today’s key question. and i have to tell you that i do not think that there is anyone here in this house who believes that the member states should sanction themselves. they did not do so in the past; why should they do it in the future? let me give your some examples. the lisbon strategy failed because there was no system of an independent institution controlling the member states on applying this lisbon strategy. and the same applies to the growth and stability pact. what happens in the growth and stability pact? when it is the small countries that are not fulfilling the conditions then we experience a greek tragedy. and when it is the large countries that are not fulfilling the conditions, as germany and france did, they simply adapt the stability and growth pact. so if the member states cannot do the job, it is an independent communautaire institution which has to do it, we are convinced of that. we have two resolutions on this that have been adopted, mostly unanimously, and a few days ago the president of the european central bank, mr trichet, gave exactly the same analysis here in this parliament. well, should it be the european parliament, the commission, the president of the european central bank? we think that it is for the european central bank, or the european commission – trichet even proposed another institution, though i do not think that we need new institutions for that – but anyway under the communautaire method the european institutions have to fulfil that role. that does not mean that the member states do not play a role; no, they have to implement this whole strategy in daily politics, in the daily strategies that they develop in their own countries and that is what we need. we need that in the growth and stability pact. we need it in the euro 2020 strategy and we need it also in the new competitiveness strategy that we are to develop in the next month. my second point is about financial supervision. what is the problem here? the problem is that the european leaders will go to the g20 and they will not be able to announce an agreement on financial supervision. the reason is very simple, the reason lies with the ministers of finance, of the european union, of ecofin who in december went back on the good proposals of the commission based on the de larosière report. that is the problem. it is not here that the message has to be given; we have to give the message to the ministers of finance. they have to go back on their proposals and go in the direction of the commission. the proposals of the commission and de larosière are based on a european supervisory authority for the banks. that is the main proposal. and what the ministers of finance did, in the ecofin in december of last year, was simply a little bit of coordination between national supervisors – national supervisors which did not work in the financial crisis. there was not one national supervisor which raised the alarm with the politicians at the moment of the financial crisis and now they are explaining that it is a good system, that they have simply to coordinate themselves. so what we need is that from the council, from the european council, pressure should now be put on the member states themselves to go in the direction of a european supervisory authority based on the proposals of de larosière.

president, vice-president of the commission, ladies and gentlemen, i should like first of all to thank the rapporteurs, mr cancian and mrs ayala sender, for the good work they have done, and at the same time to deplore the fact that the council has apparently not been able to show sufficient flexibility with regard to mr cancian's report to get a good deal out of it. it is important for us to establish a set of rules at european level for passenger rights for all modes of transport - as many colleagues have already said. we've been doing a lot of work in the last few years on aviation and railroads. i think we should learn from this and - and that is another debate - we should make an assessment fairly quickly and look together at how we can also close some gaps here and there. we now turn to passengers travelling by sea and inland waterways, and also to bus passengers. we have to use a little bit of the same methodology, keep the same red thread. first of all, our aim is to protect the most vulnerable, people who have limited mobility or who have difficulty walking. we must ensure that they have the same rights to move and to go on holiday, in other words to remain fully mobile. secondly, i think it is essential that passengers are informed about timetables, about delays and about their rights. it must be done in a high-quality way for all modes. thirdly, it is clear that we must also record things for when things go wrong. here again we try to be consistent in guaranteeing assistance, providing meals, refreshments, alternative travel options and accommodation, if needed. of course, there is still some discussion on the scope of the directive, for example. i think that we really need a good definition of regional transport in relation to the cancian report, in order to avoid back doors and ensure that there is a proper regulation.

- i'm the reporter. - mr president, if a container is transported by lorry from gdansk to antwerp, that container remains within the eu and the goods do not have to be cleared or cleared. if you take the same container by ship from gdansk to antwerp, you have to clear it first and then clear it again, because it is as if the container had left the eu. that is a disadvantage for coastal shipping and i think the commission is right to want to eliminate that disadvantage. i think that coastal shipping can take on a larger share of the freight transport in the european union and that we should encourage it. the agreement we have now reached is also part of that and i would like to thank the people in the council who have worked on it, the people in the commission and also the colleagues here in parliament. i think we have a good deal. the general outline of that agreement, the principle of administrative simplification and harmonization, has been accepted. the data are always exchanged electronically and all those involved can consult or input the data through what we call a single window. that means that these computer systems can talk to each other. everything only needs to be entered once, and that's a huge improvement. there will also be an exemption for vessels calling only on eu ports. what we have not achieved is that on the basis of the cargo a regulation, an exemption, can also be given for that cargo if a ship has called at a port in a third country. the commission is now going to examine how we can solve this problem, i.e. on the basis of the cargo. the commission is also going to look at how we can link the maritime system to the inland waterway system so that you can have a complete, very sustainable chain of water transport. we do not agree - and i regret this - on the use of one language of communication in shipping, in this case english, which we had proposed. there were a lot of colleagues here who had reservations about that. the council was totally unconcerned. the maximum we could get out of it is that there should be a recital in the directive stating that the member states should look for a common means of communication for written and oral communication. this could lead to the use of a single language, and i hope that will eventually happen. we have also failed to get the directive to include something about piloting and the fact that captains of coastal vessels who regularly call at ports can also be exempted from piloting. the commission and the council have now made a statement that they will look into this. i hope that we will move towards a european framework for this and that this will also happen as soon as possible. we had to compromise on the date. the council wanted to delay for far too long. the council felt that we wanted to go too early. we have june 1, 2015. i would have liked to have done so earlier, but i think that, thanks to the fact that we now have an agreement at first reading, we can still buy some time. i think that all in all, mr president, we are making a step forward. i think that is very important for the internal transport market. it is also a link in the sustainability of the transport sector in europe and there are many links to be made - and the commission knows that. however, encouraging coastal shipping is an important link. i have always noticed that the council is so difficult about this. the council only looks at the costs, never wants to talk about what benefits a measure might bring. the council always wants everything to be introduced later, never wants to see the importance of speeding up the creation of a single european market. the council's reluctance, its negative reflex towards a genuine european transport policy, continues to surprise me and actually annoy me. i am glad that parliament has always insisted, and this time too, on european solutions, and i hope that we can do so as soon as possible. so i am happy with the agreement we have and i would ask you, colleagues, to support it.

i have to say that i am not in favour of this. the commission has already made a number of proposals. this own-initiative report has given rise to a great deal of interesting discussion and exchange with colleagues and also to a relatively large number of amendments - 376 in number - which we have been able to summarise in some 34 compromises. if we have succeeded in doing so, it is thanks to the good cooperation with the shadow rapporteurs, the authors of these amendments, but it is also because we in the committee on transport and tourism felt that we wanted to send a clear signal to the council and the commission with this document to set the guidelines and that this should also be an interesting basis for the commission to draw up the white paper which we expect in 2010. one major aspect is that, for purely economic reasons, all modes of transport will be needed tomorrow to answer the question of mobility, whether it is passenger or freight transport. efficient co-modality has, of course, this aspect of cost-effectiveness, but also the aspect that efficiency is to be evaluated according to environmental, social and also safety criteria. this means that we want to have a co-modality in transport tomorrow which takes account of the four pillars of transport. the completion of the internal market is an important aspect for us - not only do we attach great importance to liberalisation, we must also review this liberalisation together. we must also examine how the member states have implemented what they adopted five or ten years ago. the railways are a very good example of how poorly and hesitantly this is done. the issue of passenger safety or rights is very close to the citizen. we have seen it in previous debates, we have seen it in this debate: safety will be a key concept in all modes of transport, and we want to set the objectives here too. we expect a programme for the next five years with clear objectives, because over 40 000 road deaths and over 300 000 injuries are still exaggerated and we can reduce these figures if the member states are willing to implement certain proposals. european agencies - we would simply say - are the future for us as a european regulator. but it also means that certain states in certain areas, including security, simply give up their national autonomy and give way to a more central structure in order to reduce these differences that arise at the borders. of course, co2 reduction is another important part of this report road transport with almost 70%, but also transport in general, where the shares have increased in recent years. the 27 per cent we had as the last figure is a figure that has not diminished. we must therefore make efforts and we can, and that is why we have set clear targets there too, not only for road transport but also for air transport. we believe that with a clear strategy, we can reduce co2 emissions by about 20% over the next ten years. cities are a challenge, that's clear. we expect 80% of the population to live in cities and to be mobile. that is how these objectives are defined. and finally, transport can fit perfectly into the 2020 strategy, because research for europe and in transport is an important aspect that we can support, because it can help people not only economically, not only in terms of efficiency, but also in terms of safety, and so hopefully transport and mobility will be a little more integrated into the whole of european politics than unfortunately has often been the case in the past. i would like to thank you for the very constructive cooperation i have found in this report from all the groups and from all the members. (applause from the centre)

achieve a low-carbon transport system, we will have to take a whole mix of measures, research and development, which will also have to introduce new technologies on the ground, set emission standards and internalize external costs through price mechanisms. we will also have to build up additional investments and so on and so forth. the final point, i believe, is that we use the most efficient means of transport and that we use the infrastructure we have in the most environmentally and economically optimal way. in many cases this will also have to be a combination of modes of transport and vice-president of the commission. − mr president, this has been an interesting discussion. it is clear that we are facing a very challenging task in getting the priorities right for the transport sector over the next decade while ensuring that due account is given to different aspects, sometimes of a very controversial nature. you have already signalled all these controversial issues during today’s debate, and they are very well known with regard to railways. at the last part-session you had an interesting and controversial debate about competitive rail-freight corridors, which can be a very important development. let us work together to push necessary railway reforms. railway competitiveness versus road transport and also versus the global competitiveness of europe depends very much on the internal architecture of our railways: getting rid of privileges, getting rid of political interference and all these things. it is my strong conviction after my short period in this job. is transport cohesion also cohesion or not? electric vehicles: what is the contribution of electric vehicles to congestion, which is the most detested feature in transport? finances are always controversial, especially today. then there are regional interests, already signalled here; the interests of the mediterranean area. so let us work together and try to find the right balance. i believe that we can all agree on the general goals. tackling the future challenges of the transport system requires satisfying the mobility needs of citizens and businesses in a way that uses less and cleaner energy and exploits more efficiently the infrastructure and the potential of each mode. in this framework i am confident that cooperation between all european institutions will result and bring the benefit of a high-quality future transport system.
