mr president, i think we can all agree that in an ideal world there would be no need for animal research. but the reality we know is that we live in a world of suffering, where men, women, children, sick, are waiting for treatment and therefore for the results of this research, which is vital, which is crucial. that is why we must support the excellent compromise reached by mrs jeggle of the committee on agriculture and the council. the agreement limits - and this has been said - the maximum possible suffering of animals, while not stopping this research, which is vital for millions of patients in europe and around the world. do you know that 70 percent of the nobel prizes in medicine have been won for work based on animal experimentation? we are not being asked to choose between mice and men here: this directive protects both patients and animals. this directive protects our future.
in writing. i am pleased that this report has been adopted, because from now on experiments on great apes such as chimpanzees, gorillas and orangutans will be strictly prohibited. the text also provides that animal testing should be replaced, as far as possible, by a scientifically satisfactory alternative method. the report also calls for the pain and suffering inflicted on animals to be reduced to a minimum. from now on, the use of animals will be restricted to experiments aimed at advancing research in humans, animals and diseases (cancers, multiple sclerosis, alzheimer's and parkinson's diseases). the adoption of this report is a further step in the protection and welfare of animals used for scientific purposes.
in writing. - i regret that the three proposals by the greens to amend the directive and allow for stricter national rules, to give impetus to alternative testing methods, and to further restrict the use of primates, were rejected by a majority of parliament. the new legislation on animal testing is seriously flawed. member states are no longer free to adopt stricter rules on animal testing. this restriction of national policy freedom serves no useful purpose. animals are the victims of the equalization dogma of the internal market. however, it is important that member states dare to take the lead. without national leaders, many european animal welfare rules, such as the ban on animal testing for cosmetics, would never have been put in place. the new directive improves the control of establishments and establishments breeding, trading or using laboratory animals. however, the requirement to use alternative test methods when available is less stringent than in the old directive. that is a missed opportunity to reduce animal suffering and improve the quality of research. the new rules ignore the progress made in developing alternatives to animal testing, which are often more reliable than animal testing.

author. − mr president, since i co-signed the oral question to the commission on the new israeli ‘ngo transparency bill’ – as it is referred to by the israeli ngo which calls itself ngo monitor – the bill has been considerably softened, as franziska brantner has just explained. my group and i welcome these changes. it is no longer required of all recipients of foreign funding for the purpose of political activities in israel to register under the ‘political party’ register. it seems that these organisations are no longer at risk of losing their tax exemption status, and potential breaches of the law would be punished by a fine. as i said, these changes are to be welcomed. the israeli authorities and a number of israeli ngos may not have liked our reaction to the original bill, but, as we have seen, our actions have had effect. having said this, the aim of the present draft still raises concern. firstly, it seems to be directed at intimidating the eu and its member states. in the background note that we received from the ngo which i already mentioned (israeli ngo monitor), we read the following, and i quote: ‘the eu and the member states channel tens of millions of euros annually to a narrow group of highly-politicised israeli ngos through non-transparent processes. the degree to which european states are using ngo funding to influence israeli political and public debates has no parallel in relations among democracies. therefore greater transparency regarding foreign government funding is central to the israeli democratic process and the public’s right to know.’ needless to say a number of those allegations are absolutely untrue but they are very revealing. they reveal how strong the distrust is between a number of israeli opinion makers and the eu and we need to do something about it. they also reveal how embattled important segments of israeli public opinion feel; author. - mr president, the goldstone report has had an unexpected fate. it was intended to establish the truth about possible war crimes during operation cast lead in december 2008 and in fact it caused an outcry in israel. judge goldstone was personally attacked and ngos who had the courage to testify were targeted. soon after this media storm, a set of bills aimed at giving ngos more control and weakening any opposition to government policy emerged. among them was a draft law on ngos which was originally intended to treat them as political organisations and not exempt them from taxation. secondly, a bill to criminalise the boycott of israeli products, even if they are products from the settlements, including whether the one who initiates the boycott is a government, an association or a citizen of foreign origin. clearly, if a frenchman launches a slogan of boycott of settlement products on the internet, he can be condemned; if the palestinian authority does the same, it will have the same treatment. thirdly, a bill on universal jurisdiction, which gives israel jurisdiction to judge such cases. various other projects restricting freedom of expression or movement are under way. it is impossible to enumerate them, but they converge and they are worrying. they are a direct attack on freedom of expression, an inalienable human right, a corollary of any democracy. that the ngo law has been amended by the knesset to the point of being almost acceptable to the ngos themselves is a good thing. may the other bills suffer the same fate, especially the boycott bill, which will be submitted to the knesset for a first reading on 15 september. i would draw the commission's attention to this: if we can no longer say that we must not buy illegally imported products in europe under the terms of our agreements, it is as if we were prohibiting the purchase of counterfeit or stolen watches, for example, in a market. parliament will be following this matter closely. and in this connection i ask europe: what is it that we europeans are still doing in the israeli-palestinian peace process? where was our high representative? where are we in this important process?

madam president, commissioner, i would like to express my deep concern. on the one hand, we europe advocate greater food transparency for consumers, we want to introduce traceability of animal meat from farming to the plate , so we impose very strict breeding and slaughter standards on farmers and controls to ensure the origin and quality of food as much as possible. and on the other hand, we learned this summer that british consumers had unknowingly eaten cloned beef. in my country, belgium, we have even tasted third-generation meat, which, we are told, has nothing to do with cloned meat. what kind of traceability are we talking about here? let me be humorous: as far as i'm concerned, the cloned beef bourguignon with the genetically modified carrots on my plate, i don't want that. and even more so if i didn't choose it. and in this, apparently, i really fit in with the most widespread european opinion, since all the eurobarometers on the question of cloned animals for food purposes affirm and reaffirm the very clear opposition of european citizens. that is the first argument i want to make against the presence of cloned animal products in the food chain: we cannot, on the one hand, advocate for greater consumer responsibility and wish that consumers should be better informed about what they eat and, on the other hand, refuse to hear that they do not want cloned meat on their plate. my second argument is about health. it is true that no scientific studies have proven any negative impact that the consumption of cloned meat, offspring of cloned meat or even milk from cloned meat could have. nothing has been proven, including the long-term safety of this consumption. i think the precautionary principle still applies. my third and final argument is ethical and you raised it yourself at the beginning of your speech. europe wants to be a leader in animal protection. this afternoon parliament voted in favour of limiting animal testing so that the pain and suffering inflicted on animals is kept to a minimum. but it is also true that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. it is enough to recall dolly, but also to mention the scientific opinion of the efsa of july 2008, which speaks of a significant proportion of cloned animals whose health and well-being are severely impaired, often with serious or even fatal consequences. mrs mcavan also spoke of the opinion of the european ethics group, which doubted that animal cloning for food purposes was ethically justified, especially since it was not justified by a need for food diversification. on the contrary, there is a concern that the promotion of a diet based on cloned animals may have an indirect effect on genetic diversity due to the excessive use of a limited number of animals in breeding programmes. in other food sectors, for example, dozens of tomato species have disappeared. there were 7,000 in 1900. only 150 of them remain today, of which 70 are commercially available, many of them genetically modified. is this the progress we want? that's the question we need to ask ourselves.
on behalf of the s&d group, mr president, commissioner, thank you for your reply and for your desire to establish a dialogue between users. but we in the group think that more is needed. you should know that we have always been critical of the postal directive. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a good one, but it is not enough to say that the directive is a good one. the two are closely linked. as regards the social dimension, we find that the ball has been thrown in the member states' court, but that little use is made of the possibilities for creating a level playing field between old monopolists and new players. this is a big mistake because competition is not based on innovation and innovative concepts but on cost reductions in the sector, where labour costs account for about 70% of production costs. in practice, we see postmen and women being replaced by people with precarious contracts, lower wages and greater insecurities. that is not the europe we want. the financing of a universal service, the obligation to guarantee a home order every working day, even in sparsely populated areas, has so far been paid for by the monopoly, in other words: large customers paid for the services provided to ordinary citizens. in practice, this will now have to be replaced by public support, and this in a context which will be very difficult in the budgetary terms in the coming years, and it is feared that this will come under pressure. i would therefore ask you the following, commissioner. would you agree with me that one of the main aims of the directive, namely to achieve greater social and territorial cohesion, will be difficult to achieve in this context? secondly, you refer to a report in 2013, but could you not commit yourself in the short term to a detailed analysis of the way in which the directive has been transposed in the member states and in particular to the two aspects i have mentioned, the social and the universal service? finally, how will the commission strengthen the social dialogue in the sector at european level and take initiatives to create a level playing field in social matters for all workers in the sector?
for the greens/efa group mr president, commissioner, i am of course not disputing the democratic and transparent process which led to the adoption of these various directives on the opening up of the postal market. i do not deny that they must be enforced either, but i think it is equally democratic and transparent to assess the effects of a directive and the effects of its enforcement in practice at a given moment. i think there are probably nuances between member states but i do not think that we can speak of improving the service or making the postal service more accessible to european citizens. you said something about a reasoned and accompanied attitude. i think it is reasonable to say that what we are seeing is a concentration of private operators and therefore a kind of private oligopoly, whereas we wanted to dismantle a public monopoly to organise competition. i'm not sure the outcome is what we expected. you said you were going to be a process partner. what are the regulatory practices? i am glad that there is a group of regulators but i would like to be clear about good practice in terms of regulation, there are many flaws in the system. finally, on the question of european employment, i join my colleague el khadraoui in highlighting how new jobs are created as precarious jobs and job losses are rather the rule. i am glad that you are setting up a round table to have a dialogue between all the actors. if we want, as you wish, to reconcile the large market with the citizens, the post office is really an exercise in style to show that this is possible. but this requires first of all a case-by-case assessment of the results and effects observed on the ground today. these are not the goals you set or that others set. it is high time to assess the situation before continuing to run ahead and, precisely, to make an even greater gap between the european citizens and their postal services.
mr president, commissioner, i myself asked the commission last june about the negative effects of the liberalization of public services on consumers, pointing out that numerous investigations have revealed serious malfunctions, particularly in postal services, such as price increases, a decline in the quality of service and a lack of transparency. i would once again urge the commission to analyse not only the electricity market, as it did in early 2010, but also postal services, in order to determine to what extent consumers actually benefit from a liberalised market in terms of choice, price, quality, not to mention the analysis of the quality of employment in the postal services. it is no longer a question of making grand declarations about the benefits of liberalization. it is now high time to judge the facts before taking further steps to avoid repeating the serious mistakes already made in the other liberalized sectors in the postal services. i note the establishment of the user dialogue group, but as mr el khadraoui said before me, we must go further, we expect more from you, commissioner. the commission must stop this leakage.
i am surprised, mr president, at the frivolity with which the left in this parliament is playing around with terms such as collective expulsions, when it is a question of cases in france which have been assessed on an individual basis and when it is a question of people who have received a reward for voluntarily returning to their country of origin. it is true that a member state has the right and the duty to uphold the rule of law and to act against illegal immigration and the nuisance it causes. the key to solving the problems lies with the roma communities themselves. i refer to a note by the former dutch minister for integration, van der laan, a socialist, who states, among other things, that crime among the roma is disproportionately high, that school absenteeism is enormous, that children are being abused for begging and criminal activities. with this attitude, as the socialist van der laan put it, the roma put themselves outside dutch society. so there's work to be done. i know, mr president, that it is not popular to say this in this parliament, but roma people, like all other people, have not only rights but also duties.
in writing. the debate in the european parliament on the roma issue has turned into a political debate between the extreme left and the extreme right. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. it is very clear that the roma community is discriminated against socially and economically in the vast majority of member states which have not necessarily made use of the means of integration which the union makes available. the roma community has rights, which are clearly being violated, but it also has duties, as any european citizen. the same applies to the member states, which have a duty to provide these european citizens with a place of welcome and conditions for their development. as regards the allegations against france, and pending the commission's decision on the illegality of the operations carried out, i condemn any mass expulsion not based on respect for european law. but i refuse to fall into the dubious amalgamations contained in the joint motion for a resolution, which is to compare the events of this summer to deportations. the reference to the nazi period is totally unacceptable!
on behalf of the alde group. madam president, the welcoming of the sudanese president by the kenyan authorities last august is an opportunity for the european parliament, through our resolution this afternoon, to reaffirm some very strong principles that are very close to our hearts. kenya, like all other countries, is bound to respect international law. he should have facilitated the arrest of omar al-bashir, a leader - it has been said - who is the subject of an international arrest warrant issued by the icc for crimes against humanity, war crimes and genocide in darfur. darfur - do we need to remind you? - is over 300,000 innocent victims. that said, there are other african countries that have acted exactly the same way with the sudanese president: libya, qatar, saudi arabia, to name just a few. europe must therefore have only one policy, that of firmness, in the face of the de facto complicity of the african union, which has instructed its member states not to arrest the president. our parliament must remain vigilant and call on the european union, and in particular mrs ashton, to put this respect for international law and human rights above all other considerations, making it, of course, a high point on the agenda of the next eu/african union summit to be held on 29 and 30 november in libya.
author. - madam president, i join the voice of all the members who have insisted here that mr al maleh be released immediately. like we said, he's an old man. he is accused of crimes but in the name of articles of the criminal code which seem to us to be greatly exaggerated and, behind him, above all, there are other human rights defenders, there are other lawyers, there are other doctors whom we know well, who have passed through the european parliament and who were imprisoned afterwards, who are prisoners of conscience, and we cannot accept that from syria. i would like to quote because it is important, always, to quote the name the name of mr. al hassani, of mr. al-abdallah, of mr. labouani, who has been here. they're almost all friends. i would like to say very strongly that there are many things in syria that i would like to see shared elsewhere. and it's not a huge thing that i'm saying. there is real freedom of conscience and religion. there is a real effort in education and health. there is a real effort to take in iraqi refugees, palestinian refugees. there are potentials in this country, including on the international stage, that we cannot ignore. i would like to say to president bashar el assad that i know him well "president, you do not need these prisoners of conscience to be strong in your country and to be present on the international stage. you can afford to defend human rights too". i believe that we cannot keep only the face of systematic repression from syria. it exists, ladies and gentlemen, and we cannot deny it. but there is something else in syria and that is why, commissioner, we are so attached to this association agreement. we know that syria, for the time being, has pushed it back. keep us informed of the progress of the negotiations. we would like - at any rate many of us - for syria to play the positive role it is able to play on the international stage and for peace in the middle east.
author. - madam president, one may wonder whether syria is not finally getting used to the reproaches we make to it almost every year about human rights violations on its territory. we do not despair, however, to be heard and continue to expect progress, more progress - i hear that there is progress there - for all syrian citizens who aspire to greater freedom, including freedom of expression. our resolution today is particularly concerned with mr haytham al maleh, a tireless defender of human rights. this lawyer was sentenced to three years in prison after a mock trial for, i quote, "spreading false and exaggerated information that harmed national feelings". it's the text. our resolution therefore calls for the immediate release of mr haytham al maleh for the reasons we have just mentioned and also for what has been said, his old age and the very serious health problems he suffers from. more fundamentally, of course, we want to draw attention to the human rights violations in syria: executions - we mention them in our resolution - arrests, restrictions on a whole range of freedoms and all in the name of a state of emergency, by definition temporary, which has been going on and on for over forty years. we call on the european union to make these demands, which mrs ashton has mentioned and which are fundamental, and above all to use them as a means of pressure, discussion and negotiation in the context of the signing of the association agreement. we are moving away from what was called the "prague spring" more than a decade ago, in 2000, when bashar al-assad came to power, which had, in the space of just a few months, allowed for a glimmer of clarity for syrian civil society. it is time, i think, for syria to continue to implement all these necessary reforms if it is to give a good image of the major role we hope, we want to see it play in the region.
i would like to draw attention to the problem we have with turkey. as you know, the european commission has been trying for many years to conclude a readmission agreement with turkey, but it is facing systematic opposition from that country. the situation is unacceptable for two reasons. firstly, a candidate country should be expected to cooperate rather than oppose. and secondly, turkey is not just a neighbour of the european union. it is a country with a very large common border. turkey is also one of the largest and most important countries of origin and transit for illegal immigration. i can also refer to a recent note from europol, eurojust and frontex, which mentions turkey as one of the main transit countries for heroin trafficking from afghanistan, pakistan and the balkans. it is not just the drug trade that is at issue here, it is organized crime in general. the negotiations on the readmission agreement with turkey are reportedly proceeding somewhat better than in recent years, and yet we have to note that the turkish government is now beginning to put forward certain conditions which are totally unacceptable. wherever the european side talks about 'visa facilitation', the turkish government begins to talk about the opening of the borders, about the free movement of persons for people from turkey, which of course only aggravates the problem rather than offering the only solution to the existing problems.
mr president, if you listen to the members of this house, you will find that there is a great deal of consensus on urgency. that is true, of course, but we must have the courage to get to the heart of the matter. the united nations environment programme has calculated that investing in biodiversity can yield a lot: each euro can be recovered 3 to 75 times. this means that investing in biodiversity can also bring incredible economic value. but then we must have the courage to follow this approach consistently in all our sectors, including fisheries, agriculture and our structural funds. and in the field of agriculture we have made a lot of progress in the last ten or fifteen years, but we are still very far from the final objective. agriculture can, if it chooses, contribute to the promotion of biodiversity rather than destroying it. so not just life, but following this approach consistently in all possible sectors, that is actually the challenge we have to face within the whole european union.
i voted in favour of the amendment tabled by the epp group and against the kazak report, because it is perfectly possible to maintain the best possible trade relations with turkey without the country necessarily joining the european union. the rapporteur seems to be blind when he writes that the result of the recent referendum in turkey will boost democratization in that country. on the contrary, what is indisputable is that the islamists of the akp have finally won over the power base of their party. the islamization of the streets and of public life has opened the way to the total islamization of turkish public institutions. the amendments to the constitution are nothing more or less than a direct attack on the army and the constitutional court as the last secular institutions in turkey.
the report before us is actually quite balanced. i voted against it, however, because even this balanced report continues to assume that if we continue to give more and more money and aid to africa, and in particular black africa, then this continent will eventually make it. it may be politically correct to say this, but i fear it is not. if we look at the resources we have invested in black africa since the 1940s, we see that they amount to many hundreds of billions of dollars or euros, and the result today is only greater poverty and greater misery. i think we must continue to increase emergency aid and systematically reduce permanent aid. we must make sure that we stop the systematic flow of money from north africa to the rich countries. this illegal capital flight of the last 40 years amounts to some $1.8 trillion! what we could do with those resources for black africa is hard to imagine.
in writing. like 385 of my colleagues, i voted in favour of the eu-pakistan readmission agreement which sets out the rules for the return of persons in an irregular situation to the territory of the union. after eight years of negotiations between the commission and pakistan, this instrument is added to the other eleven readmission agreements which considerably strengthen the asylum and immigration policy which europe intends to adopt. a legal framework based on international law, including the principle of non-refoulement, is therefore needed: a person cannot be deported "to a state where his freedom would be threatened because of his race, religion, nationality, membership of a social group or political opinion". i would like to say to those who are against it that this agreement has at least the merit of being consistent and effective at community level. there is nothing to prevent states from concluding bilateral agreements. our migration policy is comprehensive, and includes the fight against illegal immigration which must be discouraged, wherever possible. this is the best way to protect the migrant candidates who want to join the european eldorado and who are most often victims of unscrupulous smugglers, trafficking of all kinds and economic exploitation.
in writing. - i have approved the report on trade and economic relations with turkey. this report clearly demonstrates the importance of trade between the eu and turkey. in 2008, it amounted to no less than eur 100 billion. the report points to the wound and rightly points out that there are still many problems to be solved. the present report summarises them without distinction. it is rightly reiterated that eu policy is aimed at promoting '... democracy, the rule of law, the universality and indivisibility of human rights and fundamental freedoms, ...'. turkey will also have to make efforts to fully respect trade union rights in accordance with eu standards and international labour organisation agreements, especially as regards the right to organise and strike and the right to collective agreements. but on the european side we must also take our responsibility for visa liberalisation, not only for truck drivers but also for business people, tourists, students and the elderly. our relationship with turkey must be fair, just and honest. that means that we must clearly keep repeating that the eu will honour the promise that turkey will become a member of the eu once all the copenhagen criteria have been met.
in writing. stopping the loss of biodiversity is not only an ethical duty the notion of good stewardship requires us to maintain the planet in a state that can meet the needs of future generations but is also an ecological and economic self-evidence. recent studies have shown that the financial cost of biodiversity loss currently stands at around eur 50 billion per year (i.e. just under 1% of eu gdp) and could rise to eur 14 000 billion - or 7% of estimated annual gdp - by 2050. it is essential that the eu can play an active part in global decisions on the post-2010 biodiversity vision and targets; the report also calls for the establishment of a european union vision and targets for the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity beyond 2010.
in writing. - i approved the resolution on biodiversity, the diversity of plant and animal species, because it calls on the heads of government to act. 2010 is the year of biodiversity. in the last 40 years, it has decreased by a third. nearly half of all mammals and birds are threatened with extinction. the ep wants to halt the decline in biodiversity by 2020. that is why we must now carry out a biodiversity audit of all european subsidies and policy programmes, so that money flows to measures that have a positive impact on the environment. the tenth meeting of the convention on biological diversity will take place in mid-october. the problem is that the european environment ministers will not be able to adopt the european position until four days before the start of this biodiversity conference. the need for more extensive prior consultation was already demonstrated at the cites conference in mid-march 2010. four days before the start of a conference, it is too late to reach a common position on the eu's position with like-minded countries. the issue of biodiversity must be placed higher on the european priority list. the protection of biodiversity must be integrated into agricultural and fisheries policies and sufficient financial resources must be made available for nature conservation.
in writing. the report voted on today stresses the importance for developing countries of applying international labour standards, providing adequate funding for micro-enterprises, especially in the agricultural sector, fighting corruption and involving the social partners in economic development. it also states that education should be fully accessible as human resource development is indispensable in any development strategy and is crucial for job creation. as regards donor countries, the report calls for the pledges to devote 0.7% of gdp by 2015 to development aid to be kept, as well as for development policy coordination between the commission and eu member states. as a reminder, in 2005, the world bank estimated that 1.4 billion people were already living in extreme poverty, that is, on less than $1.25 a day. sub-saharan africa is the most affected (51%). the south asian population (40.3%) is next. in addition, 2.5 billion people still live in what is called moderate poverty, that is, on less than $2 a day.
mr president, pharmacovigilance must be to medicines what haemovigilance is to blood, and organovigilance to organs, in other words all-risk insurance. having said that, i should say, in fact, almost all risk insurance, since we know - it has been said - that taking medicines, even in strict compliance with the labels, is not without side effects. it is sometimes even fatal, 197 000 deaths a year, as our rapporteur, mrs mcavan, has pointed out in her explanatory statement, as she has also pointed out this afternoon the dramatic episodes which have marked the history of this matter. this statistic is unacceptable. it should be greatly reduced by more effective collaboration between all the actors in the chain. that is why i support all the proposals that go in this direction: better coordination through the eudravigilance database, stronger information for patients and, as has also been said, information that works both ways - mrs rivasi and mr turmes have explained this - and real transparency in the work of the new european pharmacovigilance committee. i conclude, mr president, because the independence of the expertise - and i will always come back to this - is the alpha and omega of all our policies. she's a key player here in building patient confidence.
mr president, in my view amendment no 18 to article 1 sums up perfectly the philosophy we must have: it is a question of ensuring a high level of protection of human and animal health and the environment and, at the same time, improving the functioning of the internal market. clearly, no one in this house wants to question the use of biocides, which are very useful against rodents, cockroaches, lice - i heard that this is the time for this in schools - and pests in general. the problem is, in fact, elsewhere, and i believe it is in the growing number of manufacturers putting more and more products on the market, reluctant to offer safer solutions. "too much can be bad", said the rapporteur. the other major issue is that of risk reduction policies, which are too often postponed by governments when they are ambitious but essential, because users tend to forget that biocides are familiar substances but dangerous to handle. the european parliament must also spread this warning message.

the agenda calls for the report by mr reimer böge, on behalf of the committee on budgets, on the proposal for a council regulation laying down the multiannual financial framework for the period 2007-2013 (com (2010) 0072 - 2010/0048 (cns)) (a7-0248/2010).
for the greens/efa group madam president, it is true that we are talking here calmly about an adjustment to the 2010 budget and the inscription of a line for the record, a so-called 'technical' amending budget. but we all know that the calmness of this debate compared with the previous one - which was a little less calm - is also due to a courageous and united decision taken at a time of very high tension in the euro zone last may. it was a weekend when the heads of state or government created the famous european financial stabilisation mechanism, which allows the union to borrow on the capital markets in order to be able to grant loans to member states in difficulty, and this on certain conditions. it is this spirit of solidarity which makes it possible to provide guarantees in the interests of europe as a whole and which gives the european budget real added value. that's the good example. unfortunately, the current own resources ceiling of 1.23% of gross national income has not allowed the creation of a single instrument. the budget covers only 60 billion, the remaining 444 billion being provided through an intergovernmental system called the special purpose vehicle, guaranteed by the eurozone member states, a system from which slovakia has recently withdrawn. the present calm, then, may allow us to discuss, but it is above all an obligation to go further and do more in the context of future financial prospects. and in this sense, i think we need to raise the own resources ceiling to create a real macroeconomic function for the european budget, which allows for both a buffer or european investment plans and/or guarantee mechanisms depending on the state of the european economy. we should also be designing new financial instruments. in this context, for example, eurobonds, in partnership with the european investment bank, would be very good tools. finally, we must introduce new own resources - this is urgent - such as the financial transaction tax, the carbon tax or a corporate tax, which will simultaneously reduce the contributions of the member states on the basis of gross national income and remove the famous discussion of net contributors. that is the enormous task that awaits us, particularly in the special committee, but it is also, commissioner, a matter for which we must all be willing to contribute.
mr president, commissioner, a study published yesterday by the national bank of belgium shows the extreme volatility of electricity prices in belgium, but also the serious problems of lack of price transparency for consumers. it fully confirms the many problems that have arisen with producers since liberalisation. the belgian federal regulator has publicly expressed its concern about the lack of competition in this market. all this only reinforces the urgent need for an energy consumer charter - which you have just announced within three years - so that, in the end, liberalisation does not open the door to all abuses to the detriment of consumers. can we hope that you will take a decision to speed up the creation of this consumer charter?
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, commissioner, first of all i would like to thank you for your clear answer and your very clear views. we must send a very clear message here as a parliament, and that message is one of support for the commission. because whether we think the directive is going too far or not far enough, that it is too late or too early, it is the fact that a majority in this parliament, together with the council and the commission, has adopted something which now has the force of law and which must be implemented. the commission has a duty to ensure that this legislation is implemented in an unequivocal manner. and that legislation leaves nothing to be desired in terms of clarity. it has been referred to at length and i say it again here: from 1 january 2012, all flights departing from or arriving at an airport located in the territory of a member state shall be subject to that directive. so even if they come from third countries, both for the outward and the return flights. the latter is important to emphasize because there are a number of companies that leave it in doubt. it is not possible that third countries should have to pay for only half of their flights, i.e. the outward or the return flights. this would lead to distortions of competition between european airlines on the one hand and between them on the other. that cannot be the correct interpretation. third countries facing problems with this legislation should be reminded once again that their airlines may escape the provisions of this directive if they themselves take similar measures to reduce co2 emissions in the sector. in other words, it is not our business to make potential revenue, on the contrary. if everyone followed us, we would undoubtedly make less money, but we would achieve our environmental objectives, and that is the essence of this legislation. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. as regards the united states, it should also be noted that the second eu-us aviation agreement, which was finalised last spring and is currently in the approval phase, implicitly recognises that market-based instruments on emissions should be allowed. in particular, article 15 of the agreement provides that the joint committee may make recommendations to address possible overlaps or inconsistencies, to avoid duplication of measures and costs. there is therefore no doubt that the agreement negotiated by both aviation blocs provides for mutual recognition of such emissions trading schemes. i therefore urge you once again to keep up the good work, to communicate very clearly and to make it clear that there is no turning back.
on behalf of the s&d group. madam president, commissioner, the community trade mark, which was introduced 16 years ago, has been an effective means of combating counterfeiting and providing consumers with the assurance of a quality product throughout the european union. the protection of the trade mark is the same in all the countries of the european union, whether it is the trade mark as such, the goods and services claimed or its proprietor. it is also a genuine solution for the single market, a low-cost solution, especially suited to small and mediumsized enterprises, as you have said, commissioner. this is a real step forward, because in a single market only a community trade mark can guarantee really extensive protection. in addition, the community trade mark is an ideal instrument for widespread protection in europe, as it is easy to administer and low-cost. it is also an important tool for market and customs surveillance authorities. the court of justice of the european communities ruled on 14 september that a sign consisting of the shape of a product necessary to obtain a technical result cannot be registered as a community trade mark. the commission thus responded to a request from the lego company for registration as a community trade mark of its construction briquette. the court therefore dismissed the action, thus demonstrating that the use of the community trade mark must be strictly regulated. this court judgment highlights the new challenges facing regulation 40/94 on the community trade mark. would it not be appropriate for the european commission to update this regulation in the light of new technological developments, in particular the development of the internet and globalisation? it can never be stressed enough that the primary objective of this regulation is first and foremost to guarantee consumers a quality product and complete, clear and comprehensible information throughout the european union.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, first of all i wish to thank president van rompuy for the report on this important council. i want to start with the last part of his intervention, on the roma issue. i would say very clearly and personally to my colleague in the ppe group, mr daul, that the commission has acted in the right way on the roma question and we should not criticise it. we have to help the commission in this fight. i call on the commission to stick to its point of view, because what we are facing today is a fundamental question. values are as important as market rules in the european union, and it is important that everybody sees too that the commission treats every member state equally – be they small or big member states. when it comes to values, there is no difference between small and big member states. they all have to apply the same rules. unfortunately, it is true that the european summit has been a little bit hijacked by this issue, but that happens in politics. certainly, in europe, matters are often hijacked by the issue of the day. that is naturally a pity because, on the issue of economic governance, we have no time to waste. we cannot afford to face a new economic and financial crisis without these new instruments on economic governance in place. we know that a monetary pillar cannot work without an economic pillar. if you look at the spreads today, the spreads between the german interest rates and other interest rates are still very high. we need a package on economic governance on the table as soon as possible, and i hope that the commission will come forward with this, as it has promised, on 29 september. in my opinion, to be credible – because that is the most important thing – this package has to respond to and fulfil three primary conditions. president van rompuy, the first condition, which you have always defended, is that this is not only about enforcing fiscal surveillance but also about improving macroeconomic surveillance, because that is the lesson of the greek debt crisis. it is not only about the stability pact, it is also about the state of the economy in that country. the second condition is that the package should contain effective sanctions. i personally think that the best way to do this is, firstly, through progressive sanctions; secondly, there must be a combination of financial penalties and political penalties – not a choice between one or the other but a combination of both; thirdly, in some cases this can also mean suspending the voting rights of those who are breaking the rules. that is so in normal life and that has to apply also in political life, i should say. the third and final condition, in my opinion, is that the trigger has to be in the hands of the commission and the ecb. i believe – and i have said this a few times already – that surveillance by member states simply does not work. it did not work with the lisbon treaty, it did not work with the stability pact and it will not work in the future. we have seen this with germany and france, who did not apply the stability pact in 2005 and simply changed the stability pact at that moment. this will happen again in the future, so the trigger has to be in the hands of the commission. i hope that on 29 september, based also on the work of the taskforce, a credible package will be on the table both of the council and of parliament.

president of the european council. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, a very brief reply. there's always different ways of putting things. we can be guided by perceptions, we can be guided by achievements and by facts. my character is more about achievement, reality and facts. what has been done in the first eight months of the year, with a new commission and new institutions since the entry into force of the treaty of lisbon? we have managed to agree on a major financial surveillance, an extremely important reform, with the cooperation of parliament, of course. after long negotiations, we have agreed on external action, on the external action service, a new ministry of foreign affairs, a new diplomacy for the european union. these are two extremely important achievements, in the context of a complicated institutional architecture, but one that was wanted. we have agreed on a major package to help greece survive. we have agreed on a package of 110 billion, together with such an extremely important reform programme, which is being implemented despite the major difficulties facing the greek government. we agreed on a package of 750 billion in financial support in case - of course, under certain conditions - which calmed the markets and created new prospects. under the union's impulse, major reforms are being carried out, and i am well aware that they are being challenged within the country and beyond. major reforms are under way in a number of european countries. without the pressure from the institutions, this would not have happened so quickly. in foreign policy, i would remind you that we agreed at 27 after the security council on the sanctions against iran, on additional sanctions, which was not at all obvious. we have agreed on a resolution by 27 member states on the dialogue between serbia and kosovo. you know very well that five european countries do not recognise kosovo. this dialogue is of the utmost importance for stability and peace in this area, which is also a european area and must have a european perspective. we finally agreed on a trade deal with korea. we have agreed on a package of support not only financial but also commercial for pakistan. we agreed on a comprehensive approach to the middle east. in december 2009, a common position was reached by the 27 on one of the most disputed regions in the world, the middle east. a new statement was added to it, which was taken up by the quartet a few days ago. all of this has been done. they're achievements, they're facts, and i stick to facts. as far as the task force is concerned, no, we're not working too slowly. we started in may, and now we're in september. in a few months, we made great strides. and we will conclude, not in december as planned, but at the end of october. and i can assure you that i will be able to report here on some qualitatively important conclusions from the results of the task force. there is no problem between the commission and us. we work hand in hand to achieve good results. there is no rivalry of institutions. as regards the roma problem, i would like to recall the five points, which are not council conclusions as such, but on which there is a very broad consensus. we are now awaiting the commission's investigation. we must not prejudge them. and i note, as requested by the european council, that the commission has begun work on a plan for the better integration of certain minorities, including the roma, into the european union. i will add a few words in dutch, which is a minority language, of course, but from time to time we can make it heard. i think that the balance of the first months in extremely difficult circumstances despite everything a positive balance has been. we never thought that economic growth would take place so quickly. in fact, even if it is fragile at the outset, if a few months ago one had said that in one country economic growth would be 3.5%, and today the average in europe would be about 1.5%, no one would have believed it. in a number of countries reform programmes are being set up in extremely difficult political and social circumstances. we are working to create a new kind of economic governance for europe. the situation today - and i am sure it will - in the economy and even in the labour market is better than it was a few months ago. that is not the case. this is a very inspiring moment for the whole community. but the work is glass van af. i think - that is my last word - that many european citizens today are not waiting for a battle over which method is best. i have my own idea about: natural is the most appropriate community method. but they are waiting for the results of our policies. especially if they wait for a perspective. as a better prospect, we must say more loudly, so that the european population can again have more confidence in it. this will be a major contribution not only to the support of european thought, but also to economic growth. i'm someone who keeps to the facts and doesn't let himself be distracted by perception. the facts show that we have made progress in the last eight months with all the institutions, that we are moving in the right direction. we'll be getting closer in the coming months.
vice-president of the commission. − mr president, just a quick reaction to the statements by the political leaders. first, the issue of the roma: here again i would like to assure this parliament that authorities, of civil society as well, and we need to pull with all our strength in one direction. only in this way will we be able to approach a positive solution to the plight of the roma in europe. on proposals on economic governance, i would also like to assure the political leaders that our proposals will address the issue of macro-economic imbalances through greater surveillance. we will role there is very much recognised, but it is very clear that it represented only the first steps in helping pakistan. we need to be involved more, we have to be more consistent, and we have to possible, and for that they need to be compatible with wto rules and we need the support for our wto partners. we are also looking for ways as to how we can evaluate pakistan’s eligibility for gsp plus for 2014. this would then be a measure for the long term that i believe would help pakistan and would help its speedy and successful recovery. concerning the middle east, it is very much recognised in this house, in the council, in the european council and in the commission that this is absolutely a top political priority on the global political scene. i would like to assure all of lady ashton – that we will do our best and that we will play an important role in this process.

in writing. mr president, this historic agreement was made possible by the excellent work of the belgian presidency of the council, which supported parliament's approach and succeeded where some other presidencies had failed. in keeping with the main points of the larosière report, we in the european parliament have ensured that the new european supervisors have sufficient power. they can impose binding measures on financial institutions if national supervisors fail to act, they ensure greater coordination between national authorities and settle their disputes. fortunately, this goes far beyond the non-binding coordination between national watchdogs, which many member states initially opted for. we have already seen what a lack of european supervision can lead to. the new supervisory structure not only protects the bank's customers but also the taxpayer. there's one small flaw. i regret that parliament's call for supervisors to be housed in one place, in frankfurt close to the ecb, was not heeded. fortunately, parliament was able to insert a review clause so that in three years' time it can be verified whether the european supervisors do not need more powers and whether the spread of the authorities is appropriate.
in writing. the main objective of this draft regulation is to improve the safety of biocidal products used and placed on the market in the european union and to simplify the authorisation process, also at eu level. to this end, the commission proposed in its draft regulation to bring together existing european legislation and update it in the interests of both consumers and manufacturers. it therefore aims to reform the current system by extending its scope to cover materials and articles treated with biocidal products, introducing a european-wide authorisation of known low-risk products, reducing animal testing, aligning data requirements, expanding the role of echa and ensuring uniform application of the regulation throughout the european union.
in writing. the new eu regulation on biocides is not just a question of the internal market. it is also a matter of protecting health and the environment. that is the message conveyed by the european parliament this afternoon. it is true that several requests from industry must be met with regard to these products to combat pests: access to the european market, rapidity of approval procedures, non-duplication of tests, consistency with legislation on pesticides. this is not a blank cheque for the producers. the principle of substitution for the most harmful substances is clearly stated. this is mainly the case for pbt and endocrine disruptors. the responsibility of all actors is enshrined, including the manufacturer placing biocidal-treated articles on the market. let's remember the case of the poisoned sofas! the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of the environment. one finding: with limited resources, it will be difficult to obtain reliable tests and thus reduce the health risks to users.
in writing. for ordinary mortals, the term "property" always refers to the possession of material goods. but it is too often forgotten that intellectual production must also lead to property rights: copyright. artists, writers, composers, authors and creators of all kinds should therefore be able to legitimately be paid for the use of their works, while consumers should have access to a legal and diverse offer of content on the internet. the report by mrs gallo, which was voted on wednesday, 22 september, states that the phenomenon of online piracy, against which copyright holders seem to be helpless, is taking on significant proportions. it is therefore necessary to have a system for the protection of literary and artistic property rights which takes into account the rights and obligations of users, fundamental freedoms and stimulates innovation within a framework of legal certainty for the benefit of both consumers and rightholders. i therefore support the text voted on this afternoon.

the agenda calls for the report by véronique de keyser, on behalf of the committee on development, on health care systems in sub-saharan africa and global health (2010/2070(ini) (a7-0245/2010).
the rapporteur. - madam president, commissioner, thank you for being here again. this report is in fact a response to a commission communication on health at the global level, but it has taken a slightly different approach and focused much more on the mutual systems that are now flourishing in africa. let me take a little detour. in fact, the health situation in africa is dramatic despite all the international aid and it's due to a significant amount of factors, whether it's climate change, financial crisis, wars or bad governance in some countries, greed that is excited by the very wealth that is found in africa. this, plus the big pandemics like aids, malaria, tuberculosis, etc., all of these factors together make this a real disaster. so, i have to first of all commend the work of ngos and some churches as well, who are doing quite remarkable work in emergency, in conflict zones, but that cannot mean a long-term response, it is not a sustainable response. there are so-called vertical funds, which we have mentioned, and which are used to combat major pathologies, such as aids, malaria, tuberculosis. they drain a lot of money and are quite attractive for private charity because you feel like you're giving to a cause you're really good at. but in reality, these funds, which are doing their job, are also only covering a small part of the problem because, unfortunately, more people are dying in africa today for lack of sanitation, for lack of clean water -- children dying of dysentery -- or for lack of a nearby clinic -- than, alas, from aids or malaria. hence the attention that has been paid to an emerging movement since the 1990s, which are the initiatives of insurance systems but especially mutual insurance companies that have sprung up a little bit everywhere in different african countries. the interest of these systems of mutual health care is, of course, to involve the population in the management of their health. let us not hide the fact, they will not be self-sufficient, they will not be able to cover the entire financing of care or access to medicines, but, combined with other funds, with other subsidies, they do in any case make it possible to ensure coverage of health care and medicinal care with, in addition, participation, a kind of social dynamic that has settled in. so there are hundreds of them, they're in different countries, they're in different sectors. there are women's health insurance, coffee shop workers' health insurance, etc. so the challenge for us is to support this social dynamic, to finance it, to coordinate it, while saying that maybe one day it will be self-sufficient, but it's not today. so if these mutuals can exist, they can only exist if there are, of course, next to them, what are called horizontal systems. that is, why go to insurance or pay for any health accident, for any surgery, if there is no hospital, if there is no doctor, if there is no medicine. so, as a corollary to this, we believe that the european union must not only support, coordinate and support these mutual societies, but also put a very strong emphasis on horizontal systems, on basic health care, even asking the vertical funds to part-finance this so-called horizontal care, this basic care which is necessary, which is the balance for the mutual societies to exist. this is the set of problems that is mentioned in the report. i have no more time, but i may be able to answer some colleagues who have some concerns about reproductive health which is mentioned in this report and which is a point which i hold very dear.
the debate is closed. the vote will take place tomorrow. the commission shall be assisted by the committee of the regions.
in writing. i support the commission's proposal to abolish the visa regime for citizens of albania and bosnia and herzegovina. the visa regime must not isolate the citizens of the western balkans or divert them from the european union, their direct neighbour. the decision to liberalize visas is a consequence of the progress made by these two countries, particularly in strengthening the fight against organised crime. this is a decision that will have a significant psychological impact on the population, for example on students, who until now have been queuing up at consulates.

in writing. is the european parliament prepared to vote for a moratorium on all new offshore oil drilling? and so we can learn from the human and ecological tragedy of the oil disaster that occurred on april 20 in the gulf of mexico: 11 dead, 800 million litres of oil spilled and a devastated biodiversity. that is the only real political issue at stake in this afternoon's vote. the european parliament's plenary session, by voting no to the moratorium, has sent a clear message which does not respond to the concerns of the citizens. it is clear that drilling on the high seas is a risky activity and all precautionary measures must be taken: reinforcement of the principle of environmental responsibility, creation of a special european fund fund funded by the mandatory contributions of the operators of offshore installations. especially when such drilling is done in the arctic in extreme cold and deep conditions. the european parliament, by refusing to vote for a pause in the exploitation of new drilling on the high seas, is falling short of the position of the european commission, which, on 7 july, announced through commissioner oettinger the de facto installation of a moratorium.
in writing. the european union must adopt the same aggressive attitude on the international scene in the protection of biodiversity as it has in the fight against climate change. the challenge in nagoya is as much to preserve biodiversity as it is in cancun to reverse the hellish trend of global warming. the figures are a reminder of the scale of the challenge: 30% of animal and plant species have disappeared since 1970. in europe, despite the growing awareness of the need to protect nature, which has resulted in the establishment of natura 2000 ecological zones covering almost 18% of the european territory, biodiversity loss continues. what to expect in nagoya from october 18 to 29, 2010? at best, the european union should speak with one voice. it must also have the courage to speak out on the issues that are causing concern, including with the host country, japan, which cannot allow unsustainable and particularly barbaric whaling and dolphin fishing to go on with impunity. and to convey the right message: the best way to protect species diversity is still to integrate this concern in a transversal way in economic activities: trade, tourism and transport.

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, this day in the european parliament is largely about the fight against poverty in the world. this is important, but as mrs figueiredo said, poverty is still too widespread in europe too. the fight against this phenomenon is one of the priorities of the 2020 strategy. but while it is good to set goals that should have been more ambitious, it is better to give yourself the means to achieve them. and adequate minimum income for all in europe is clearly one of those means. the excellent report by our colleague shows its usefulness in this fight and shows real progress. but without a legislative initiative from the commission, which also recognizes the role of the minimum income, we fear that the states will not give effect to our wish to reject poverty. this report must therefore be supplemented by a framework directive which respects national practices.
president, commissioner, one in six people in the european union is at risk of poverty. the majority of the victims are under 17 years of age, but many older people are also vulnerable. poverty is increasing, even among europeans who have jobs. i therefore believe that we must also take an initiative at european level, ladies and gentlemen. economic growth, better education, more jobs. we are all for this, of course, but it is clear that this is not enough and that we need a specific policy against poverty. it is, moreover, - as has already been said here - one of the flagships, one of the priorities of our new 2020 strategy. what is it about, exactly? it is about dignity of life, the right to dignity of life, it is about food, housing, clothing, basic things which in 2010 are apparently not guaranteed in our prosperous european union. i therefore believe, ladies and gentlemen, that we must also take action at european level to achieve this minimum income. it is not an instrument - as mrs bastos has already said - which should be filled in in the same way throughout the european union. there should be sufficient scope for member states to choose their own specific solution. but we must keep up the pressure to avoid leaving vulnerable people behind. we therefore expressly call for a system of guaranteed minimum incomes to be introduced in all the countries of the european union. this would be an important signal of hope for all the weakest in europe, who are today affected by poverty and social exclusion.
a few days after the world day against poverty and just two months before the end of the european year against poverty and social exclusion, the european parliament has the opportunity to make its voice heard. there is one choice we must make here: will 2010 be the year of sweet talk and empty promises against poverty, or will we take concrete action that will help millions of europeans move forward? parliament can choose the latter option by requiring the commission to come forward with a proposal for a framework directive laying down the principle of an adequate minimum income in europe. today, there are too many disparities in the eu, with some countries even lacking a minimum income. but even in the countries which do better (such as belgium), living wages are often still below the poverty line. i believe that everyone deserves equal opportunity. when you push people below the poverty line, you deprive them of those opportunities, and that manifests itself on different levels (health, housing, employment, ...). moreover, if we really want to make a difference in achieving the eu 2020 objectives, we simply cannot afford to give up on people.
in writing. i support the report on women in precarious employment because i cannot imagine that in the 21st century there are still large disparities in employment, quality of work, income and equal pay for equal work or work of equal value for women in the eu. unfortunately, the overrepresentation of women in precarious jobs is a major contributor to the gender pay gap, and i share the view that improving the quality of employment for women will help to reduce this pay gap. i also believe that all workers, including women workers, in precarious situations must have the right to education and vocational training and that girls and young women should have better access to quality education, training and studies. finally, it is essential that the commission continue to support the member states in the development of an action plan to enable the gradual transition from the status of a precarious worker to that of a regular worker.

on behalf of the alde group. madam president, i just want to say that i think it is a good document produced by the commission, except that it comes 15 months too late. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. indeed, whenever there are negotiations, we are sold a mid-term review, which comes at the end of the term. this is not right at all, and it must not be repeated. secondly, the main point of this dossier concerns own resources. why would you do that? because it is the basis of the european union. the founding fathers of the union created a european union based on own resources, i.e. customs duties, taxes on imported agricultural products and vat. that's it. it is because of the british cheque that we started with national contributions. this is the reality. we must therefore return to these own resources as the basis for financing. it must be done now because it is the only way to move europe forward. it must also be done for democratic reasons. in a democracy, there is a need for people to pay their various incomes directly to europe and thus to be able to contribute and control, through these resources, the functioning of the european union. my second point, madam president, is actually about the things that are being said: that the european budget and contributions at european level must be reduced to clean up public finances is nonsense. the reality is that the fiscal deficits, the budget deficits in europe, are €868 billion today, or 7% of the gross domestic product of the european union. even if we follow the idea of curbing expenditure at european level and having a complete freeze of the european budget, these 868 billion fall to 860 billion. that's the reality of it. what is needed is the reverse, the transfer of powers from the national to the european level to reduce the costs of member states' expenditure. the european union can do better in defence, research, diplomacy and infrastructure, and these can help to reduce budget deficits. and that is how we must act now, mr president-in-office, towards your colleagues: we must really open the own resources gap because otherwise you will not agree on the 2011 budget.
the interim evaluation of the multiannual financial framework which the commission is presenting to us today is both disappointing and hopeful. disappointing because the document does not open any prospect of a revision of the multiannual framework for 2010-2012. the 2011 budget has shown, however, that this is unsustainable. the financial framework does not take into account new tasks under the lisbon treaty, nor the 2020 objectives. that is untenable. at the very least, there should be room for greater flexibility. fortunately, the commission is also in favour of this. this increased flexibility should apply as from 2012. the document is also hopeful, because the commission is finally opening up the debate on own resources. this may well be the great debate of the coming years. a difficult but inevitable debate. the treaty stipulates that the eu budget must be financed from own resources. the current financing is 80% by transfers from national budgets. these are not european own resources. they are part of the expenditure of the national budgets. they are putting pressure on the national deficits which the european stability pact is trying to reduce. member states therefore try to limit these transfers. at the same time, they expect more from europe, as shown by the lisbon treaty and the 2020 strategy. we must break this contradiction. this can only be done by resorting to genuine own resources. these should make it possible to reduce the contributions of the member states and increase the resources of the eu at the same time. the commission rightly points to new forms of taxation directly linked to policy areas which are being developed mainly at european level as possible own resources. i refer to the regulation of the financial sector and climate policy. i hope that the european council and the council will also understand the need to open the debate on own resources now, as a matter of urgency, with a view to the financial perspective beyond 2014.

mr president, i would like to address the commission and the council just to point out the three shortcomings that the european governance project presents to us. first deficit, we have extremely strict discipline on what is deficit and debt and we have extremely flexible, or loose, discipline on what is investment, and i am thinking of europe 2020. we really need to have equally strong discipline on both sides, because austerity alone is not going to restore economic activity. secondly, it is clear that if we are to exercise control over expenditure, we must also ensure that we have the necessary revenue. i have often said that there can be no coordination at the budgetary level without coordination at the fiscal level. thirdly, we consider the democratic deficit to be a deficit, and this is particularly striking to me in the proposals of the task force. for the latter, parliament obviously does not exist and i think that is unacceptable.

in writing. the extension of maternity leave is an important project for the place of babies and parenting in our society. i support the proposal to grant 18 weeks of maternity leave and the principle of paternity leave. the perverse effects of overly generous measures which risk discrimination in recruitment must be avoided. i am also in favour of giving more freedom of choice to pregnant women and women who have recently given birth. they should be able to decide when they wish to take the non-compulsory part of their maternity leave. this should help them to better reconcile their private and professional lives and preserve their employability. it is also important to develop a genuine status for the returning woman who, after a career break, wishes to return to work. i would also draw attention to the fact that the right to parental leave and the guarantee of return to work must be afforded to employers, especially in small and mediumsized enterprises, which may otherwise hesitate to hire or promote women of childbearing age.
in writing. i welcome the adoption of this report on the proposal for a directive on the implementation of measures to promote the improvement of the safety and health of pregnant workers, workers who have recently given birth or are breastfeeding at work and measures to help workers reconcile work and family life. we have extended and improved maternity leave and, for the first time in european history, we have introduced paternity leave. this vote will be a milestone in the history of the fundamental rights of european mothers and fathers. i say to all those who want to sacrifice social rights on the altar of the economic crisis that they should go and get the money where it is and not penalize the citizens any further. a more comfortable maternity leave and the creation of paternity leave are also struggles for a society that is a little more humane, where the family is increasingly the last refuge from the torments of life.
in writing. - president, ladies and gentlemen, in order to maintain our prosperity and to be able to pay for pensions, more men and women must go to work and stay at work. we must therefore, even in times of intense international competition and budgetary constraints, dare to take measures which invest in families and which alleviate the pressure of combining work and family life. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the social partners are able to take part in the negotiations. that is why i am in favour of extending maternity leave. but in difficult budgetary times, we must be realistic. twenty weeks with full pay is not feasible for social security and the government budget. although i am in favour of extending maternity leave, i abstained on that basis in the final vote. i do, however, support the original commission proposal to extend maternity leave to 18 weeks, while maintaining the current capped benefit system. i hope that this proposal will have a better chance of passing the second reading in parliament.
in writing. i voted for the estrela report because i do not want to create any misunderstanding about this: it goes without saying that women have special needs during and immediately after pregnancy and that society as a whole has a great interest in this and therefore a great responsibility. i would like to ask a few questions of principle. first of all: is it really reasonable to impose from our european ivory towers mandatory rules that will apply throughout the union, including the member states that have a long way to go economically? and who's gonna pay for that? and that brings us to the second fundamental observation: is it reasonable to place the burden of these measures on the shoulders of employers alone? will this not ultimately lead to fewer jobs for young women - because employers will not be inclined to bear the "risks" of pregnancy of their young workers all alone? it is easy to vote 'social' in this parliament. but we are not the ones who have to bear the burden of this social voting behaviour.
in writing. by adopting by an overwhelming majority the report by my colleague barbara weiler on the proposal for a directive on late payment, the european parliament has created balanced and clear rules that support solvency, innovation and employment. small businesses and public hospitals will benefit from the measures we propose. the former will no longer face financial problems due to late payments and the latter will be able to benefit from an extension of the payment deadline to 60 days due to their special nature, the financing coming from reimbursements under social security systems. the agreement we should have with the council will also allow the directive to enter into force quickly and be transposed by the member states as from january 2011. i welcome the efficiency of this vote.
in writing. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we have just voted on the new directive on combating late payment and i have given my full support to the agreement. excessive payment delays and certain late payments threaten the sound management of a company, affect its competitiveness and profitability and may ultimately endanger the survival of the company. the current directive is not sufficient to reduce the backlog of payments. i therefore support tightening up the existing rules. we are providing additional security for companies in terms of maximum payment periods, since payments must in principle be made within 30 days. this is certainly important for payments between enterprises and public authorities. the member states and their governments must now set the right example. it is a matter of credibility that the european institutions will also respect the same legal deadlines. the fact that the directive clearly states that contractual deviation from standard payment terms is only possible for objective and not unfair reasons will be an important factor in its enforcement. finally, i hope that the fixed compensation for collection costs will put late payers on the right track, or better yet keep them on the right track. our businesses and our jobs will be lost.
