in writing. the resolution on the forthcoming eu-us summit and the transatlantic council aims to reaffirm the transatlantic agenda and strengthen eu-us cooperation on issues of common interest. this relationship remains a major priority for the eu. in addition to the fact that the eu and the us share a number of common interests and values such as promoting peace, democracy, respect for human rights and a market economy, europe and the us are linked by the world's largest bilateral trade and investment relationship: the $4.28 trillion transatlantic economy. the reality is that we are increasingly economically integrated. this relationship is also very important in terms of development cooperation, since together, the us and the eu provide almost 80% of development aid. finally, our partnership is more than necessary to advance world stability and to discuss the major multilateral issues, namely climate change, energy, the economic and financial crisis, crisis management, development cooperation, regional issues, non-proliferation and disarmament, security.
in writing. i fully share the objective of preventing and combating terrorism and other serious forms of transnational crime. more and more countries are using passenger data in this context. however, in the digital age, it is essential to pay particular attention to the respect for and protection of civil liberties and fundamental rights, including the right to privacy and data protection. the essential principles to be given priority in this area are necessity and proportionality. the collection of transit data must not only be useful but necessary. the principle of proportionality must also prevent the measures envisaged from going beyond what is necessary to achieve the objectives pursued. thus, there can be no question of using the data for the purpose of exploiting it or profiling it. since the entry into force of the treaty of lisbon, the european parliament has played a major role in the approval of these agreements and should therefore be fully and regularly informed of all developments in the field of pnr data.
in writing. the steady increase in life expectancy of european citizens is excellent news, promoting intergenerational exchange and dynamism. on the contrary, viewed from the perspective of the sustainability of our pension systems, the ageing of the population is a real headache for policy makers and those who are concerned with budgetary balance. if we add to this the low birth rate in many of the 27 member states, intergenerational solidarity is like proust's madeleine of european societies. paraphrasing thomas mann's excellent report, europe must meet the demographic challenge, and jean bodin's famous phrase: "there is no wealth but in men" is now becoming very true! to maintain a just and united society, that is to say to refuse the transfer of public debts to future generations and to promote a balance between the number of pensioners and the number of assets, there is no single solution. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a very good one.
in writing. the thomas mann report on the demographic challenge and solidarity between generations highlights the challenges of demographic change, including unemployment and precarious work, education and discrimination against vulnerable groups. it stresses the need for solidarity between generations and makes relevant suggestions, including an initiative for a european pact for people aged 50 and over, including the fight against age discrimination, the goal of full employment up to the legal retirement age and assistance for the reintegration of older people with disabilities. i abstained in the final vote, although this report was more than satisfactory, because paragraph 24 calling for the abolition of the mandatory retirement age was retained. but if we do not have safeguards to enable our elderly to retire, we will not be able to ensure that they have a happy end to their lives. i refuse to let our elders work until they die like in the 19th century.
in writing. there is growing discouragement from researchers, research institutes, universities and businesses towards the research framework programmes due to the complexity of the calls for tenders launched by the european commission. the 2020 strategy aims to improve the framework conditions and access to finance for research and innovation, to ensure that innovative ideas can be transformed into products and services that create growth and jobs. therefore, in the interests of efficiency and in order to promote the highest quality research, it was highly desirable to simplify the implementation of the research framework programmes. we had to take measures to remain attractive in order to attract and keep the best scientists in europe and to remain competitive in a global economy. i welcome the draft report which seeks to promote a balance between trust and control, between risk taking and risk prevention, while ensuring sound financial management.
in writing. this resolution on the livestock sector is yet another call by parliament to take account of the growing difficulties faced by our european farmers. while the latter have to comply with increasingly stringent rules on traceability, hygiene, animal welfare and environmental protection, they are now seeing their production costs skyrocket: fuel, fertilisers and, above all, cereals now account for 60% of the total cost. we call on the commission and commissioner cioloş to take more measures to curb price volatility and create a safety net. our farmers must survive the pressure of imports and work in a fair and competitive market. because no one pays for work, we simply want to enable them to live from their work.
in writing. i was receptive to the reading of the original motion for a resolution on the current crisis in the eu livestock sector, which denounces in particular the lack of transparency in the food chain, making producers and consumers the weak links in the agri-food chain, the extreme volatility of the eu markets and the eu dairy sector in particular, the distortions of competition between eu producers and those in third countries subject to less stringent regulations on food quality and safety, and calls for the need for effective market regulation and a strong cap budget post-2013. but i would also like to denounce the adoption of this resolution and, in particular, amendments nos 1, 2 and 3, which open the way to the presence of genetically modified soya and maize from third countries on european territory and to the return of animal meal to the diet of non-ruminants when food safety cannot be guaranteed. that is why i have taken my responsibility in voting against this joint resolution, which is contrary to the precautionary principle which should inspire us all!
i should like to begin by congratulating the rapporteur. mrs evans has a remarkable perseverance, which is why we have reached a compromise today that the s&d group can support. i would like to thank the commissioner for his report and for the work he has done. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. thirdly, and this is very important to me personally, a good methodology, which has been heavily negotiated by parliament, which should ensure that substances are reviewed to see what their possible effects might be. in addition, there are a number of substances (hbcdd) - i have to look at my paper for that - one of the most widely used and controversial brominated flame retardants and three important phthalates which will be dealt with as a matter of priority. of course, there are also steps forward with regard to nanomaterials. we would have gone much further, but we are glad that the commission is now coming forward with a statement on this - very important. would we have gone further? yes, of course, we are dissatisfied with some aspects. naturally, we in our group would have liked to see a number of additional substances banned, a more rapid review and fewer exceptions. as for the latter, i cannot fail to return to the solar panels. the s&d group was the only group to oppose to the last moment a totally wrong idea to keep solar panels out of the scope. a special lobby has been set up to this end, and i think it is completely wrong, because this will allow cadmium, a substance which we have banned since 2003, to continue to be used for at least the next ten years. i find that particularly regrettable, especially for a sector which should be taking the lead in this area. nevertheless, the s&d group will be happy to support this compromise. i only hope that the edf group has not got the wrong file, because i did not see much of the edf during the trilogue.
- i'm the reporter. - the resolution which we are hoping to adopt on thursday is a very important signal. it shows that there is a great deal of support in this parliament for our typical european media model, which allows for both commercial broadcasting on the one hand and public broadcasting, public media companies, on the other. that model, mr president, is typically european, because you will hardly find it in the united states or japan, let alone in china. the dual media model has proved to be the best guarantee of media pluralism, of independent editorial independence and of press freedom in general in most of the member states of the european union. but unfortunately, that model is under heavy pressure in the european union. the pressure comes from all sides. this is due to the heavy cuts faced by the competent national or regional authorities, but also to the great pressure exerted by the private media companies on public broadcasters. they consider public service broadcasters to be representatives of unfair competition, especially on the internet. this overall situation means that public service broadcasting in a number of member states of the european union is having a real difficulty in keeping its head above water. this puts the delicate balance of the dual system on the line, and it is compromised. we must now do everything we can to ensure that our european media model survives these turbulent times and that it is anchored in the future. this requires a number of measures, which we shall set out in the resolution. the first point, and i do not know whether this body is aware of it, is that all the member states of the european union formally committed themselves at that time within the council of europe to guaranteeing freedom of the press. this includes a clearly defined mandate and funding for public service broadcasting. long-term funding is important. it is time that we as the european parliament reminded all 27 member states that they made those commitments at the time and that they must also honour them. another point: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the media are free to express their views. that is why we want to use the so-called media pluralism monitor. that is an instrument which has been developed, commissioner, at the initiative of the commission. it is a very useful instrument that can serve as a beacon in a number of member states, in a number of regions where media diversity and media pluralism are threatened. and then, last but not least, i think the biggest challenge for all media is to be online, digitally on the internet. the growing nervousness - we might even say growing hostility - towards initiatives of the public broadcasters on the internet is worrying. some colleagues in politics, and particularly in national politics and also in the media, seem to forget that clear agreements were made last year on the online activities of public broadcasting, and it is important that they are respected. i think that we should also develop models in which search engines and internet service providers also play a part. i'm going round. i think we can no longer sit on the sidelines, or we will wake up at some point to find that google and youtube and apple have taken over our media landscape. that is, of course, absolutely undesirable.
i am speaking on behalf of the alde group. mr president, our sympathy goes first and foremost to the victims. in my country four people drowned, and i hear that in other countries hundreds, thousands of people have suffered enormous financial and emotional losses in their businesses and in their homes. of course, our thoughts go to them first. in the last few weeks, months, even years, many member states have had problems with water, with floods, with floods. i have a question for the commission. do you think it would be useful in evaluating what has happened now to bring together experts from the different member states, from the different regions, and to have those people give an opinion on what has happened? so we can get a better idea of what's causing it. it is a very complex matter, but i think that if we can understand the causes better, we can also talk to those experts about measures. i think that a risk analysis by river basin, as we do for the environment in the water directive, is appropriate here. and also consultation on preventive measures. how can we prevent such a disaster? because it will still rain heavily and very concentrated, because that is the cause. and finally , i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the water is not used in the same way as it is in the past. i would ask the commission to bring together experts to make a proper assessment of the disasters that have now occurred.
for the greens/efa group mr president, on behalf of the greens, i would like to extend my condolences to all the families and people affected by these tragic floods, to all those who have lost loved ones and to all those who have lost some of their property. but i would also like to express my thoughts and my thanks to all the firefighters, rescuers and volunteers who have been very active in the last few weeks in all the affected areas. in the immediate future, and in the months ahead, we must help and compensate the victims. we also need to have a coherent and innovative policy that tackles the root causes of the problem, and i am proposing three types of measures to the commission. first, to implement renovation and construction projects that reduce the water-resistance of the soil, because every time you build an industrial zone, a shopping centre, a housing estate, they have to be apprehended so as not to water-resist the soil a little more. this provision does not apply only to flood-prone areas. secondly, we need to change our farming practices. intensive agricultural policies have made the earth more vulnerable to weather changes. all agri-environmental measures in this direction should therefore be strengthened. finally, the issue of alerting and coordinating local authorities to prepare for and prevent such incidents when they occur. we believe that the information and mobilisation have sometimes been too slow and that more active coordination procedures for the circulation of weather alerts may be needed. i therefore propose that in all its policies - prevention policies and active policies in all other areas - the european union should take stock of this and be able to develop useful policies in this respect, otherwise, in a few months' time, when the next somewhat extreme weather events occur, we will be meeting here to offer our condolences to the victims, to individuals, to families, to businesses. i think we must avoid this and we can do so in all our policies.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, it is now ten years since the united nations security council adopted, for the first time in its history, a resolution highlighting the specific situation of women in war situations and their role in conflict resolution. ten years after that resolution, thousands of women are still being raped in countries at war, such as congo. i myself have spoken out against the impunity enjoyed by the perpetrators of these atrocities for many years. moreover, these atrocities are being committed while un forces are sometimes, and often even, on the ground. i think we are sorely lacking in political ambition and adequate funding. in particular, i stress the need to train the staff of the european and international institutions on the ground and to raise awareness among the victims of the violence. we need to make it clear to everyone that gender mainstreaming improves efficiency in all sectors. we must put an end to the impunity which perpetrators of violence against women too often enjoy and establish an adequate complaint procedure. we also need to increase financial resources within the european institutions for work on gender and human rights, including for implementing mechanisms and assessing the progress of the european union. i note, as minister chastel has said, that efforts have been made, in particular through the exchange of good practice or through participation in a task force. in addition, 10 member states now have a national action plan. i hope that the other 17 member states will do the same soon. i should also like to say that i have high hopes for the future of the foreign service. i hope that it will involve equal participation of women and men, especially on the ground. we must never forget the importance of women in conflict resolution, nor the need for trust-based communication between the forces of international institutions and the people on the ground.
for the greens/efa group madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the greens have no objection to public money being invested, provided that this money is the catalyst for the transformation that our society, our industry and our economy must undergo in order to become more just and, above all, more sustainable. and aid to uncompetitive coal mines, let us remember, runs counter to these objectives. it is to keep a business in a state of flux which is not economically sustainable - otherwise it would not need subsidies - or environmentally sustainable. as mrs veldt has just pointed out, the coal industry has been calling for a prolongation of this transition for several decades, with no end in sight. it is also true, ladies and gentlemen, that for several decades many european countries have been trying to put an end to an industry which was not sustainable. this is the case in my country, in france and in many other countries. we therefore support the commission's proposal, with the corollary that the subsidies should be used to enable the workers concerned to undergo a decent and dignified conversion. but now, under the pressure of what you have heard today, the commission is preparing to retire in disgrace, mr almunia. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this matter, but that it should be a matter of the member states themselves, and that the commission should be involved in this matter. he's not hiding from it. i would like to say in conclusion that as long as the european union, as long as the member states devote all their energy to supporting the industries of the past with public money which - and i hope the irish crisis reminds us of this - has become something extremely rare, as long as we support with this public money the industries of the 19th century, we will not put europe in a position to conquer leadership positions in the industries of the 21st century. meanwhile, the world is moving, and moving fast.
in writing. the entry into force of the agreement for scientific and technical cooperation between the european union and the government of the faroe islands will allow the faroe islands to participate in all activities under the 7th framework programme of the european union for research, technological development and demonstration activities. despite the small size of the research community, researchers from the faroe islands have already successfully participated in community-funded projects. they have expertise in research areas related to the geographical situation of the faroe islands, including marine resources and the environment. the agreement will allow these researchers to participate in other fields such as energy, food, agriculture, fisheries or biotechnology. regular meetings will identify common research priorities and areas where joint efforts can be mutually beneficial. it will also promote the mobility of students and researchers in higher education.
in writing. - the report (doc. i agree with the rapporteur's position on the directive and also with the explanatory statement in his report. the european union has been operating a transitional vat system since 1993. attempts to move to a so-called definitive system have so far failed. however, this should not be a reason not to keep trying. the vat system is not just a matter of rates or bandwidths, but also of a simple, legally secure system that works well in an internal market, is tailored to smes and is resistant to fraud. the commission's intention to take up this matter again is good news. i would like to encourage the commissioner responsible to work on this difficult dossier.
in writing. should the european union continue to subsidize industrial sectors that are destined to disappear in the long term within the european union? it is this difficult question that we had to answer with the adoption of the rapkay report on state aid for the closure of uncompetitive coal mines. the european commission has proposed a scheduled end to the subsidies by october 2014. a wise proposal that takes into account the negative environmental impact of aid to the coal industry and the need for member states to provide for appropriate measures, for example in the field of energy efficiency, renewable energy or carbon capture and storage. the overwhelming finding is that €1,288 million in production aid for a total of €2.9 billion in subsidies to the coal sector between 2003 and 2008 did nothing to limit the loss of market shares, nor did it allow the 100,000 workers in the sector to be effectively supported in their vocational conversion. i therefore regret that this afternoon the european parliament did not have the courage to say no to the extension of aid to coal mines until the end of 2018 and to turn decisively to new sources of energy, sources of jobs for the european population.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, in december 2005 there was a prime minister who sat here in the house. i think one thing we all learned is that we really need to see a seriously reformed budget for the future. let us all together – the commission, the council, member states and the european parliament – work out how in the next few years we can build a consensus for a changed and reformed europe in which the budget is a rational part of the future and not an unfortunate piece of horse-trading. well, that prime minister was tony blair, the man who made the last deal on the financial perspectives. here we are now five years later. what mr blair said is the essential thing that parliament wants to achieve: the engagement of the eu institutions and all involved parties to reinvent the financing of the union and to base this financing on rational grounds and not on a horse-trading competition, which is the case today. on the figures, let us be very clear, it is no secret that parliament could be ready to accept the council figures, of course within a broader deal. but we need also to agree on a second element, colleagues, which is flexibility. let us be very clear on this. that flexibility is not a concession from the council side; flexibility is necessary, indispensable, otherwise we cannot finance a number of important issues. what is more is that flexibility is an ‘aquis’. it forms part of the interinstitutional agreement that we have already. in fact, the council has broken its word by refusing this now in this budget. finally, and this is the most important thing, we must agree on the future financing of the union. therefore the issue of own resources must be reopened; no doubt about it. let me be clear that own resources is not a question of changing the treaties. own resources is also not a question of new competencies. it is a question of applying the existing treaties and it is a question of financing existing competencies, because the union was always meant to have its own revenue. mrs merkel, mr sarkozy and mr cameron do not know this, but the union was founded with its own revenue. that was the basis of the financing of the union by the founding fathers. however, we all know that with time and a contribution from a certain iron lady, the union lost the link to its own resources, so instead we now have absurd discussions about who benefits more and who benefits less from the union. not seeking the eu interest but only focusing on the net payment position of every single member state is what the budget is today in the european union. that is called business for the moment in the budget issues of the union. this way of acting is killing the union – it is destroying the spirit of unity in the union and it is destroying the solidarity in the union. i repeat we can only require genuine own resources which can also lead to what a number of countries want, which is to have less national contributions. if we do not change the system, governments and not citizens will stay in charge. if, on the contrary, we go in the direction of own resources, not governments, but citizens will be in charge. in short, you could say that own resources for europe colleagues means more european democracy; it is the basis for a modern european democracy. so i have one request to you mr barroso. my request is very simple. whatever the format is – a convention, a conference, an igc with or without national parliaments – do not listen to the voices of the past. go for the future, use your right of initiative and put a formal proposal on the table as fast as possible. the pro-european majority in this house is fully behind you and behind that initiative.
it would not hurt, mr president, if the european parliament for once set a good example and thus defended the principle that the european union should put its finger on the pulse. at a time when savings are needed everywhere and citizens need to tighten their belts, it is a completely wrong signal to advocate a large increase in the eu budget. the eu is attracting new powers and wants more money for them. well, there are plenty of opportunities to save seriously. the european court of auditors estimates the financial impact of irregularities in the agricultural policy at between 2% and 5% of expenditure. the financial error margin for the regional funds is above 5%, and that is the case for the two main budgetary expenditure headings. the commission has also proposed that the council should be able to adopt a decision on the matter. instead of always advocating an increase in incomes, we should also talk about saving. a direct eu tax, as the commission wants, is unacceptable for several reasons. it is another step towards a european superstate, it will inevitably lead to ever new expenditure and it will equally inevitably lead - and this is also admitted by commissioner lewandowski - to an increase in the overall tax burden in the net paying countries, and this is not negotiable at the moment.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, like my colleague mr niculescu, i too welcome the commission's intention to continue its efforts to simplify the cap, but i am also concerned about the follow-up to my colleague mr richard ashworth's report on administrative simplification. in fact, given the difficult circumstances facing our farmers today, i would like the cap after 2013 to allow farmers to focus on the main objective of producing safe, quality food, while encouraging them to provide non-market public goods. it is high time to lighten the administrative burden on our producers, to eliminate unnecessary legislation and to give farmers in the 27 member states the same chance to understand what their obligations are and what their rights are. it is a simpler and more responsive cap that they need. at a time when the world is facing competition from farmers, despite successive food and agricultural crises in both the north and the south, and when farmers are fighting for fair prices and fairer pay for their work, the least we can do to help them is to ask the commission to consult regularly with the agricultural sector in order to assess the impact of our regulations on the ground and to define simple, practical and transparent rules for our farmers and our taxpayers. i'll close with a quote from charles baudelaire, who said that absolute simplicity is the best way to stand out. i would ask the european commission to devote itself to this essential task and i naturally place my full confidence in the commissioner to achieve this objective.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i have decided to table several amendments to mr paolo de castro's excellent proposal because beekeeping is an essential sector of activity. i am not going to argue with the honourable member, mrs lulling, who has been dealing with this subject since 1994. i think we can pay our respects tonight. we must realize that the disappearance of bees would be a turning point in human history. such a change in biodiversity would endanger the vital diversity of food. this profound disruption of ecosystems would destroy any prospects for future generations. the disappearance of pollinators is a matter of serious concern, as the decline is now being measured globally, in both wild and domestic bee populations. cocoa, vanilla, melon, passion fruit, all these tropical crops are totally dependent on pollinators for their fruit and seed production. on every continent, and increasingly, honey bees are dying in excess of their numbers as winter approaches. many beekeepers in europe have had to close their doors. today, more than 80% of the world's flowering plant species and 80% of the european cultivated species depend directly on insect pollination, mainly by bees. without the foragers, most crops would not be able to produce as well as they should. this is true of many wild species, but also of fruit trees, protein crops and vegetable crops. it is therefore difficult to imagine a meal that does not have bees as a close associate. the causes of their decline are well known: the elimination of their nesting sites, the scarcity of plants that provide them with nectar and pollen, but also diseases, parasites, and especially the spraying of pesticides, and more specifically neurotoxic pesticides, which are particularly destructive to bees. farmers and gardeners must be aware that bees are completely deprived of toxic products and that they are valuable crop helpers who must be protected as a matter of priority. i would like to conclude by saying that we must react because we cannot accept honey from elsewhere - of lower quality and not meeting our strict production criteria - as a fatality, as the only alternative to the slowly developing shortage of honey in europe. i therefore ask the commissioner and the commission to integrate beekeeping into the cap by specific measures.

mr president, in too many member states we have lived for the last 25 years on a model of economic growth based essentially on debt, private debt and public debt. the problem is that this debt first financed consumption and financial speculation rather than investment, at the very time when the rest of the world, china, brazil, india were starting to invest. we may read in the history books that this is the moment when europe really lost its footing. but there is no such thing as fate. of course we need robust european economic governance, but we must first tackle the fire caused by this debt, and to think that we will solve the problem by reducing public spending alone is to ignore reality. we will not get out of this crisis without restructuring and rescheduling the debt where it exceeds the sustainable level and the real ability of the debtors to repay. because we need to be clear. both debtors and creditors are responsible for the accumulation of debt. some have borrowed beyond their means, but others have lent irresponsibly, lured by the hope of substantial profits without risk, since the taxpayer would of course always be there to bail them out. so we will have to join the effort, and if we do not do this, we will be condemning ourselves to a japanese-style scenario, that is to say, a scenario of the decline of the european union. i think the citizens of this continent deserve much better than that.
if you listen to the debate today, mr president, you will notice something, in particular the big question - which is important to almost all the groups - of economic governance. the agreement is only apparent, because there is a very big difference in the way you interpret economic governance. if you look at the way the council does that - and you see that in the council conclusions - it is a one-sided focus on what the council thinks is necessary, namely saving, saving and saving again. it is almost an ideology that is elevated to an economic legality: if you save enough, everything will be fine. that is not the way we interpret economic governance. - no, it's the opposite. it will require something quite different, and it is not just our group that is saying that. look at economists, look at yesterday's financial economic times, not a socialist propaganda paper, which says 'if you only save, you undermine already fairly weakening economies and that makes it increasingly difficult to pay back the debt'. what you need is an investment. a very good and obvious example of what is needed is an increase in the level of participation. you can pay off your debts. in all our member states. but then you have to have the courage to invest in education, you have to have the courage to invest in the combination of family and work. if you look at the savings in the different member states, that is the kind of investment that is being cut right now. it is necessary to have a vision, a vision of economic policy, and on that basis you put the resources to do that. at that point, you will see how best to shape the stability pact. i think that is the big difference between how we and the council look at economic governance.

in writing. the strengthening of moldova's participation in community programmes will, on the one hand, strengthen ties in the fields of culture, education, the environment, technology and science and, on the other hand, also strengthen political relations through the eastern partnership and the negotiations of the new association agreement. the implementation of the protocol requires, however, political stability and democratic development on the part of moldova. the failure of the constitutional referendum on 5 september 2010 is regrettable. it is essential that moldova applies the european neighbourhood policy action plan and the reforms outlined in the 'rethink moldova' document. the holding of parliamentary elections on 28 november is an essential step for moldova's future stability and prosperity, as well as for the development of its relations with the european union. it is important that these elections take the country forward on this path and are fully in line with international standards for the holding of free and fair elections.
in writing. i voted against this report, which i have always strongly opposed. while i support the idea of better informing patients, i disapprove of the means by which this is achieved. the pharmaceutical industry will never be a philanthropic cause! and the line between information and advertising is very thin. it is true that the text which was approved almost unanimously has nothing in common with the commission's original idea, which would have opened the door to advertising disguised as 'information'. this proposal was in fact a clear expectation of the pharmaceutical industry to be able to get out of the legal ban on advertising for medicines. the report as it stands today contains many more guarantees: that the information supplied by pharmaceutical companies and intended for dissemination will be subject to prior checks and will not be disseminated in the media. however, the authorisation to make summaries of product information public means that there will be elements to highlight and others, such as side effects, that might well be regularly excluded from summaries because they are not very 'selling'. isn't that already a publicity stunt? was that really the goal? was the gamble really worth it?
in writing. advertising of prescription drugs is prohibited in the european union, and so on. pharmaceutical companies must follow clear rules of objectivity in the non-promotional information they disseminate. those are the two important messages delivered this afternoon with the passage of the patient information act. a unanimous vote of the plenary (558/48) which only confirms the unanimous vote, minus one vote of the health committee. there are safeguards in place, and there are many. the european commission proposed a ban on information on prescription drugs on television or radio, the european parliament decided to extend it to the print media. manufacturers are subject to a number of obligations: product characteristics described, enhanced labelling, prior authorization from health authorities. i do not understand all the "noise" made about the matter, especially in french-speaking belgium, by consumer associations and mutual societies, relayed by some european elected representatives who, even before the start of the discussions, had already condemned these two pieces of legislation. it is an easy political stance which i regret, because patients have a long wait for their illness, and without it, citizens would be left to the internet cowboys.
in writing. − in times of self-requested information, patients need to be informed in a clear, objective, reliable and good way about the way medicines work. although advertising for medicines is prohibited in the union, the commission did create an opening. the commission's proposal - which i did not approve - lacked a sound strategy and the distinction with advertising was too vague. the commission has also given too many rights and responsibilities to the pharmaceutical industry. now that parliament has made important changes, i can support the proposal. companies are restricted in the use of official documents (product summaries, evaluation reports) and in the channels through which the information is provided: health magazines, internet sites or letters to patients are no longer allowed. all the pharmaceutical labels must be pre-screened and approved by the competent authorities. this excludes self-regulation, which was still present in the original proposal. i still question the feasibility of this proposal because a binding information strategy from the government is still stronger than the government controlling the policies of the companies. however, i voted for the bill because it does provide better information for patients in the end.
in writing. the fjellner report on drug information raises legitimate concerns about the possibilities for pharmaceutical companies to advertise to patients. my colleagues in the committee on the environment and public health have certainly improved the text by refusing to endorse various forms of disguised advertising and by proposing that the health authorities of the member states should monitor the communications of pharmaceutical companies to the general public about their prescription medicines, but i remain very cautious about the extremely blurred line between information and advertising. i am strongly opposed, for example, to the idea of allowing companies to send patients, through medical staff, advertisements for their medicines. i don't want doctors to be pressured into becoming the salesmen of the pharmaceutical industry. so, despite the strict controls of my colleagues, i abstained on a text which was clearly problematic for me.
in writing. i voted against this report, which i have always strongly opposed. while i support the idea of better informing patients, i disapprove of the means by which this is achieved. the pharmaceutical industry will never be a philanthropic cause! and the line between information and advertising is very thin. it is true that the text which was approved almost unanimously has nothing in common with the commission's original idea, which would have open the door to advertising disguised as 'information'. this proposal was in fact a clear expectation of the pharmaceutical industry to be able to get out of the legal ban on advertising for medicines. the report as it stands today contains many more guarantees: that information supplied by pharmaceutical companies and intended for dissemination is controlled ex ante and that it is not disseminated in the media. however, the authorisation to make summaries of product information public means that there will be elements to highlight and others, such as side effects, that might well be regularly excluded from summaries because they are not very 'selling'. isn't that already a publicity stunt? was that really the goal? was the gamble really worth it?
in writing. in order to ensure that patients' rights and interests are respected, patients must be able to obtain high-quality, objective, reliable and non-advertising information on medicinal products. if we want to protect health, pharmaceutical labels must be as clear and complete as possible, meeting the essential quality criteria. in the same spirit of patient protection and information for the general public, it is necessary to promote the easiest possible access to information, in particular by providing a summary of the product characteristics and a package leaflet in both electronic and printed form. this is why it will be necessary to ensure that certified and registered websites are available which provide independent, objective and non-promotional information.
in writing. almost eight years ago, the european parliament adopted a directive on the reduction of hazardous substances (rohs directive), responding to a strong consumer demand for safe and non-harmful consumer products. this safety approach has been mainly achieved by banning the use of lead, mercury and cadmium in products as diverse as household appliances, radio and television sets, electric trains and video games. i welcome the revision adopted today which extends the scope of the directive to other products. but without initiating a lawsuit of intent with regard to substances as important as pvc. in the same way, it was important to revert to the restrictive position taken in june by the environment committee on nanomaterials. this revolution of the infinitely small deserves better than a simplistic pro- or anti-position, as in the case of gmos. the practicability of the rohs directive is also essential and i welcome the consideration of the potential health and environmental effects of these substances, the establishment of a methodology and the complementarity with the reach regulation.
in writing. - i have approved the report on hazardous substances in electrical and electronic waste. this report is a step in the right direction. the category of equipment is extended to include medical devices. in addition to heavy metals such as mercury and lead, a number of brominated flame retardants are also banned. the report also provides for an open scope so that in future all electronic devices (with a number of specific exceptions) and other dangerous substances may also be covered by this directive. clear criteria have been established for this. i regret, however, that a ban on pvc has not been included in this directive, as proposed by the green group. it is very important that hazardous substances in electronic waste decrease. despite european legislation prohibiting the export of hazardous waste for recycling, studies show that more than two thirds of european e-waste is exported. often this e-waste is dumped illegally in, for example, west africa, where it is very under-processed and causes enormous health problems and environmental pollution. this rapidly growing waste market is a money maker; the commission has also proposed that the member states should be able to take measures to ensure that the waste is not disposed of in a manner that would jeopardise the environment.
in writing. - president, ladies and gentlemen, electrical and electronic equipment is an integral part of our daily lives. the european rohs system, which regulates the use of hazardous substances in these electronics, has in fact become a universal system used in no less than 28 countries outside the eu. so far, restrictions have been imposed on mercury, lead, cadmium and a number of flame retardants on the basis of the existing directive. rapid technological progress and improved scientific knowledge require us to re-arrange this legislation. the recasting of the directive is also necessary to make rohs an efficient and flexible instrument. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the use of these substances. it also sets out how reach and rohs relate to each other, avoiding duplication of legislation. for these reasons, i have today given my full support to the agreement which parliament has been able to conclude with the council. i hope that agreement can also be reached quickly on the directive on waste electrical and electronic equipment (weee). the two legislative instruments are inextricably linked.
in writing. - i voted in favour of the evans report because the overall balance of the agreement reached with the council is positive. nevertheless, as a shadow rapporteur, i would like to express my displeasure at the fact that solar panels are being kept out of the scope of the directive, thanks to intensive lobbying from the industry. as a result, cadmium may remain in solar panels placed on the european market for (at least) another 10 years, whereas other electrical and electronic equipment is not allowed to do so. i strongly deplore the fact that a sector which should be a driver in our evolution towards a green economy has been eager to escape this important environmental legislation and that a majority in parliament and in the council has given in to it in favour of one technology in particular (the use of cadmium telluride thin film solar panels).
the word that has been used most often here today is strategy. you're right to be. we therefore welcome the commission's strategy. we are a little disappointed, however, commissioner, about the strategy, because a good strategy means making a good analysis, which is related to the supply problems, which is mainly related to the ecological problems and to the social problems. and from that strategy, we prioritize. and i'm very much attached to the word "priority", because that means you're making a ranking. at the top of the list is energy efficiency. you have referred to it, but why is it so difficult, if we are so concerned about energy efficiency, to formulate objectives which we want to enforce in our member states? that's the key. we know it works, and it's a way to get where we need to get. so i would ask the commissioner to include this priority anyway. and if you are preparing for the big summit in february, to actually put it forward.

in writing. - i voted in favour of the resolution on the situation in western sahara. this joint resolution sends a strong signal from the european parliament condemning the recent upsurge of violence in western sahara. on 8 november, the sahrawi protest camps were violently evacuated. several people were killed on both sides and many civilians were injured. the camps were a peaceful protest by the sahrawi against repression, marginalization, the plundering of their natural resources (including fish) and the poor living conditions under moroccan occupation. the resolution calls for an immediate end to the violence and for an independent inquiry into the events in gdaim izik and laayoune. the moroccan authorities are called upon to allow free access to western sahara for journalists, humanitarian organisations, members of the european parliament and independent observers. the increased repression by the moroccan authorities against human rights defenders and sahrawi activists (arbitrary arrests, prosecutions and imprisonment) is very worrying. these events put pressure on the informal talks between morocco and the polisario front, which were resumed under un auspices in early november. however, a just and lasting political solution is necessary to improve the situation of the sahari people.
author. - madam president, since the iraq war, this country, instead of being liberated from a military dictatorship, has been plunged into violence. the spectacle of saddam hussein's hanging was in no way a means of mitigating this violence. on the contrary, it has exacerbated tensions in that country. you know that the european union's fight against the death penalty is a constant battle. we believe that by abolishing the death penalty in europe we have contributed to a step forward in the progress of mankind. that is why the idea of a possible execution by hanging of tarek aziz, who worked with saddam hussein, and two of his deputies, seems to us to be an indignity. not because of the personality of tarek aziz, who was, however, and i knew him very well, a remarkable personality, unfortunately embedded in a totalitarian system. but i think - and i say this in a personal capacity - that he has made, especially at the last moment, major efforts to try to open up his country. but it's not the case of tarek aziz that's important. we cannot have the hanging of tarek aziz as a symbol of the liberation of iraq on our television sets tomorrow. that is why i ask my colleagues to vote for this resolution, which also includes a part on the persecution of christians in iraq, which i support.

author. - madam president, there is sometimes good news, even in the case of emergencies. so i would like everyone to remember the emotion that gripped our throats when we saw the release of aung san suu kyi on television. we have a special connection with this remarkable woman who won one of our first sakharov prizes in 1990 and who, in her 20 or 21 years of life, spent almost 15 years in prison. she is a symbol of a political woman who can stay in a country that is as tough as burma, that is, free and always resistant. this is absolutely extraordinary. but this event should not make us forget that its release is fragile. she's already been released and then reassigned to house arrest. secondly, let us not forget that the recent elections in burma were a sham, a sham democracy. before the elections, laws were passed to stifle freedom of speech. people, like monks, were prevented from voting. there are still 22,000 prisoners of conscience in burma today and it remains one of the most terrible dictatorships in the world. we therefore call on the burmese authorities to respect the rights of association and freedom of expression and to release political prisoners, and we trust this remarkable woman, whom we are waiting for in the european parliament or to whom we will go if she cannot receive her sakharov prize, to maintain a spirit of freedom in this torn country.

in writing. the number of major disasters in both eu and non-eu countries is increasing due to climate change and increasing urbanisation. disasters hit the least developed countries more severely, resulting in particularly high loss of life. the economic cost of such disasters is also much higher in these countries. the creation of a rapid reaction force will help to coordinate eu and member state activities better and give greater visibility to eu work. that is why i supported this recommendation. like the author of this recommendation, i would like to call for the many excellent practical proposals made in the barnier report to be taken into account.
in writing. − with climate change increasing the number of disasters worldwide, causing serious humanitarian, economic and environmental damage, it is essential that the european union can respond quickly. the creation of a european civil protection force provides a clear structure for this rapid response. lessons have been learned from the earthquake in haiti to increase the union's effectiveness, coordination and visibility. i am therefore in favour of bringing civil protection and humanitarian aid together in the portfolio of a single commissioner. i also believe that humanitarian aid operations should always be carried out under the coordinating umbrella of the un wherever possible. the report calls on the commission to work with national governments, local authorities and civil society organisations in the affected areas to set up programmes to enable local communities to take on the responsibility for disaster prevention and management. the deployment of military and civil defence means in the event of disasters should be used only as a last resort.
in writing. cbrn risks give a new dimension to terrorist attacks, accidents, natural disasters and/or pandemics because they know no borders and can cause a large number of casualties. the response to this hazard must be considered at the stage of handling, storage, access restriction or control of the materials. interventions following an incident involve extensive cooperation between the different authorities in a member state and between the national authorities of different member states and the european bodies. it is a priority to strengthen the non-proliferation and disarmament regime by ensuring the universal and unreserved application of all relevant international treaties and agreements. it is also important to address the risk of proliferation through terrorist action. in addition, a european crisis response mechanism should be established as a matter of urgency to coordinate civil and military resources to enable the european union to have a rapid reaction capability in the event of a cbrn disaster. finally, european standards for quality and safety must be developed.
in writing. i am in favour of this regulation which aims to adapt the regulation of 19 february 2004 establishing an immigration liaison officer network. these adaptations are necessary as a result of the evolution of european law and practice in this field. the new regulation provides in particular for: • a legal basis between the frontex agency and the liaison officers; • better use of icon; and • greater rationalisation of reporting systems on network activities. with this new regulation we hope that this network of immigration liaison officers will be more effective.
in writing. i voted in favour of the draft directive of the european parliament and of the council on the european protection decision. i am not in favour of limiting the scope to strictly criminal proceedings, as some of the more resistant member states may be (the united kingdom, the netherlands since the last change of government, etc.), but i am in favour of covering a wider range of proceedings. i hope that the strong signal sent by the european parliament in plenary will not only unblock the current blocking minority in the council but also ensure that the next hungarian presidency takes up the matter.
in writing. any victim of crime who enjoys protection in a member state of the european union must be able to enjoy the same protection when travelling to another member state. although most of the measures concern women victims of gender-based violence, it should be noted that any other victim of violence, including children, can benefit from this initiative. it is also welcome that a protection order can also be sought to protect the family of a protected person. however, protection should not be limited to the physical protection of the victim. the dignity of the person as a human being must also be taken into account. i am well aware of the complexity of the initiative and the challenges it may face, in particular because of the different legal systems and criminal, civil or administrative procedures that may vary from one member state to another. nevertheless, the member states can only be encouraged to move in this direction.
in writing. i welcome the overwhelming adoption of the report on the european protection decision. the measure adopted today will ensure that a person who enjoys protection in one member state will also be protected in any other member state to which he or she travels. the directive covers not only victims of domestic violence but also forced marriages, trafficking in human beings and female genital mutilation. this is a major step forward for the rights of victims and their mobility in the european union. i now call on the council to follow the european parliament's position by massively supporting the european protection decision.
in writing. we have just adopted by an overwhelming majority the report on the proposal for a directive on the prevention and combating of trafficking in human beings and the protection of victims. this is a historic vote for several reasons. firstly, it is the first time in the history of the european union that binding european legislation has been adopted to combat trafficking in human beings. in addition, the european parliament and the council reached agreement at first reading, which means that the provisions of this directive will be implemented as soon as possible. finally, the directive increases the penalties for traffickers (minimum 5 years) and strengthens the protection and assistance provided to victims. the new rules will apply to trafficking in the sex industry and to labour exploitation, for example in construction, agriculture or domestic services. i therefore welcome this historic vote.

on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, i shall try to be briefer than the previous speaker. my group proposes to adopt the budget as agreed by the council. it offers a small increase and that is a reasonable compromise. keep in mind that eu member states and citizens have to cut their budgets. unfortunately, the debate on the budget had a false start. the parliament demanded an increase of 6%, which was ludicrous and showed that this congregation was out of touch. here, the debate was even inflamed by hotheads who wanted to kidnap the budget to force access to own resources, in other words eu taxation. we have always opposed that. lisbon is clear on it. the parliament has a right to advise, not a right to co-decide. the parliament lost this battle, as we predicted. now it is moving to the position which the european conservatives defended all along – a budget of 1% of gdp. the hotheads here must cool down, particularly the greens. just imagine that the eu had no budget next year while the euro was in a tailspin. is that the image you want to offer the financial markets? they would not be at all happy. i would like to congratulate the governments of the united kingdom and the netherlands for their principled position. i thank the belgian government for their role as honest broker and i thank the commission for its knowledgeable and cool-headed work.
in writing. the conclusion of the cancún climate summit is hardly a super-achievement. but it's also more than nothing. there have been some concrete steps forward, on the fight against deforestation for example, and there is a green light for the start of a $100 billion climate fund. but it's all so terribly tiring and slow. a real procession from echternach. meanwhile, the warm-up clock is ticking. and europe in this story? the eu has certainly learned from the mistakes of last year's copenhagen summit. in cancún, europe was at the heart of the negotiations and was acting as one. the eu model is looked to from all over the world because we have the most progressive and binding agreements. europe is a role model when it comes to climate. we must maintain that frontline position stubbornly for years to come. it is a profound and positive difference from almost all other continents, a huge asset in terms of image, and it also has great economic and social benefits.

on behalf of the alde group. mr president, i believe that in life there is a rule that applies everywhere: when a group is attacked, we must react with unity and solidarity. it is exactly the opposite that happened in 2010 when the euro was attacked, since since the greek crisis, we have witnessed only disagreements in the discussions, but certainly no unity, and certainly not enough solidarity. in fact, we must have the courage now - and i say this also to the president of the commission - to recognise that all the temporary measures that have been taken are not enough. this is not my analysis, it is the analysis of the international monetary fund, the oecd, mr trichet - who is still the head of the euro, the president of the bank - for whom all the measures that are being taken and all the measures that are in the pipeline do not go far enough. everyone says that we need to act in four areas: firstly, a stability pact with real sanctions mechanisms; secondly, an extended and permanent crisis fund - i am not saying this, mr trichet says that we must expand, the heads of state do not want to expand, and we want confidence to return to the markets; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. these are the four things that must be done, because nowhere in the world, ladies and gentlemen, is there a currency that is not backed by a government, by an economic strategy and by a bond market. it doesn't exist anywhere in the world. and what are we being told here today, in this chamber? we are told, "yes, that's a good idea, but we have to wait a little longer". wait for what? we may have to wait for complete chaos or for the euro to disappear! the time has come to take this decision and i expect the commission, mr president, not to say to us today: 'yes, it will be difficult, we must not continue these discussions on eurobonds; it is a good idea but it is not yet the time since we have the crisis fund, which we are going to make permanent now.' one has nothing to do with the other. the crisis fund is needed now to counter attacks on the euro, but the euro bonds are also needed for the future stability of the euro in the medium and long term. there is no contradiction between the two, both are needed, as is once again the case throughout the world. i therefore believe that, apart from the heads of government who will be discussing tomorrow and the day after tomorrow and who will say 'yes, we are going to change the treaty a little and perhaps make the crisis fund which normally needs to be increased permanent, as everyone else is asking for', i believe that the time has come for the commission to table as soon as possible a much larger, courageous, comprehensive and coherent package on this approach, and containing these four elements. as far as the stability pact with real sanctioning mechanisms is concerned, parliament can do its job, since the package is here and we are going to return to the commission's initial proposals. and as for the other three elements, an expanded crisis fund, but propose it. bring it up! take a stand on this and say that the crisis fund must be expanded. why would you do that? because with that we will stop speculating against the euro. secondly, propose a comprehensive package on fiscal and economic union and thirdly, have no fear, propose a single market for euro bonds because we know that in the end this is what will stabilise the euro in the long term.
mr president, i am very sensitive to martin schulz's call for us to try to restore the confidence of our citizens, and that means telling the truth and avoiding simplistic statements. the first is that eurobonds relieve governments of their responsibilities. this is obviously wrong, because no one has ever asked member states to pool 100% of their debts. in any case, under any eurobond scheme, the states will have to continue to use the direct markets for part of their debts and there they will feel the quality of their signature, which will be reflected in the interest rates they will have to pay. so this is a simplistic view that we must get out of. the second simplistic approach is the simplistic approach of saying: 'we are going to the aid of these greeks, these irish people who are a little incapable'. i would like to remind you that what we do is lend money, and lend money at rates that are particularly favorable to lenders. we're making a good business out of it. and so, one of two things: either we consider that greece and ireland, with our loans, no longer present any risk and we must lend to them at a rate which corresponds to zero risk or at any rate extremely limited or there is a risk that they will default and then, we must puncture the abscess, we must restructure these debts and put an end to the uncertainty. i would also like to end by speaking to our german friends, especially the cdu. mr langen, you spoke of reunification and you are right. when germany was reunified, the entire deutsche mark area, of which belgium was a part and to which france was ultimately largely attached, paid for it in the form of higher interest rates. and it was just! it was both historically right, because the reunification of germany was the reunification of our europe, and economically right, because in the end, everyone would benefit from the additional economic growth that would result. so i say to the cdu today: "take this into account", and we are asking the same of germany today.
